PySpark for AWS Data Engineers

A complete course that explains PySpark the way a senior engineer would explain it to a new teammate: what each idea means in plain English, a picture of what happens inside Spark, working code with its real output, the mistakes people make at work, and how to answer it in an interview.

You do not need any other website to finish this course. Every term is explained where it first appears, every code example shows its output, and the diagrams with a ▶ Play button walk through what Spark does one step at a time. If a word is unfamiliar, the Glossary explains it.

A short, jargon-free explanation of the idea before anything technical.

An analogy and a diagram. Animated diagrams have Back / Play / Next buttons; click any step to jump to it.

Runnable PySpark with the exact output Spark 3.5 prints, then a line-by-line explanation.

How it shows up on Glue and EMR, the common mistakes, a model answer to say out loud, and a “Remember” summary.

Spark and AWS versions in 2026

All code in this course targets Apache Spark 3.5.6 or newer in the 3.5 line, because that is what most production AWS jobs run today: AWS Glue 5.1 uses Spark 3.5.6 and recent Amazon EMR 7.x releases use Spark 3.5.6 to 3.5.8. Spark 4 exists and AWS offers it on Glue 6.0, but most teams have not moved yet. Whenever something works only in Spark 4, it carries this badge: Spark 4 only.

What runs where

PlatformSparkPythonIcebergNotes
AWS Glue 5.13.5.63.111.10.0Default version for new Glue jobs; Java 17. The baseline for this course.
AWS Glue 5.03.5.43.111.7.1Still common. Same APIs as 5.1; upgrade to 5.1 to get Spark 3.5.6 fixes and newer Iceberg.
AWS Glue 6.04.1.13.131.11.0Released August 2026. Spark 4 runtime, Iceberg v3 features, lower DPU price. Spark 4
AWS Glue 4.03.3.03.10—Older jobs. Upgrade path is to 5.1.
Amazon EMR 7.11–7.133.5.63.9 / 3.111.9.1–1.10.0EMR on EC2, EMR Serverless and EMR on EKS share release labels.
Amazon EMR 7.143.5.83.9 / 3.111.10.1Latest 3.5 line on EMR at the time of writing.

Sources: the AWS Glue release-notes page and the EMR 7.x application-versions page, checked November 2026. Check them again before an interview, because AWS ships new releases often.

Why Spark 3.5 and not Spark 4 as the default?

Interviewers in 2026 mostly ask about the Spark version their own jobs run, and that is overwhelmingly 3.5. Spark 4 changes some default behaviours, most importantly ANSI SQL mode is on by default, which turns silent NULLs (for example a failed cast or a divide by zero) into errors. That is good for data quality but breaks old jobs, so teams migrate deliberately. You should know what Spark 4 brings and be able to say so, which is what the Spark 4 chapter is for.

Spark 4 in one paragraph

Spark 4.0 (2025) and 4.1 bring ANSI mode on by default, a VARIANT type for semi-structured JSON, a much more complete Spark Connect (thin client talking to a remote Spark server), a Python Data Source API, SQL pipe syntax, a new streaming state API (transformWithState), and drop Java 8/11 and Scala 2.12. On AWS you get it through Glue 6.0. Everything else in this course works the same on 3.5 and 4.x unless marked.

Set up the same version on your laptop

Local install matching Glue 5.1shell
python3.11 -m venv .venv && source .venv/bin/activate
pip install "pyspark==3.5.6" pandas pyarrow pytest
java -version          # needs Java 17 (or 11); Spark 3.5 also runs on Java 8
pyspark                # opens an interactive shell; Spark UI at http://localhost:4040

If you prefer containers, AWS publishes a Glue 5.x Docker image so you can run Glue scripts (including awsglue imports) locally. Plain pyspark is enough for everything except the Glue-specific APIs.

  • Course baseline: Spark 3.5.6+ (Glue 5.1, EMR 7.11+).
  • Glue 6.0 = Spark 4.1. Anything Spark-4-only is badged.
  • Biggest Spark 4 behaviour change to mention in interviews: ANSI mode on by default.

Why distributed computing exists

A single computer can only read so many gigabytes per second and hold so much in memory. When your data is 2 TB of orders, one machine would take hours just to read it. Distributed computing splits the data into many small pieces and gives each piece to a different computer (or CPU core) at the same time. If 200 cores each read 10 GB in parallel, the 2 TB is read in roughly the time it takes one core to read 10 GB.

Imagine you must count every vote in a city. One person counting 2 million ballots takes weeks. Instead you split the ballots into 200 boxes, give each box to a counter, and each counter writes their totals on a slip. A supervisor adds up the 200 slips. That is exactly how Spark works: the boxes are partitions, the counters are executor cores, and the supervisor is the driver.

The three hard problems Spark solves for you

  1. Splitting the work. Spark decides how to cut the input into pieces (partitions) and how many workers to use.
  2. Moving data when it has to meet. To total revenue per country, all rows for “IN” must end up on the same worker. Spark moves them over the network. This movement is called a shuffle and is the most expensive thing Spark does. You will hear this word in every interview.
  3. Surviving failures. With 200 workers, one will eventually crash. Spark remembers how each piece was produced and simply redoes the lost piece on another worker.

Where this fits in your daily work

As an AWS data engineer, your data usually lives in Amazon S3 as Parquet, JSON or CSV files, or in Iceberg tables. You write PySpark code, and a service such as AWS Glue or Amazon EMR provides the cluster of computers that runs it. You never manage the individual machines in Glue; you just choose a worker type and count.

Spark is a distributed processing engine. It splits large datasets into partitions, processes them in parallel across executor cores, moves data between machines only when an operation needs related rows together, and recovers from failures by recomputing lost partitions from their lineage.

  • Distributed = split data into pieces, process pieces at the same time, combine results.
  • Partition = a piece of data. Core = a worker that processes one piece at a time.
  • Shuffle = moving rows between machines so related rows meet. It is slow; good Spark code minimises it.

What Spark is and how PySpark fits

Apache Spark is an open-source engine for processing large amounts of data across many machines. It is written in Scala and runs on the Java Virtual Machine (JVM). PySpark is the Python way to use Spark: you write Python, and PySpark translates your instructions into work that the Spark engine runs in the JVM. For DataFrame code, the heavy lifting never happens in Python at all, which is why PySpark is just as fast as Scala Spark for most jobs.

The pieces of Spark

How Python talks to the JVM

When you start PySpark, two processes run on the driver machine: your Python program and a JVM. They talk through a bridge library called Py4J. When you write df.filter(...), Python does not filter anything; it sends a description of the filter to the JVM. The JVM builds the plan and the executors (also JVMs) do the work.

Spark versus what came before

Hadoop MapReduce (old)Spark
Between stepsWrites every intermediate result to disk (HDFS)Keeps intermediate data in memory; disk only for shuffles and spills
ProgrammingOnly map and reduce; long Java codeRich APIs: SQL, DataFrames, Python
OptimiserNoneCatalyst rewrites your query for speed
WorkersNew JVM per taskExecutors live for the whole application and run many tasks
Typical speedBaselineOften 10× or more faster for multi-step jobs
Interview: Is PySpark slower than Scala Spark?

For DataFrame and SQL code, no meaningful difference. Both produce the same plan, which runs in the JVM. PySpark is slower only when rows must go to Python: Python UDFs, RDD lambdas and collect()/toPandas() to the driver. Pandas UDFs reduce that cost using Apache Arrow; built-in functions remove it.

Interview: Why is Spark faster than MapReduce?

Spark keeps data in memory between steps instead of writing to disk after each one, reuses long-lived executor JVMs instead of starting a JVM per task, pipelines several operations into one pass over the data, and optimises the whole query before it runs (Catalyst plus generated code).

  • Spark = engine in the JVM. PySpark = Python front end that sends instructions over Py4J.
  • DataFrame code runs in the JVM at full speed. Python UDFs are the exception.
  • Spark SQL / DataFrames are what you use for almost all AWS ETL.

Architecture: driver, executors, cluster manager

Every Spark application has one driver (the boss that plans the work) and many executors (the workers that do it). A cluster manager hands out machines. On AWS Glue the cluster manager is hidden from you; on EMR it is YARN. Press Play to watch a job from start to finish.

Each role in detail

Driver
One per application. Runs your main code, keeps track of all executors and data blocks, turns your code into an execution plan, sends out tasks and collects results of actions like count() or collect(). In Glue the driver runs on one of your workers.
Executor
A JVM process on a worker machine. It has a fixed number of cores (how many tasks it can run at the same time) and a fixed amount of memory. It runs tasks, caches data you ask it to cache, and writes shuffle files to its local disk. In Glue, each worker runs one executor (for G.1X: 4 vCPU, 16 GB).
Cluster manager
Hands out machines and containers. On EMR on EC2 it is YARN. On EMR on EKS it is Kubernetes. Glue and EMR Serverless use a managed one you never see.
Task
The smallest unit of work: one piece of code applied to one partition. If a stage has 400 partitions, it has 400 tasks.
Core (slot)
One task runs on one core. Total parallelism = number of executors × cores per executor.
SparkSession
The entry point object (spark) you use to read data, run SQL and configure Spark. It wraps the older SparkContext (spark.sparkContext), which you still use for low-level things like setting the log level.

Client mode and cluster mode

This only matters on EMR, where you choose where the driver runs.

Client mode

The driver runs on the machine where you typed spark-submit (usually the EMR primary node, or your notebook). Driver logs print to your console. If your SSH session dies, the job dies. Good for interactive work.

Cluster mode

The driver runs inside a YARN container on one of the cluster's worker nodes. Your terminal can disconnect safely. Driver logs go to YARN container logs (and to S3 on EMR). Use this for production steps.

Code: start a session

Plain Spark (EMR, local, EMR Serverless)pyspark
from pyspark.sql import SparkSession

spark = (SparkSession.builder
         .appName("orders-daily")                          # name shown in the Spark UI
         .config("spark.sql.shuffle.partitions", "400")    # how many pieces after a shuffle
         .getOrCreate())                                   # reuse a session if one exists

print(spark.version)                         # 3.5.6
print(spark.sparkContext.defaultParallelism) # total cores Spark can use
AWS Glue job skeletonaws glue
import sys
from awsglue.utils import getResolvedOptions
from awsglue.context import GlueContext
from awsglue.job import Job
from pyspark.context import SparkContext

args = getResolvedOptions(sys.argv, ["JOB_NAME", "run_date"])  # read --JOB_NAME and --run_date
sc = SparkContext.getOrCreate()
glue_context = GlueContext(sc)
spark = glue_context.spark_session        # a normal SparkSession: all PySpark code works
job = Job(glue_context)
job.init(args["JOB_NAME"], args)          # needed for job bookmarks and job metrics

orders = spark.read.parquet(f"s3://my-raw/orders/dt={args['run_date']}/")
# ... transformations ...
job.commit()                              # saves bookmark progress; call at the very end

Glue gives you a GlueContext on top of Spark. You can ignore most of it and write plain PySpark through glue_context.spark_session. You need Job.init and job.commit if you use job bookmarks (covered in the Glue lesson).

Interview: What happens when you call spark-submit?

The driver starts and creates the SparkSession. It requests executors from the cluster manager, which launches them; they register back with the driver. The code builds a logical plan lazily. When an action runs, Catalyst optimises the plan into a physical plan, the DAG scheduler splits it into stages at shuffle boundaries, and the task scheduler sends one task per partition to free executor cores, preferring cores close to the data. Executors run the tasks, write shuffle output, and the next stage reads it. Results go to storage or back to the driver. When the application ends, executors are released.

Interview: What happens if an executor dies? What if the driver dies?

If an executor dies, the driver re-runs its in-flight tasks on other executors and recomputes any lost cached or shuffle data from lineage. The job continues, just slower. If the driver dies, the application ends because the driver holds the plan and all scheduling state. That is why driver memory problems (for example collect() on a large table) are fatal.

  • One driver plans; many executors work; the cluster manager hands out machines.
  • Parallelism = executors × cores. One task per partition per core at a time.
  • Executor death = retry and continue. Driver death = job fails.
  • Glue: one executor per worker, driver on one worker. EMR: you size executors yourself.

Your first PySpark program

A DataFrame is a table: rows and named columns, each column with a type. It looks like a pandas DataFrame or a SQL table, but its rows are spread across many machines. You create one by reading files or from a small Python list, then you chain operations on it.

We will use the same small e-commerce dataset in many lessons so you can focus on the idea, not the data: an orders table and a customers table.

Create sample data and look at itpyspark
from pyspark.sql import SparkSession, functions as F

spark = SparkSession.builder.master("local[4]").appName("learn").getOrCreate()

orders = spark.createDataFrame([
    ("o1", "c1", "2026-11-01 09:15:00", "SHIPPED",   1200.00, "IN"),
    ("o2", "c2", "2026-11-01 10:02:00", "PAID",       450.50, "US"),
    ("o3", "c1", "2026-11-02 14:30:00", "SHIPPED",     80.00, "IN"),
    ("o4", "c3", "2026-11-02 16:45:00", "CANCELLED",  300.00, "UK"),
    ("o5", "c4", "2026-11-03 08:05:00", "SHIPPED",   2200.00, "US"),
    ("o6", None, "2026-11-03 11:20:00", "PAID",        99.99, "IN"),
], "order_id string, customer_id string, order_ts string, status string, amount double, country string")

orders = orders.withColumn("order_ts", F.to_timestamp("order_ts"))   # text -> real timestamp
orders.show()
orders.printSchema()
Output
+--------+-----------+-------------------+---------+------+-------+
|order_id|customer_id|           order_ts|   status|amount|country|
+--------+-----------+-------------------+---------+------+-------+
|      o1|         c1|2026-11-01 09:15:00|  SHIPPED|1200.0|     IN|
|      o2|         c2|2026-11-01 10:02:00|     PAID| 450.5|     US|
|      o3|         c1|2026-11-02 14:30:00|  SHIPPED|  80.0|     IN|
|      o4|         c3|2026-11-02 16:45:00|CANCELLED| 300.0|     UK|
|      o5|         c4|2026-11-03 08:05:00|  SHIPPED|2200.0|     US|
|      o6|       NULL|2026-11-03 11:20:00|     PAID| 99.99|     IN|

root
 |-- order_id: string (nullable = true)
 |-- customer_id: string (nullable = true)
 |-- order_ts: timestamp (nullable = true)
 |-- status: string (nullable = true)
 |-- amount: double (nullable = true)
 |-- country: string (nullable = true)
  1. master("local[4]") runs Spark on your laptop with 4 cores. On Glue or EMR you leave this out; the platform sets it.
  2. The second argument to createDataFrame is the schema as a DDL string: column names and types. Spark would guess types otherwise.
  3. withColumn("order_ts", ...) replaces the column with a converted version. DataFrames are immutable: every operation returns a new DataFrame, so we assign it back to orders.
  4. show() prints the first 20 rows. printSchema() prints column names, types and whether NULL is allowed.

Ask a business question

“What is the revenue from shipped orders per country, biggest first?”

filter → groupBy → agg → orderBypyspark
revenue = (orders
    .filter(F.col("status") == "SHIPPED")                 # keep shipped orders only
    .groupBy("country")                                   # one group per country
    .agg(F.sum("amount").alias("revenue"),                # total per group
         F.count("*").alias("orders"))                    # rows per group
    .orderBy(F.desc("revenue")))                          # biggest first
revenue.show()
Output
+-------+-------+------+
|country|revenue|orders|
+-------+-------+------+
|     US| 2200.0|     1|
|     IN| 1280.0|     2|
+-------+-------+------+

The same question in Spark SQL gives the same plan and the same speed:

orders.createOrReplaceTempView("orders")
spark.sql("""
  SELECT country, SUM(amount) AS revenue, COUNT(*) AS orders
  FROM orders WHERE status = 'SHIPPED'
  GROUP BY country ORDER BY revenue DESC
""").show()

Three ways to refer to a column

StyleExampleWhen to use
String nameorders.select("amount")Simple selects, groupBy keys
F.col()F.col("amount") > 100Expressions and conditions. The standard choice.
Attributeorders.amount or orders["amount"]Telling apart same-named columns after a join (orders.customer_id vs cust.customer_id)
Common beginner mistakes
  • Forgetting to assign: orders.filter(...) on its own line does nothing to orders.
  • Using Python and / or in conditions. Use & / | and wrap each condition in brackets: (F.col("a") > 1) & (F.col("b") == "x").
  • Calling .collect() or .toPandas() on a big DataFrame. That pulls every row into the driver's memory and crashes it.
  • DataFrame = distributed table with a schema. Operations return new DataFrames.
  • Use from pyspark.sql import functions as F and build expressions with F.col.
  • DataFrame API and Spark SQL compile to the same plan. Pick whichever reads better.

Lazy evaluation: transformations and actions

Spark does not run anything when you write filter or select. It only writes them down in a plan. Work starts when you ask for a result, such as count(), show() or write. Operations that only add to the plan are transformations; operations that ask for a result are actions. Waiting lets Spark see the whole job first and find the fastest way to run it.

A restaurant waiter writes down your whole order (starter, main, drink) before going to the kitchen. If they ran to the kitchen after each item, the chef could not plan. Because the chef sees the whole order, they can start the slow dish first and skip things you cancelled. Transformations are the order being written; the action is “send it to the kitchen”.

Which operations are which

Transformations (lazy, return a DataFrame)Actions (run a job, return a result)
select, filter/where, withColumn, drop, join, groupBy().agg(), orderBy, distinct, dropDuplicates, union, repartition, coalesce, explode, cache show, count, collect, take, first, head, toPandas, write.*, saveAsTable, foreach, foreachPartition

The cost of lazy evaluation: repeated work

Because nothing is stored by default, every action runs the whole plan again from the source.

This reads S3 twicecommon bug
clean = spark.read.parquet("s3://raw/orders/").filter("amount > 0").dropDuplicates(["order_id"])
print("rows:", clean.count())                         # job 1: read S3, filter, dedup, count
clean.write.mode("overwrite").parquet("s3://curated/orders/")   # job 2: read S3, filter, dedup AGAIN, write

Fixes: drop the count if it is only for logging; or cache clean before the two actions (see Caching); or get the count afterwards from the written data or the job metrics. In Glue this “hidden double read” is one of the most common reasons a job costs twice what it should.

Small exceptions

A few calls do run a small job immediately: reading CSV with inferSchema=True or JSON without a schema (Spark samples the data to guess types), and pivot() without a list of values (Spark finds the distinct values first). This is another reason to always pass schemas.

Interview: What are the benefits of lazy evaluation?

Whole-plan optimisation (filter pushdown, column pruning, combining steps, choosing join strategies), pipelining several narrow operations into a single pass over each partition, and skipping work whose results are never used. The downside is that each action recomputes the lineage unless you cache.

  • Transformations build a plan; actions run it.
  • Laziness lets the optimiser read less data and combine steps.
  • Every action re-runs the plan from the source unless something is cached.

Jobs, stages, tasks and the shuffle

One action creates a job. Spark cuts the job into stages wherever data must move between machines (a shuffle). Each stage runs as many tasks as it has partitions. Operations that can work on each partition alone are narrow and stay in the same stage; operations that need rows from other partitions are wide and start a new stage.

Back to counting votes. Step 1: every counter tallies their own box per candidate (narrow: no one needs anyone else's box). Step 2: all slips for candidate A go to one table, all slips for B to another (that is the shuffle). Step 3: each table adds up its slips (a new stage). You cannot start step 3 until every step 1 counter is finished, which is why a stage boundary is also a waiting point.

Narrow versus wide

Narrow (no shuffle)

Each output partition comes from one input partition.

select, filter, withColumn, drop, cast, union, coalesce, explode, map and a broadcast join.

Wide (shuffle, new stage)

Each output partition needs rows from many input partitions.

groupBy().agg(), join (not broadcast), distinct, dropDuplicates, orderBy, repartition, window with partitionBy, intersect, except.

Why the shuffle is expensive

  1. CPU: rows are serialised (turned into bytes) and compressed.
  2. Disk: every map task writes shuffle files to the executor's local disk.
  3. Network: every reduce task fetches pieces from every map task. With 1,000 map tasks and 1,000 reduce tasks that is up to a million small fetches.
  4. Waiting: the next stage cannot start until the slowest task of the previous stage finishes. One slow task holds everyone up.

Count the stages in real code

How many stages?pyspark
orders = spark.read.parquet("s3://lake/orders/")        # 120 files -> about 120 partitions
cust   = spark.read.parquet("s3://lake/customers/")     # large table, not broadcast

result = (orders.filter("status = 'SHIPPED'")
          .join(cust, "customer_id")                    # shuffle both sides by customer_id
          .groupBy("country").agg(F.sum("amount"))      # shuffle by country
          .orderBy("country"))                          # shuffle into sorted ranges
result.write.parquet("s3://out/by_country/")

Stage boundaries: scan + filter orders (stage 1), scan customers (stage 2), join + partial sum (stage 3), final sum (stage 4), sort (stage 5). The orderBy also adds a small sampling job to decide the sort ranges, so the UI may show two jobs for one action. With AQE on (the default), Spark may skip or merge stages at run time. Do not expect a perfect one-action-one-job match in the UI.

Interview: What decides the number of tasks in a stage?

The number of partitions in that stage. For a file scan: total input size divided by spark.sql.files.maxPartitionBytes (128 MB default), adjusted for many small files. After a shuffle: spark.sql.shuffle.partitions (200 default), which AQE can merge down. After repartition(n): n.

Interview: What is a skipped stage in the Spark UI?

A stage whose shuffle output already exists from an earlier job in the same application, so Spark reuses the shuffle files instead of computing them again. You see it when two actions share the same upstream shuffle.

A job is created by an action. The DAG scheduler splits it into stages at every shuffle boundary, because a wide operation like a groupBy or a non-broadcast join needs rows from all partitions. Inside a stage, narrow operations are pipelined per partition, and the stage runs one task per partition. The shuffle is the expensive part: serialisation, local disk writes and network fetches, and the next stage waits for the slowest task.

  • Action → job → stages (cut at shuffles) → tasks (one per partition).
  • Narrow = stays in a stage. Wide = shuffle = new stage.
  • Reducing shuffles is the core of Spark performance tuning.

Partitions: the unit of parallelism

A partition is one chunk of a DataFrame's rows. One task processes one partition, on one core. The number and size of partitions decide how much work happens in parallel and how much memory each task needs. Too few big partitions: cores sit idle and tasks run out of memory. Too many tiny partitions: Spark spends more time scheduling than working, and you write thousands of tiny files.

You have 8 friends to help move house (8 cores). If you pack everything into 2 giant boxes, 6 friends stand around and the 2 carrying boxes might collapse (out of memory). If you pack 10,000 tiny boxes, everyone spends the day walking back and forth. About 16–32 sensible boxes keeps everyone busy.

Two different things are both called “partition”

In-memory partition (this lesson): a chunk of rows a task processes while the job runs. On-disk partition: a folder in S3 such as dt=2026-11-01/, created by write.partitionBy("dt"), used to skip folders when reading. They are related but not the same; the disk partitioning lesson covers the second kind.

Where partition counts come from

SituationNumber of partitionsSetting
Reading Parquet / ORC / uncompressed CSVAbout total size ÷ 128 MBspark.sql.files.maxPartitionBytes (128 MB), spark.sql.files.openCostInBytes (4 MB)
Reading gzip CSV or JSONOne per file. Gzip cannot be split, so a 20 GB .gz file is read by one coreUse bzip2, uncompressed, or Parquet
After a shuffle (groupBy, join)200 by default, then AQE may mergespark.sql.shuffle.partitions
After repartition(n) / coalesce(n)nYour code
JDBC read1 unless you set numPartitions + partitionColumnRead options
Rules of thumb
  • Aim for 100–200 MB of data per partition while processing.
  • Have at least 2–3 times as many partitions as total cores, so fast cores pick up more work.
  • Output files: 128 MB to 1 GB each.

Look at and change partitions

Inspect partitions and rows per partitionpyspark
orders.rdd.getNumPartitions()                      # how many partitions right now

(orders.withColumn("pid", F.spark_partition_id())  # which partition each row is in
       .groupBy("pid").count()
       .orderBy(F.desc("count")).show(5))          # biggest partitions first: spot skew
repartition vs coalescepyspark
orders.repartition(400)                     # full shuffle into 400 even partitions
orders.repartition(400, "customer_id")      # full shuffle, rows with same key together
orders.repartition("dt")                    # by key, uses spark.sql.shuffle.partitions
orders.repartitionByRange(100, "order_ts")  # sorted ranges (used for sorted output)
orders.coalesce(20)                         # merge partitions WITHOUT a shuffle (only reduce)
repartition(n)coalesce(n)
Shuffle?Yes, full shuffleNo, it merges neighbouring partitions
Can increase?YesNo, only decrease
EvennessEven sizesCan be uneven
Hidden catchCosts one shuffleBecause it is narrow, it is pipelined with the steps before it, so those steps also run with only n tasks
The coalesce(1) trapcommon bug
# Takes hours: the heavy join and aggregation now run in ONE task on ONE core
big_result.coalesce(1).write.csv("s3://out/report/")

# Fast: heavy work stays parallel, only the small final result is shuffled into 1 file
big_result.repartition(1).write.csv("s3://out/report/")
Interview: When would you use repartition and when coalesce?

Use coalesce to reduce the number of partitions cheaply when the data is already small and evenly spread, for example after a selective filter and just before a write. Use repartition when you need more partitions, need even sizes, need rows with the same key together (repartition("dt") before partitionBy("dt")), or when coalescing would reduce the parallelism of expensive upstream work.

  • One partition = one task = one core at a time.
  • Target 100–200 MB per partition and 2–3× more partitions than cores.
  • Gzip files cannot be split: one file, one task.
  • coalesce is cheap but shrinks upstream parallelism; repartition shuffles but balances.

RDD vs DataFrame vs Dataset

Spark has three APIs. The RDD (Resilient Distributed Dataset) is the original low-level one: a distributed list of Python or Java objects with no schema, so Spark cannot optimise it. The DataFrame adds a schema (column names and types), which lets Spark's optimiser rewrite your query and store data compactly. The Dataset is a typed DataFrame that only exists in Scala and Java. In PySpark you use DataFrames for almost everything.

RDDDataFrameDataset
DataObjects, no schemaRows with named, typed columnsTyped objects with a schema
OptimiserNone: runs your functions as writtenCatalyst + TungstenCatalyst + Tungsten
In PythonYes, but every row passes through Python (slow)Yes, runs in the JVM (fast)Not available
MemoryMany small objects, heavy garbage collectionCompact binary formatCompact + encoders
Use it forLegacy code, rare low-level controlEverything elseType safety in Scala
Word count both wayspyspark
# RDD: every line and every word goes through Python lambdas
counts_rdd = (spark.sparkContext.textFile("s3://in/logs/")
              .flatMap(lambda line: line.split())
              .map(lambda w: (w.lower(), 1))
              .reduceByKey(lambda a, b: a + b))

# DataFrame: same result, runs entirely in the JVM, optimised
counts_df = (spark.read.text("s3://in/logs/")
             .select(F.explode(F.split(F.lower("value"), r"\s+")).alias("word"))
             .filter("word != ''")
             .groupBy("word").count())

RDD terms interviewers still ask

reduceByKey vs groupByKey
reduceByKey adds values up inside each partition first, then shuffles the small partial sums. groupByKey shuffles every single value and builds a list per key, which is slower and can run out of memory on popular keys. DataFrame groupBy().agg() behaves like reduceByKey.
map vs flatMap
map turns one item into exactly one item. flatMap turns one item into zero or more items and flattens them (one line → many words).
mapPartitions
Your function is called once per partition with all its rows. Useful to open one database or HTTP connection per partition instead of one per row.
Lineage
The recipe of transformations that produced an RDD. Spark uses it to rebuild lost partitions, which is the “resilient” in RDD.
df.rdd
Converts a DataFrame to an RDD of Row objects. It throws away optimisation and costs serialisation. Avoid in hot code.
Interview: When would you still use an RDD today?

Rarely. When the DataFrame API cannot express the logic (custom partitioners, processing raw binary records, very specific per-partition side effects) or when maintaining old code. Even then I would prefer mapPartitions to amortise setup cost, and convert back to a DataFrame as early as possible.

  • DataFrame = RDD + schema + optimiser. Use DataFrames.
  • Know reduceByKey vs groupByKey and mapPartitions for interviews.

Schemas and data types

A schema is the list of columns in a DataFrame, with each column's name, data type and whether it may be empty (NULL). Spark needs it to store data efficiently and to optimise your query. You can let Spark guess the schema by reading the data (inference) or give it yourself (explicit schema). In production, always give it yourself.

Why explicit schemas matter

Inferred

  • Spark reads the data an extra time to guess types (slow on big S3 folders).
  • Guesses can be wrong: zip code 01234 becomes the number 1234; an ID column becomes a double.
  • A single odd file changes the schema of the whole job.

Explicit

  • No extra read.
  • Types are exactly what downstream tables expect.
  • Bad values are caught by the read mode instead of silently changing types.
  • The schema doubles as documentation of the data contract.

Two ways to write a schema

StructType or DDL stringpyspark
from pyspark.sql.types import (StructType, StructField, StringType, IntegerType,
                               DecimalType, TimestampType, ArrayType)

schema = StructType([
    StructField("order_id",    StringType(),        nullable=False),
    StructField("customer_id", StringType(),        nullable=True),
    StructField("qty",         IntegerType(),       nullable=True),
    StructField("amount",      DecimalType(12, 2),  nullable=True),   # money: never float/double
    StructField("order_ts",    TimestampType(),     nullable=True),
    StructField("tags",        ArrayType(StringType()), nullable=True),
])

# The same schema as a DDL string (shorter, easy to keep in config files)
ddl = "order_id STRING NOT NULL, customer_id STRING, qty INT, amount DECIMAL(12,2), order_ts TIMESTAMP, tags ARRAY<STRING>"

df = spark.read.schema(ddl).json("s3://raw/orders/")

The types you will use

Spark typeDDLUse forWatch out
StringTypeSTRINGIDs, codes, textKeep IDs as strings to protect leading zeros
IntegerType / LongTypeINT / BIGINTCounts, quantitiesINT overflows at about 2.1 billion
DecimalType(p, s)DECIMAL(12,2)Money and exact numbersDoubles drift (0.1 + 0.2 ≠ 0.3)
DoubleTypeDOUBLEMeasurements, scoresNot exact
DateType / TimestampTypeDATE / TIMESTAMPDates and moments in timeTimestamps are shown in the session time zone
BooleanTypeBOOLEANFlagsCan also be NULL
ArrayType / MapType / StructTypeARRAY<…>, MAP<k,v>, STRUCT<…>Nested JSONSee the nested-data lesson
VariantType Spark 4 onlyVARIANTSemi-structured JSON without a fixed schemaNot available on Glue 5.x / EMR 7.x
Inspect and change typespyspark
df.printSchema()                       # tree view
df.dtypes                              # [('order_id', 'string'), ('qty', 'int'), ...]
df.schema.simpleString()               # one-line DDL-like string (store it, compare it later)
df.withColumn("qty", F.col("qty").cast("bigint"))     # change one column's type
df.select(F.expr("try_cast(amount AS DECIMAL(12,2))").alias("amount"))  # NULL instead of an error

In Spark 3.5 (ANSI mode off by default), a failed cast quietly returns NULL: cast('abc' AS INT) → NULL, and 10/0 → NULL. With ANSI on (the Spark 4 default), the same cast fails with [CAST_INVALID_INPUT] The value 'abc' of the type "STRING" cannot be cast to "INT" because it is malformed. We ran both on Spark 3.5.6 to confirm. try_cast returns NULL in both modes, so it is the safe choice when you expect bad values. In Spark 4 there is also a Column.try_cast() method Spark 4 only.

Interview: How do you protect a pipeline against upstream schema changes?

Read with an explicit schema so a new or changed column cannot change my types; compare the incoming file schema with the expected one at the start of the job and fail or quarantine on breaking changes (type change, removed column) while allowing additive ones; alert on spikes in NULL rates per column, because a silent type change often shows up as NULLs; and store curated data in Iceberg, which supports safe schema evolution by column ID.

  • Always pass an explicit schema in production reads.
  • Money = DECIMAL. IDs = STRING.
  • Failed casts return NULL in Spark 3.5 and raise an error in Spark 4 (ANSI).

Reading data and handling bad records

You read data with spark.read, choose a format (Parquet, CSV, JSON, JDBC, a catalog table), add options and a schema, and point it at a path. Real data is messy, so you also choose what Spark does with rows that do not fit the schema: keep them with NULLs, drop them, or stop the job.

The common readerspyspark
spark.read.parquet("s3://lake/orders/")                                # schema is inside the files
spark.read.schema(ddl).option("header", "true").csv("s3://raw/orders/")
spark.read.schema(ddl).json("s3://raw/events/")                        # one JSON object per line
spark.read.schema(ddl).option("multiLine", "true").json(path)          # one JSON document over many lines
spark.read.table("glue_db.orders")                                     # a Glue Data Catalog table

# read only some partitions but keep the partition column dt
spark.read.option("basePath", "s3://lake/orders/").parquet("s3://lake/orders/dt=2026-11-0*/")

# JDBC in parallel: 16 tasks, each reads a range of ids
(spark.read.format("jdbc")
   .option("url", "jdbc:postgresql://host:5432/shop").option("dbtable", "public.orders")
   .option("user", user).option("password", password)
   .option("partitionColumn", "order_pk").option("lowerBound", 1).option("upperBound", 50_000_000)
   .option("numPartitions", 16).option("fetchsize", 10_000)
   .load())

Without partitionColumn/numPartitions, a JDBC read runs as one task on one core however big the table is. lowerBound/upperBound only decide how the ranges are cut; they do not filter rows.

Bad records: the three read modes

Here is a small CSV with two bad rows: abc is not a number, and 02/11/2026 is not in the default date format.

orders.csvinput
order_id,customer_id,amount,order_date
o1,c1,1200.00,2026-11-01
o2,c2,abc,2026-11-01
o3,c1,80.00,2026-11-02
o4,c3,300.00,02/11/2026
PERMISSIVE with a corrupt-record columnpyspark
df = (spark.read.option("header", "true")
      .schema("order_id string, customer_id string, amount decimal(12,2), order_date date, _corrupt string")
      .option("mode", "PERMISSIVE")                        # the default
      .option("columnNameOfCorruptRecord", "_corrupt")     # keep the raw text of bad rows here
      .csv("orders.csv"))
df.show(truncate=False)
Output
+--------+-----------+-------+----------+-----------------------+
|order_id|customer_id|amount |order_date|_corrupt               |
+--------+-----------+-------+----------+-----------------------+
|o1      |c1         |1200.00|2026-11-01|NULL                   |
|o2      |c2         |NULL   |2026-11-01|o2,c2,abc,2026-11-01   |
|o3      |c1         |80.00  |2026-11-02|NULL                   |
|o4      |c3         |300.00 |NULL      |o4,c3,300.00,02/11/2026|
+--------+-----------+-------+----------+-----------------------+

The bad fields became NULL and the original line was kept in _corrupt, so nothing is lost. You must add the corrupt column to the schema yourself, as a string.

ModeWhat happens to a bad rowUse when
PERMISSIVE (default)Bad fields → NULL; raw text kept if you add the corrupt columnMost pipelines: split good rows from bad and quarantine the bad
DROPMALFORMEDRow silently removedRarely. You lose data and nobody knows
FAILFASTJob fails on the first bad row with MALFORMED_RECORD_IN_PARSINGStrict contracts (finance) where bad input must stop the pipeline

The quarantine pattern

Split good and bad rows, keep bothpyspark
raw = (spark.read.schema(schema_with_corrupt).option("mode", "PERMISSIVE")
         .option("columnNameOfCorruptRecord", "_corrupt").json("s3://raw/orders/dt=2026-11-03/"))
raw = raw.cache()     # Spark refuses queries that reference ONLY the corrupt column on raw JSON/CSV;
                      # caching (or selecting other columns too) avoids that error
bad  = raw.filter(F.col("_corrupt").isNotNull())
good = raw.filter(F.col("_corrupt").isNull()).drop("_corrupt")

bad.withColumn("ingest_ts", F.current_timestamp()) \
   .write.mode("append").json("s3://lake/quarantine/orders/")
good.write.mode("overwrite").parquet("s3://lake/clean/orders/dt=2026-11-03/")
raw.unpersist()

At work you then alert when the bad count is above a threshold (for example 1% of rows), so data issues reach a human instead of silently shrinking reports.

Read performance tips

  • Prefer Parquet or Iceberg tables over CSV/JSON: columnar, typed, compressed, and Spark can skip data.
  • Point at the narrowest folder or filter on the partition column so Spark lists fewer S3 objects.
  • Gzip CSV/JSON cannot be split; many medium files are better than one giant .gz.
  • mergeSchema=true on Parquet reads every file footer; use it only when you must.
Interview: How do you handle corrupt or malformed records?

Read in PERMISSIVE mode with an explicit schema and a corrupt-record column, split the DataFrame into good and bad rows, write bad rows with the raw text and a load timestamp to a quarantine location, continue with good rows, and alert when the bad ratio crosses a threshold. For strict feeds I use FAILFAST so the job stops. I avoid DROPMALFORMED because it hides data loss.

  • spark.read.schema(...).option(...).format(path) is the pattern.
  • PERMISSIVE + corrupt column + quarantine is the production standard.
  • JDBC reads need partitionColumn + numPartitions to run in parallel.

Writing data: save modes and overwrite

Writing is an action. You choose a format, a save mode (what to do if data already exists) and optionally partition columns (which become folders like dt=2026-11-03/). One setting decides whether “overwrite” replaces only the folders you are writing or deletes the whole table first. Getting it wrong is one of the most damaging mistakes in data engineering.

A standard partitioned writepyspark
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.sources.partitionOverwriteMode", "dynamic")   # see below: critical

(daily_orders
   .repartition("dt")                     # put each date's rows in one task -> fewer files per folder
   .write
   .mode("overwrite")                     # replace what is there (which part depends on the mode above)
   .partitionBy("dt")                     # folder per value: .../dt=2026-11-03/part-0000.snappy.parquet
   .option("compression", "snappy")       # default for Parquet
   .option("maxRecordsPerFile", 5_000_000)
   .parquet("s3://lake/curated/orders/"))
Save modeIf data exists
errorifexists / error (default)Fail
appendAdd new files next to the existing ones
overwriteReplace existing data (static or dynamic, see below)
ignoreDo nothing, silently

Static versus dynamic partition overwrite

This is real output from Spark 3.5.6 running the scenario above:

Proofoutput
static:  ['dt=2026-11-02']
dynamic: ['dt=2026-11-01', 'dt=2026-11-02', 'dt=2026-11-03']

What a write produces on S3

Files after a partitioned writes3 listing
s3://lake/curated/orders/_SUCCESS
s3://lake/curated/orders/dt=2026-11-02/part-00000-3f1c...-c000.snappy.parquet
s3://lake/curated/orders/dt=2026-11-02/part-00001-3f1c...-c000.snappy.parquet
s3://lake/curated/orders/dt=2026-11-03/part-00000-3f1c...-c000.snappy.parquet

Each write task writes one file per partition value it holds. 200 tasks × 30 dates can create 6,000 files. That is why we repartition("dt") before partitionBy("dt"): each date's rows sit in one task, so each folder gets few files. The small files lesson goes deeper.

Retries and append

If a job appends data, fails afterwards, and your orchestrator retries it, the same rows get appended twice. Production loads should be idempotent (running twice gives the same result): overwrite the run's partitions with dynamic mode, or MERGE on a key with Iceberg.

Interview: How do you make a daily batch load idempotent?

Partition the output by the business date and overwrite only that date's partition with dynamic partition overwrite, or use Iceberg overwritePartitions() or a MERGE keyed on the business key. The job takes the run date as a parameter, so a re-run for the same date replaces the same data. I never rely on plain append for retried jobs.

  • Default overwrite is static: it wipes the whole path. Set partitionOverwriteMode=dynamic.
  • repartition(col) before partitionBy(col) to control file counts.
  • Design every load to be safe to re-run.

File formats and how Parquet works

Row formats (CSV, JSON, Avro) store one record after another. Columnar formats (Parquet, ORC) store all values of one column together. Analytics queries usually read a few columns from many rows, so columnar formats let Spark read only the columns it needs and skip blocks of rows that cannot match a filter. On AWS, Parquet is the default choice for data lakes, and Iceberg tables store their data as Parquet too.

Inside a Parquet file

FormatTypeStrengthsWeak spotsTypical AWS use
ParquetColumnarColumn pruning, pushdown, compression, schema insideNo updates/deletes, small-file proneLake storage, Athena, Redshift Spectrum
ORCColumnarLike Parquet, bloom filtersLess common outside HiveLegacy Hive tables
AvroRowFast writes, strong schema evolutionSlow analytic scansKafka/MSK messages, CDC
CSV / JSONRow, textHuman readableNo types, slow parsing, gzip not splittableLanding zone from source systems
Iceberg / Delta / HudiTable format over ParquetACID, MERGE, time travel, schema evolutionNeeds maintenanceCurated lakehouse tables (see Iceberg chapter)
Compression

Snappy (default for Parquet) is fast with moderate compression. Zstd compresses better at similar speed and is a good choice for cold data. Gzip is slower. Compression codec applies inside Parquet pages, so a .snappy.parquet file is still splittable.

Interview: Why is Parquet faster than CSV for analytics?

Spark reads only the columns the query needs (column pruning), skips whole row groups using min/max statistics in the footer (predicate pushdown), decodes values in vectorised batches, and reads far fewer bytes because columnar data compresses well. CSV must be read fully and parsed as text, with no types or statistics.

  • Columnar = read only needed columns + skip row groups by statistics.
  • Parquet file = row groups of column chunks + a footer with stats.
  • Sorted data makes pushdown effective.

Selecting and transforming columns

Most ETL code is “take these columns, clean them, compute new ones”. You do this with select (choose and compute columns), withColumn (add or replace one column), when/otherwise (if/else per row) and the built-in functions in pyspark.sql.functions. Built-in functions run inside the JVM and are always faster than writing your own Python function.

Derive columnspyspark
tiers = orders.select(
    "order_id", "amount",
    F.when(F.col("amount") >= 1000, "GOLD")          # first matching condition wins
     .when(F.col("amount") >= 100,  "SILVER")
     .otherwise("BRONZE").alias("tier"),
    F.to_date("order_ts").alias("order_date"))
tiers.show()
Output
+--------+------+------+----------+
|order_id|amount|  tier|order_date|
+--------+------+------+----------+
|      o1|1200.0|  GOLD|2026-11-01|
|      o2| 450.5|SILVER|2026-11-01|
|      o3|  80.0|BRONZE|2026-11-02|
|      o4| 300.0|SILVER|2026-11-02|
|      o5|2200.0|  GOLD|2026-11-03|
|      o6| 99.99|BRONZE|2026-11-03|
+--------+------+------+----------+

The everyday toolkit

Common column operationspyspark
df = (orders
  .withColumn("amount_gst", F.round(F.col("amount") * 1.18, 2))      # add a column
  .withColumn("country", F.upper(F.trim("country")))                 # replace a column
  .withColumn("source", F.lit("shopify"))                            # a constant
  .withColumnRenamed("order_ts", "ordered_at")                       # rename
  .drop("status"))                                                   # remove

# Many columns at once: one projection instead of a long withColumn chain
df = df.withColumns({"y": F.year("ordered_at"), "m": F.month("ordered_at")})
df = df.withColumnsRenamed({"y": "order_year", "m": "order_month"})

# Apply the same function to many columns
string_cols = [f.name for f in df.schema.fields if f.dataType.simpleString() == "string"]
df = df.select(*[F.trim(F.col(c)).alias(c) if c in string_cols else F.col(c) for c in df.columns])

# SQL expressions inside the DataFrame API
df.selectExpr("order_id", "amount * 1.18 AS amount_gst", "CASE WHEN amount > 1000 THEN 'big' END AS size")
Avoid long withColumn loops

Calling withColumn 200 times in a loop adds 200 projection steps to the plan. The analyser re-checks the whole plan each time, so building the plan can take minutes and the driver can even run out of stack. Use one select with a list, or withColumns with a dictionary.

Function families worth memorising

FamilyFunctions
Stringconcat, concat_ws, substring, split, regexp_extract, regexp_replace, lpad, rpad, trim, lower, upper, length, instr, translate, initcap, format_string
Null handlingcoalesce, isnull, isnan, nanvl, na.fill, na.drop, na.replace
Conditionalwhen/otherwise, greatest, least, expr("CASE … END")
Mathround, bround, ceil, floor, abs, pow, sqrt, log; safe: try_divide, try_add (3.5)
Hashing / IDsmd5, sha2(col, 256), xxhash64, hash, monotonically_increasing_id, expr("uuid()")
DatesSee Dates and time zones
Arrays / JSONSee Nested data and JSON
Interview: Is monotonically_increasing_id() a good surrogate key?

It is unique and increasing within one run, but not consecutive (the partition number is stored in the upper bits, so you see jumps like 0, 1, 8589934592) and not stable between runs because partitioning changes. For a stable key I hash the natural key: sha2(concat_ws('||', k1, k2), 256). For consecutive numbers use row_number() over a window, knowing that an unpartitioned window moves all rows to one task.

  • select/withColumns for many columns; avoid withColumn loops.
  • when().when().otherwise() is per-row if/else.
  • Built-in functions beat Python UDFs every time.

Filtering and NULL handling

NULL means “unknown”. Any comparison with an unknown value is also unknown, and filter keeps only rows where the condition is definitely true. So status != 'CANCELLED' quietly drops rows where status is NULL. This rule (three-valued logic) causes more silent data loss than any other Spark behaviour.

Expressionstatus = 'PAID'status = NULL
status == 'PAID'trueNULL → row dropped by filter
status != 'CANCELLED'trueNULL → row dropped
~status.isin('CANCELLED')trueNULL → row dropped
status.isNull()falsetrue
status.eqNullSafe('PAID') (<=>)truefalse (never NULL)
Seeing the trappyspark
# 6 orders: one is CANCELLED, one has status NULL
print(orders2.count())                                                     # 6
print(orders2.filter(~F.col("status").isin("CANCELLED")).count())          # 4  <- NULL row lost
print(orders2.filter(F.col("status").isNull() |
                     ~F.col("status").isin("CANCELLED")).count())          # 5  <- correct

NULL toolkit

Fill, drop, coalesce, countpyspark
df.na.fill({"customer_id": "UNKNOWN", "country": "NA"})         # per-column defaults
df.na.drop(subset=["order_id"])                                  # drop rows with NULL order_id
df.withColumn("city", F.coalesce("city", "billing_city", F.lit("NA")))  # first non-NULL wins

# Null count per column in ONE pass (great first step when exploring data)
df.select([F.count(F.when(F.col(c).isNull(), c)).alias(c) for c in df.columns]).show()
Output
+--------+-----------+-------+
|order_id|customer_id|country|
+--------+-----------+-------+
|       0|          2|      2|
+--------+-----------+-------+
NULLs in aggregates and joins
  • count("col"), sum, avg, countDistinct ignore NULLs; count("*") counts every row.
  • NULL keys never match in a normal join. In a left join, NULL-key rows survive with no match, and if there are millions of them they all land in one task (a skew source).
  • Use eqNullSafe in joins only when NULL should match NULL.
Interview: Why did my filter return fewer rows than expected?

Most likely NULLs: a !=, NOT IN or ~isin condition evaluates to NULL for NULL values, and filter drops NULL. I check with a null count on the column and rewrite the filter as col.isNull() | (condition), or use eqNullSafe.

  • Filter keeps only TRUE; NULL comparisons give NULL; those rows vanish.
  • Use & | ~ with brackets, never Python and/or/not.
  • count(*) counts NULLs; count(col) does not.

Removing duplicates the right way

Duplicate rows arrive all the time: retried API calls, CDC feeds that send every change, files loaded twice. distinct() removes rows that are identical in every column. dropDuplicates(["key"]) keeps one row per key, but Spark does not promise which one. When you need the latest version of each record, rank rows with a window and keep rank 1.

A CDC feed sends every status change of an order. We want the current state.

Rank versions per keypyspark
from pyspark.sql import Window as W

w = (W.partitionBy("order_id")                                  # one group per order
       .orderBy(F.desc("updated_at"), F.desc("seq")))           # newest first; seq breaks ties
ranked = cdc.withColumn("rn", F.row_number().over(w))
ranked.show()
latest = ranked.filter("rn = 1").drop("rn")
latest.show()
Output
+--------+---------+-------------------+---+---+
|order_id|   status|         updated_at|seq| rn|
+--------+---------+-------------------+---+---+
|      o1|  SHIPPED|2026-11-01 12:00:00|  3|  1|
|      o1|     PAID|2026-11-01 10:05:00|  2|  2|
|      o1|  CREATED|2026-11-01 10:00:00|  1|  3|
|      o2|CANCELLED|2026-11-01 11:00:00|  5|  1|
|      o2|  CREATED|2026-11-01 11:00:00|  4|  2|
+--------+---------+-------------------+---+---+

+--------+---------+-------------------+---+
|order_id|   status|         updated_at|seq|
+--------+---------+-------------------+---+
|      o1|  SHIPPED|2026-11-01 12:00:00|  3|
|      o2|CANCELLED|2026-11-01 11:00:00|  5|
+--------+---------+-------------------+---+

Order o2 has two events with the same timestamp. Without the seq tie-breaker, Spark could pick either one, and re-runs might give different answers. Always add a tie-breaker (a sequence number, an ingest timestamp, or a file name).

MethodKeepsDeterministic?Cost
distinct()Unique full rowsYesOne shuffle on all columns
dropDuplicates(["k"])Any one row per keyNoOne shuffle
row_number() windowThe row you choose per keyYes, with a tie-breakerShuffle + sort within key
dropDuplicatesWithinWatermark (3.5, streaming)First row per key within the watermarkYesState store

For “latest record per key” I never use dropDuplicates, because it keeps an arbitrary row. I use row_number over a window partitioned by the business key and ordered by the change timestamp descending with a deterministic tie-breaker, then keep rank one. At scale the same logic goes into the source query of an Iceberg MERGE.

  • dropDuplicates = arbitrary survivor. row_number = your choice of survivor.
  • Always add a tie-breaker column.

Aggregations, pivot and subtotals

Aggregation collapses many rows into one row per group: total revenue per country, number of orders per day. You call groupBy(columns) then agg(...) with one or more aggregate functions. Spark does this in two steps: a partial result inside each partition, then a shuffle, then a final merge.

Several aggregates in one passpyspark
orders.groupBy("country").agg(
    F.count("*").alias("orders"),                         # all rows
    F.countDistinct("customer_id").alias("customers"),    # ignores NULL customer_id
    F.round(F.sum("amount"), 2).alias("revenue"),
    F.round(F.avg("amount"), 2).alias("avg_order"),
    F.max("amount").alias("biggest"),
).orderBy("country").show()
Output
+-------+------+---------+-------+---------+-------+
|country|orders|customers|revenue|avg_order|biggest|
+-------+------+---------+-------+---------+-------+
|     IN|     3|        1|1379.99|    460.0| 1200.0|
|     UK|     1|        1|  300.0|    300.0|  300.0|
|     US|     2|        2| 2650.5|  1325.25| 2200.0|
+-------+------+---------+-------+---------+-------+

India has 3 orders but only 1 distinct customer: order o6 has a NULL customer and o1/o3 both belong to c1. countDistinct skips NULLs.

Fast and slow aggregates

FunctionCostNotes
sum, count, min, max, avgCheapCombine well on the map side
countDistinctExpensiveSpark must keep every distinct value; several in one query multiply rows internally
approx_count_distinct(col, 0.02)CheapHyperLogLog sketch, about 2% error. Ideal for dashboards
collect_list / collect_setRiskyBuilds an array per key; a popular key can run out of memory
percentile_approx, median (3.4+)MediumExact percentiles need sorting; approximate is usually fine
count_if(cond), any_value (3.5)CheapNewer helpers; on older versions use sum(when(cond,1).otherwise(0))

Pivot (rows to columns) and unpivot

Monthly revenue per storepyspark
wide = (sales.groupBy("store")
             .pivot("month", ["2026-09", "2026-10"])       # ALWAYS pass the values list
             .agg(F.sum("revenue")))
wide.show()
wide.unpivot("store", ["2026-09", "2026-10"], "month", "revenue").show()   # Spark 3.4+
Output
+-----+-------+-------+
|store|2026-09|2026-10|
+-----+-------+-------+
|   S2|     80|    120|
|   S1|    100|    200|
+-----+-------+-------+

+-----+-------+-------+
|store|  month|revenue|
+-----+-------+-------+
|   S2|2026-09|     80|
|   S2|2026-10|    120|
|   S1|2026-09|    100|
|   S1|2026-10|    200|
+-----+-------+-------+

Without the values list, Spark runs an extra job just to find the distinct months. With many distinct values, a pivot creates hundreds of columns; agree the list with the business first.

Subtotals with rollup

Country and city totals plus a grand totalpyspark
r.rollup("country", "city").agg(F.sum("amt").alias("amt"), F.grouping_id().alias("level")) \
 .orderBy("country", "city").show()
Output
+-------+------+---+-----+
|country|  city|amt|level|
+-------+------+---+-----+
|   NULL|  NULL| 22|    3|     <- grand total
|     IN|  NULL| 15|    1|     <- India subtotal
|     IN|   Hyd| 10|    0|
|     IN|  Pune|  5|    0|
|     US|  NULL|  7|    1|
|     US|Austin|  7|    0|
+-------+------+---+-----+

cube does every combination of the columns instead of a hierarchy. grouping_id tells subtotal rows apart from real NULL values.

Interview: How does Spark execute groupBy().sum() physically?

As two HashAggregate operators around one shuffle. Each map task computes partial_sum per key for its partition, the Exchange hash-partitions the partial rows by the grouping key into spark.sql.shuffle.partitions pieces, and each reduce task merges partials into the final sum. If a task's hash map does not fit in memory, Spark spills and falls back to sort-based aggregation. In explain() you see HashAggregate(partial_sum) → Exchange hashpartitioning → HashAggregate(sum).

  • groupBy = partial aggregate → shuffle → final aggregate.
  • Prefer approx_count_distinct when exactness is not required.
  • Pass the values list to pivot.

Joins: every type with real output

A join combines rows from two DataFrames that share a key. The join type decides what happens to rows that have no partner on the other side. Below, the same two tables are joined every possible way so you can compare the results side by side.

orders (left)
order_idcustomer_idamount
o1c11200.0
o2c2450.5
o3c180.0
o4c3300.0
o5c42200.0
o6NULL99.99
customers (right)
customer_idname
c1Asha
c2Ravi
c3Meena
c5John

Highlighted keys have no partner: order o5 references customer c4 who does not exist, order o6 has no customer, and customer c5 (John) has no orders.

The codepyspark
o = orders.select("order_id", "customer_id", "amount")
c = cust.select("customer_id", "name")
o.join(c, "customer_id", how)      # how = "inner", "left", "right", "full", "left_semi", "left_anti"
inner: only matches (4 rows)
custordername
c1o1Asha
c1o3Asha
c2o2Ravi
c3o4Meena
left: all orders (6 rows)
custordername
NULLo6NULL
c1o1Asha
c1o3Asha
c2o2Ravi
c3o4Meena
c4o5NULL
right: all customers (5 rows)
custordername
c1o3Asha
c1o1Asha
c2o2Ravi
c3o4Meena
c5NULLJohn
full: everything (7 rows)
custordername
NULLo6NULL
c1o1Asha
c1o3Asha
c2o2Ravi
c3o4Meena
c4o5NULL

left_semi: orders WITH a customer
custorderamount
c1o11200.0

NULLo699.99

TypeKeepsTypical use
innerRows with a match on both sidesEnrich facts that must have a dimension
leftAll left rows; NULLs where no matchEnrich without losing facts (the safe default for fact → dim)
rightAll right rowsRare; swap tables and use left instead
fullAll rows from bothReconciliation: compare two sources
left_semiLeft rows that have a match; only left columns; never duplicatesWHERE EXISTS: “orders from known customers”
left_antiLeft rows with no matchWHERE NOT EXISTS: orphans, new records, deletes
crossJoinEvery pairSmall calendars × stores; dangerous on big data

Notice the left anti join found orphan order o5 (customer c4 missing) and o6 (no customer). This is a standard data-quality check.

Join syntax variants

Different key names, multiple keys, conditionspyspark
o.join(c, ["customer_id", "region"], "left")                    # several keys with the same names
o.join(c, o.cust_id == c.id, "left")                             # different names: both columns kept
o.alias("o").join(c.alias("c"), F.col("o.cust_id") == F.col("c.id")).select("o.*", "c.name")

# range (non-equi) join: events inside a promotion window
events.join(promos, (events.ts >= promos.start_ts) & (events.ts < promos.end_ts))

Joining on a column name (string or list) keeps one copy of the key. Joining on an expression keeps both copies, and selecting the key later fails with AMBIGUOUS_REFERENCE. Alias both sides or drop one copy.

Interview: left_semi vs inner join — what is the difference?

A semi join returns each left row at most once, even when the right side has several matches, and returns only left columns. An inner join returns one row per matching pair, so duplicates on the right multiply left rows. Semi is also cheaper because it can stop at the first match. Anti join is the safe NOT EXISTS; unlike NOT IN, it is not broken by NULLs on the right side.

  • left = keep all facts; inner = only matched; anti = find missing; semi = filter by existence.
  • Join on column names to avoid duplicate key columns.
  • How Spark physically runs joins (broadcast vs sort-merge) is in Join strategies.

Join pitfalls that break production

Joins rarely fail loudly. They usually return the wrong number of rows. These five mistakes cover most join bugs you will meet at work and in interviews.

1. Duplicate keys on the “lookup” side multiply rows

If customers accidentally has two rows for c1, every order of c1 appears twice and revenue doubles. Always check uniqueness before trusting a join:

Key uniqueness checkpyspark
dups = cust.groupBy("customer_id").count().filter("count > 1")
if dups.limit(1).count() > 0:
    raise ValueError("customers has duplicate customer_id values")

2. A WHERE on the right table turns a left join into an inner join

Wrong

o.join(c, "customer_id", "left")
 .filter(F.col("segment") == "GOLD")

Orders without a customer have segment NULL, so the filter removes them. You just wrote an inner join.

Right

gold = c.filter(F.col("segment") == "GOLD")
o.join(gold, "customer_id", "left")

Filter the right side before joining.

3. NULL keys

NULLs never match. In big left joins, millions of NULL keys all hash to the same partition and create one giant task. Filter them out, join the rest, and union the NULL rows back.

4. Type mismatch

Joining a string customer_id to a bigint one forces a cast on every row, can lose matches ("007" vs 7), and can stop Spark from using statistics. Cast both sides to the same type first.

5. Accidental cross join

A missing or always-true condition multiplies 1 million × 1 million rows. Spark detects some cases (Detected implicit cartesian product). In plans, CartesianProduct or BroadcastNestedLoopJoin is a red flag.

Interview: After a join, your row count went from 10 million to 14 million. Why?

The right side has duplicate keys, so some left rows matched more than once. I would group the right side by the join key and count, find the duplicated keys, then fix it at the source or deduplicate (for a dimension, keep the current version with row_number). If I only need to check existence, I switch to a left semi join.

  • Check key uniqueness on the lookup side.
  • Filter the right table before a left join, not after.
  • Match key types; watch for NULL keys and missing conditions.

Window functions explained

A window function calculates a value for each row by looking at a group of related rows, without collapsing them. A groupBy turns ten rows into one; a window keeps all ten rows and adds a column, such as “rank within department” or “running total so far”. You describe the window with three parts: partitionBy (which rows belong together), orderBy (their order), and the frame (how many neighbouring rows to look at).

Think of a school results board. partitionBy("class") puts each class on its own board. orderBy(marks desc) sorts each board. Now every student gets a rank on their own board, and every student is still listed. That is a window. A groupBy would just print one line per class with the average.

Ranking functions side by side

row_number vs rank vs dense_rankpyspark
from pyspark.sql import Window as W
w = W.partitionBy("dept").orderBy(F.desc("salary"))
emp.select("dept", "name", "salary",
           F.row_number().over(w).alias("row_number"),
           F.rank().over(w).alias("rank"),
           F.dense_rank().over(w).alias("dense_rank")).show()
Output
+----+-----+------+----------+----+----------+
|dept| name|salary|row_number|rank|dense_rank|
+----+-----+------+----------+----+----------+
| ENG| Asha|180000|         1|   1|         1|
| ENG| Ravi|150000|         2|   2|         2|
| ENG|Meena|150000|         3|   2|         2|
| ENG| John|120000|         4|   4|         3|
| OPS| Sara| 95000|         1|   1|         1|
| OPS|   Li| 90000|         2|   2|         2|
| OPS| Omar| 70000|         3|   3|         3|
+----+-----+------+----------+----+----------+
FunctionTiesUse for
row_numberUnique numbers, ties broken arbitrarilyDedup, “keep one row per key”
rankTies share a number, then a gapCompetition ranking (top 3 including ties)
dense_rankTies share a number, no gap“Second highest salary”
percent_rank, ntile(n)Relative position, bucketsQuartiles, percentiles

Looking at neighbours: lag, lead and running totals

Previous order and running total per customerpyspark
wd = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy("order_ts")
orders.filter("customer_id IS NOT NULL").select(
    "customer_id", "order_id", F.to_date("order_ts").alias("d"), "amount",
    F.lag("amount").over(wd).alias("prev_amount"),                    # value from the row before
    F.sum("amount").over(wd.rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0)).alias("running_total"),
).show()
Output
+-----------+--------+----------+------+-----------+-------------+
|customer_id|order_id|         d|amount|prev_amount|running_total|
+-----------+--------+----------+------+-----------+-------------+
|         c1|      o1|2026-11-01|1200.0|       NULL|       1200.0|
|         c1|      o3|2026-11-02|  80.0|     1200.0|       1280.0|
|         c2|      o2|2026-11-01| 450.5|       NULL|        450.5|
|         c3|      o4|2026-11-02| 300.0|       NULL|        300.0|
|         c4|      o5|2026-11-03|2200.0|       NULL|       2200.0|
+-----------+--------+----------+------+-----------+-------------+
Window without partitionBy

W.orderBy("ts") with no partitionBy moves all rows into one partition, so one task does all the work. Spark warns: No Partition Defined for Window operation! Moving all data to a single partition. Fine for a 30-row monthly summary, fatal for a billion-row table.

A window function computes a value per row over a set of related rows without collapsing them. partitionBy defines the groups and causes a shuffle, orderBy sorts within each group, and the frame decides which neighbouring rows are included. I use row_number for dedup, dense_rank for top-N with ties, lag and lead for comparisons with previous events, and running sums with rowsBetween unboundedPreceding and currentRow.

  • Window = keep every row, add a calculated column.
  • partitionBy → shuffle; orderBy → sort; frame → which rows count.
  • No partitionBy = everything in one task.

Window frames: rows vs range

The frame says which rows around the current row a window function uses. rowsBetween counts physical rows (“this row and the one before”). rangeBetween uses the values of the order column (“every row within 1 day before this row”). When dates have gaps, the two give different answers, and only range gives a true time-based window.

A store has sales on 1, 2, 5 and 6 November (no sales on 3 and 4).

“Last 2 rows” vs “last 2 days”pyspark
wr = W.partitionBy("store").orderBy("d")
secs = F.col("d").cast("timestamp").cast("long")            # range needs a number: seconds
sales.select("store", "d", "rev",
    F.sum("rev").over(wr.rowsBetween(-1, 0)).alias("rows_last2"),
    F.sum("rev").over(W.partitionBy("store").orderBy(secs).rangeBetween(-86400, 0)).alias("range_last2days"),
).show()
Output
+-----+----------+---+----------+---------------+
|store|         d|rev|rows_last2|range_last2days|
+-----+----------+---+----------+---------------+
|   S1|2026-11-01|100|       100|            100|
|   S1|2026-11-02|200|       300|            300|
|   S1|2026-11-05|300|       500|            300|
|   S1|2026-11-06|400|       700|            700|
+-----+----------+---+----------+---------------+

On 5 November, “last 2 rows” adds 2 November (200 + 300 = 500) even though that was 3 days earlier. “Last 2 days” (5 Nov and 4 Nov) correctly gives 300. For business metrics like “7-day moving average”, use range over a numeric time column.

Default frames and the last() trap

Window specDefault frame
partitionBy onlyWhole partition
partitionBy + orderByRANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW (rows with equal order values are included together)
Why last() gives the current valuepyspark
sales.select("d", "rev",
    F.last("rev").over(wr).alias("last_default"),
    F.last("rev").over(wr.rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, W.unboundedFollowing)).alias("last_full")).show()
Output
+----------+---+------------+---------+
|         d|rev|last_default|last_full|
+----------+---+------------+---------+
|2026-11-01|100|         100|      400|
|2026-11-02|200|         200|      400|
|2026-11-05|300|         300|      400|
|2026-11-06|400|         400|      400|
+----------+---+------------+---------+

With orderBy, the default frame stops at the current row, so the “last” row in the frame is the current row. Give an explicit full frame when you want the partition's last value. The same default is what makes F.last("x", ignorenulls=True) perfect for forward-filling missing values.

  • rows = count of rows. range = distance in values (time).
  • Use range for true time windows when dates have gaps.
  • Default frame with orderBy ends at the current row.

Nested data: arrays, structs, maps, JSON

Data from APIs, clickstream and event systems is nested: an order contains a customer object and a list of items. Spark stores these as structs (an object with named fields), arrays (a list) and maps (key → value pairs). You reach into structs with dots, turn arrays into rows with explode, and parse JSON text with from_json.

Explode and reach into structspyspark
# schema: order_id string, items array<struct<sku:string,qty:int,price:double>>, tags array<string>
j.select("order_id", F.explode("items").alias("item")) \
 .select("order_id", "item.sku", "item.qty", "item.price").show()

j.select("order_id", F.explode_outer("items").alias("item")).show(truncate=False)
Output
+--------+---+---+-----+
|order_id|sku|qty|price|
+--------+---+---+-----+
|      o1| A1|  2|250.0|
|      o1| B7|  1|999.0|
+--------+---+---+-----+          <- o2 had an empty array and vanished

+--------+--------------+
|order_id|item          |
+--------+--------------+
|o1      |{A1, 2, 250.0}|
|o1      |{B7, 1, 999.0}|
|o2      |NULL          |          <- explode_outer kept it
+--------+--------------+

Higher-order functions: work on arrays without exploding

transform, filter, aggregatepyspark
j.select("order_id",
    F.aggregate("items", F.lit(0.0), lambda acc, i: acc + i["qty"] * i["price"]).alias("order_total"),
    F.transform("items", lambda i: i["sku"]).alias("skus"),
    F.filter("items", lambda i: i["qty"] > 1).alias("multi_qty"),
    F.size("tags"), F.array_contains("tags", "prime"), F.array_join("tags", ",")).show()
Output (first columns)
+--------+-----------+
|order_id|order_total|
+--------+-----------+
|      o1|     1499.0|
|      o2|        0.0|
+--------+-----------+

These lambdas look like Python but they are not UDFs: PySpark turns them into Spark expressions that run in the JVM. They avoid the row explosion of explode + groupBy and are much faster.

Parsing JSON strings

from_json with a schemapyspark
ev_schema = "user string, event string, props map<string,string>, ts timestamp"
raw.select(F.from_json("body", ev_schema).alias("j")).select("j.*").show()

raw.select(F.get_json_object("body", "$.props.page"))      # one field, no schema needed
raw.select(F.schema_of_json(F.lit(sample_json)))           # derive a DDL once, then hard-code it
df.select(F.to_json(F.struct("*")).alias("value"))         # back to JSON (Kafka, SQS, APIs)
Output
+----+-----+--------------+-------------------+
|user|event|         props|                 ts|
+----+-----+--------------+-------------------+
|  u1|click|{page -> home}|2026-11-01 10:00:00|
|NULL| NULL|          NULL|               NULL|     <- the input "not json" became all NULLs
+----+-----+--------------+-------------------+
Spark 4: VARIANT

Spark 4 adds a VARIANT type and parse_json() to store JSON without declaring a schema, while still reading fields quickly (and Iceberg v3 on Glue 6.0 supports variant shredding). On Spark 3.5 you keep raw JSON as a string and parse it with from_json. Spark 4 only

Flatten a deeply nested record

Generic flattener (common interview ask)pyspark
from pyspark.sql.types import StructType, ArrayType

def flatten(df, sep="_"):
    """Expand structs into columns and explode arrays until nothing nested remains."""
    while True:
        nested = [(f.name, f.dataType) for f in df.schema.fields
                  if isinstance(f.dataType, (StructType, ArrayType))]
        if not nested:
            return df
        name, dtype = nested[0]
        if isinstance(dtype, StructType):          # struct -> one column per field
            new_cols = [F.col(f"`{name}`.`{c.name}`").alias(f"{name}{sep}{c.name}") for c in dtype.fields]
            df = df.select([F.col(f"`{c}`") for c in df.columns if c != name] + new_cols)
        else:                                      # array -> one row per element
            df = df.withColumn(name, F.explode_outer(F.col(f"`{name}`")))

Mention the trade-off in an interview: exploding two independent arrays in the same record multiplies rows (3 items × 4 tags = 12 rows). In real pipelines you write a header table and separate child tables (order_items, order_tags), each keyed by the parent id.

  • Struct → dot access. Array → explode / explode_outer. Map → col["key"], map_keys.
  • Higher-order functions (transform, filter, aggregate) avoid explode and stay in the JVM.
  • from_json needs a schema; invalid JSON becomes NULL.

Messy JSON: multi-line files and bad records

Spark expects one JSON object per line; anything else needs multiLine, and broken records need a plan (keep, drop, or fail).

What is it?

Spark's JSON reader by default reads JSON Lines: one complete object per line. API exports and pretty-printed files spread one object over many lines, often inside a [ ... ] array. Real feeds also contain broken lines and wrong types.

Why does it matter?

Read a pretty-printed file the default way and every line is “corrupt”: the job fails or silently produces nothing. Read bad records the wrong way and you lose data without noticing.

How does it work?

multiLine=true makes Spark parse each file as a whole document. The mode option decides what happens to unparseable records: PERMISSIVE (keep, nulls + raw text), DROPMALFORMED (drop), FAILFAST (stop).

How do I check it?

Look at printSchema(): a schema with only _corrupt_record means the format is wrong. Count rows where _corrupt_record is not null.

Try it: a pretty-printed API export

orders_pretty.jsoninput file
[
  { "order_id": "o1", "customer": {"id": 7, "name": "Asha"},
    "items": [{"sku": "A1", "qty": 2}, {"sku": "B2", "qty": 1}] },
  { "order_id": "o2", "customer": {"id": 9, "name": "Ravi"},
    "items": [{"sku": "C3", "qty": 5}] }
]
Default readreal output, Spark 3.5.6
df = spark.read.json("orders_pretty.json")
df.printSchema(); df.show()
root
 |-- _corrupt_record: string (nullable = true)

AnalysisException: Since Spark 2.3, the queries from raw JSON/CSV files are disallowed when the
referenced columns only include the internal corrupt record column ...

Every line is a fragment, so the only column Spark can infer is _corrupt_record, and Spark refuses to query a raw file by that column alone.

With multiLine, then flatten the arrayreal output, Spark 3.5.6
df = spark.read.option("multiLine", "true").json("orders_pretty.json")
(df.select("order_id", F.col("customer.name").alias("customer"), F.explode("items").alias("it"))
   .select("order_id", "customer", "it.sku", "it.qty").show())
+--------+--------+---+---+
|order_id|customer|sku|qty|
+--------+--------+---+---+
|      o1|    Asha| A1|  2|
|      o1|    Asha| B2|  1|
|      o2|    Ravi| C3|  5|
+--------+--------+---+---+

A top-level array becomes one row per element. explode turns the nested items array into one row per item.

multiLine has a cost

With multiLine, a whole file is parsed by one task, because Spark can't split a JSON document in the middle. A single 5 GB pretty-printed file means one slow task. Ask producers for JSON Lines, or pre-split large files.

Try it: bad records

events.jsonl (row o2 has a text amount, row o4 is broken JSON)input file
{"order_id":"o1","amount":120.5}
{"order_id":"o2","amount":"abc"}
{"order_id":"o3","amount":99}
{"order_id":"o4", amount: 10
PERMISSIVE (the default) with a corrupt-record columnreal output, Spark 3.5.6
schema = "order_id STRING, amount DOUBLE, _corrupt_record STRING"
e = spark.read.schema(schema).json("events.jsonl").cache()     # cache: see the note below
e.show(truncate=False)
e.filter("_corrupt_record IS NOT NULL").count()                 # 2
+--------+------+--------------------------------+
|order_id|amount|_corrupt_record                 |
+--------+------+--------------------------------+
|o1      |120.5 |NULL                            |
|o2      |NULL  |{"order_id":"o2","amount":"abc"}|
|o3      |99.0  |NULL                            |
|NULL    |NULL  |{"order_id":"o4", amount: 10    |
+--------+------+--------------------------------+

Good rows load normally; bad rows keep their raw text so you can send them to a quarantine location. Without .cache(), filtering on _corrupt_record alone fails with the same AnalysisException as above.

modeBad recordsOur testUse when
PERMISSIVE + _corrupt_recordKept with nulls; raw text saved4 rows, 2 flaggedProduction: quarantine and alert
DROPMALFORMEDSilently droppedcollect() → 2 rows, but count() → 3Rarely: you lose data with no record
FAILFASTJob fails on the first oneMALFORMED_RECORD_IN_PARSING errorStrict contracts where any bad row is an incident
The count() trap (real result)

With DROPMALFORMED, count() returned 3 but collect() returned 2 rows. count() needs no columns, so Spark skips parsing the fields and never notices that "abc" isn't a number. Never validate a load with count() alone when dropping bad records.

Production pattern: quarantine bad rowspyspark
raw  = spark.read.schema(schema).json(src).cache()
bad  = raw.filter("_corrupt_record IS NOT NULL")
good = raw.filter("_corrupt_record IS NULL").drop("_corrupt_record")

bad.withColumn("run_id", F.lit(run_id)).write.mode("append").json("s3://lake/quarantine/orders/")
if bad.count() > 0.01 * raw.count():
