    raise ValueError("More than 1% bad records: stopping")   # fail loudly on a broken feed
good.write.mode("append").parquet(dst)

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

A partner API drops hourly JSON exports in S3 as pretty-printed arrays. The Glue job reads them with multiLine and an explicit schema (no inference: faster and stable), explodes the arrays, quarantines bad records to S3 with the run ID, raises a CloudWatch alarm if the bad-record rate passes a threshold, and writes good rows to Iceberg.

In the interview

Spark's JSON reader expects JSON Lines. For pretty-printed or array files I set multiLine, knowing each file is parsed by one task. For bad records I use PERMISSIVE with an explicit schema and a _corrupt_record column, cache, split good and bad rows, quarantine the bad ones and fail the job if the rate passes a threshold. I avoid DROPMALFORMED because it loses data silently; even count() can disagree with the real rows.

Q: Why give an explicit schema instead of inferring?

Inference reads the data an extra time, can guess different types on different days (int one day, string the next), and can't flag type errors as corrupt. An explicit schema is faster, stable, and makes bad values show up as corrupt records.

  • Pretty-printed or array JSON → multiLine=true (one task per file).
  • PERMISSIVE + explicit schema + _corrupt_record, cache, quarantine.
  • DROPMALFORMED loses data; count() may not even agree.

XML and fixed-width files

XML needs a reader that knows which tag is a row; fixed-width files are plain text that you cut into columns by position.

XML: what and why

Banks, insurers, healthcare and government systems still send XML. Each record is an element (for example <order>) inside a big document. You tell the reader the row tag, and it turns each such element into a row, with nested elements as structs and repeated ones as arrays.

XML: which version has it

Spark 3.5 has no built-in XML reader (real error below). On AWS Glue, use Glue's XML format. On EMR or local Spark 3.5, add the spark-xml package. Native format("xml") arrives in Spark 4.0 (so Glue 6.0).

Fixed-width: what and why

Mainframe and legacy exports have no separators: each field sits at a fixed position, for example characters 1–7 are the order ID. There's no Spark reader for it; you read lines as text and cut them with substring.

XML on Spark 3.5: the real error

format("xml") on Spark 3.5real output, Spark 3.5.6
spark.read.format("xml").option("rowTag", "order").load(path)
[DATA_SOURCE_NOT_FOUND] Failed to find the data source: xml.

Reading XML on AWS

orders.xmlinput
<orders>
  <order id="o1"><customer>Asha</customer>
    <item sku="A1" qty="2"/><item sku="B2" qty="1"/></order>
  <order id="o2"><customer>Ravi</customer><item sku="C3" qty="5"/></order>
</orders>
Glue (DynamicFrame), Glue/EMR with spark-xml, or Spark 4pyspark
# AWS Glue: built-in XML format
dyf = glueContext.create_dynamic_frame.from_options(
    connection_type="s3", connection_options={"paths": ["s3://raw/orders/"]},
    format="xml", format_options={"rowTag": "order"})
df = dyf.toDF()

# Spark 3.5 elsewhere: add the package (e.g. --packages com.databricks:spark-xml_2.12:0.18.0)
# Spark 4.0+ (Glue 6.0): built in
df = spark.read.format("xml").option("rowTag", "order").schema(xml_schema).load("s3://raw/orders/")

# Typical shape: attributes get a "_" prefix, repeated elements become arrays
flat = df.select(F.col("_id").alias("order_id"), "customer", F.explode("item").alias("it")) \
         .select("order_id", "customer", F.col("it._sku").alias("sku"), F.col("it._qty").alias("qty"))

The two classic XML gotchas: an element that appears once in one record and several times in another is inferred as a struct in one file and an array in another; and inference can be slow on big files. Both are solved by passing an explicit schema (declare item as an array). The row tag can't be a self-closing element.

Fixed-width files

fixed.txt (layout: id 1–7, date 8–15, name 16–30, amount in paise 31–39)input
O0001  20261101Asha Rao       000012050
O0002  20261102Ravi Kumar     000000999
O0003  20261102Meena S        000150000
Cut by positionreal output, Spark 3.5.6
layout = [("order_id", 1, 7), ("order_date", 8, 8), ("customer", 16, 15), ("amount", 31, 9)]  # name, start, length
raw = spark.read.text("fixed.txt")
cols = {name: F.substring("value", start, length) for name, start, length in layout}
fw = raw.select(
    F.trim(cols["order_id"]).alias("order_id"),
    F.to_date(cols["order_date"], "yyyyMMdd").alias("order_date"),
    F.trim(cols["customer"]).alias("customer"),
    (cols["amount"].cast("long") / 100).cast("decimal(12,2)").alias("amount"))
fw.show()
+--------+----------+----------+-------+
|order_id|order_date|  customer| amount|
+--------+----------+----------+-------+
|   O0001|2026-11-01|  Asha Rao| 120.50|
|   O0002|2026-11-02|Ravi Kumar|   9.99|
|   O0003|2026-11-02|   Meena S|1500.00|
+--------+----------+----------+-------+

Keep the layout in a config file (the “copybook” from the source team) rather than in code. substring is 1-based. Validate line length: a line shorter than expected means a broken record; send it to quarantine. Mainframe files may also be EBCDIC-encoded or have packed decimals, which need conversion before this step.

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

An insurance client sends nightly claims as XML and a legacy policy system exports fixed-width files. Glue jobs read XML with the built-in format and an explicit schema, read fixed-width with a layout file from S3, validate record lengths and required fields, quarantine failures, and write both to Iceberg in the same curated model.

In the interview

For XML I set the row tag and pass an explicit schema, because inference can turn a repeated element into a struct in one file and an array in another. On Glue I use the built-in XML format; Spark 3.5 itself needs the spark-xml package, and Spark 4 has XML built in. For fixed-width files I read them as text and cut columns with substring from a layout config, then trim, cast and validate line length.

  • XML: rowTag + explicit schema. Spark 3.5: Glue format or spark-xml; Spark 4: built in.
  • Fixed-width: read.text → substring by layout → trim, cast, validate.

Dates, timestamps and time zones

A timestamp in Spark is a moment in time, stored internally in UTC. When you show it or convert it to a date, Spark uses the session time zone. If one Glue job runs with UTC and another with Asia/Kolkata, the same order can land on different dates. Set the time zone explicitly in every job.

The time zone changes the datepyspark
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.session.timeZone", "UTC")      # set this in every job
t.select("utc",
         F.from_utc_timestamp("utc", "Asia/Kolkata").alias("ist"),
         F.to_date(F.from_utc_timestamp("utc", "Asia/Kolkata")).alias("ist_date"),
         F.to_date("utc").alias("utc_date")).show()
Output
+-------------------+-------------------+----------+----------+
|                utc|                ist|  ist_date|  utc_date|
+-------------------+-------------------+----------+----------+
|2026-11-01 20:30:00|2026-11-02 02:00:00|2026-11-02|2026-11-01|
+-------------------+-------------------+----------+----------+

An order placed at 20:30 UTC on 1 November belongs to 2 November for an Indian business day. Decide which zone the business reports in and convert explicitly.

Everyday date functionspyspark
df.select(
  F.to_date("raw", "dd/MM/yyyy"),                  # parse text with a pattern
  F.to_timestamp("raw_ts", "yyyy-MM-dd HH:mm:ss"),
  F.date_format("order_ts", "yyyy-MM"),            # format as text
  F.date_trunc("hour", "order_ts"),                # cut down to the hour
  F.trunc("order_date", "month"),                  # first day of the month
  F.date_add("order_date", 30), F.add_months("order_date", 1),
  F.datediff(F.current_date(), "order_date"),      # whole days between
  F.last_day("order_date"), F.dayofweek("order_date"),   # 1 = Sunday
  F.unix_timestamp("order_ts"), F.from_unixtime("epoch_seconds"),
)
Spark 3 date parsing is strict

Spark 3 uses Java's modern date parser. Patterns that worked in Spark 2 can fail with SparkUpgradeException: Fail to parse ... in the new parser. Fix the pattern (for example yyyy not YYYY, which means week-year). Setting spark.sql.legacy.timeParserPolicy=LEGACY is a temporary workaround only. Unparseable values become NULL in Spark 3.5 and raise errors in Spark 4 with ANSI on; use try_to_timestamp (3.5+) when you expect bad input.

  • Set spark.sql.session.timeZone explicitly (usually UTC).
  • Convert to the business time zone before taking the date.
  • yyyy (year) not YYYY (week-year).

UDFs and pandas UDFs

A UDF (user-defined function) is your own Python function applied to each row. Spark cannot see inside it, so it cannot optimise around it, and every row must be copied from the JVM into a Python process and back. A pandas UDF sends rows in batches using Apache Arrow and processes a whole pandas Series at once, which is much faster. Use built-in functions first, pandas UDFs second, plain UDFs last.

OptionSpeedNotes
Built-in functions / F.expr()FastestRuns in generated JVM code; Catalyst can optimise
Pandas UDF @pandas_udfFastVectorised with Arrow; good for maths, ML scoring, libraries
Arrow-optimised Python UDF @udf(useArrow=True) (3.5)MediumRow-style code, Arrow transfer
Plain @udfSlowRow by row, pickle
A plain UDF next to the built-in versionpyspark
from pyspark.sql.functions import udf

@udf("string")
def mask_email(e):
    if e is None:                       # you must handle NULL yourself or the task crashes
        return None
    user, _, domain = e.partition("@")
    return user[:2] + "***@" + domain

u.select(mask_email("email").alias("masked"),
         F.regexp_replace("email", r"(^.{2})[^@]*(@.*$)", "$1***$2").alias("builtin")).show()
u.select(mask_email("email")).explain()
Output
+--------------+--------------+
|        masked|       builtin|
+--------------+--------------+
|as***@mail.com|as***@mail.com|
|          NULL|          NULL|
+--------------+--------------+

== Physical Plan ==
*(2) Project [pythonUDF0#22 AS mask(email)#20]
+- BatchEvalPython [mask(email#0)#19], [pythonUDF0#22]
   +- *(1) Project [email#0]
      +- *(1) Scan ExistingRDD[email#0,amount#1]

Same answer, but the plan shows BatchEvalPython: rows leave the JVM. Notice the code generation is split into two parts (*(1) and *(2)) around it. With a pandas UDF you would see ArrowEvalPython.

Pandas UDFspyspark
import pandas as pd
from pyspark.sql.functions import pandas_udf

@pandas_udf("double")
def fx_convert(amount: pd.Series, rate: pd.Series) -> pd.Series:
    return amount * rate                         # whole batch at once

orders.select(fx_convert("amount", "fx_rate").alias("amount_usd"))

# Grouped map: arbitrary pandas code per group (each group must fit in one executor's memory)
def add_zscore(pdf: pd.DataFrame) -> pd.DataFrame:
    pdf["z"] = (pdf["amount"] - pdf["amount"].mean()) / pdf["amount"].std()
    return pdf
orders.groupBy("country").applyInPandas(add_zscore, schema="order_id string, country string, amount double, z double")
Memory

Python workers live outside the JVM heap, inside the executor's memory overhead. Heavy pandas UDFs are a top cause of “Container killed … exceeding memory limits”. Lower spark.sql.execution.arrow.maxRecordsPerBatch (default 10,000) for wide rows, and raise spark.executor.memoryOverhead on EMR or choose a larger / R-type worker on Glue.

Interview: Why are Python UDFs slow, exactly?

Three reasons. Each row is converted from Spark's binary format, pickled, sent over a socket to a Python worker, unpickled, processed and sent back. Catalyst treats the UDF as a black box, so it cannot push filters through it or include it in whole-stage code generation. And the Python process needs its own memory outside the JVM. UDFs can also run more than once per row, so they must be deterministic or marked asNondeterministic().

  • Built-in > pandas UDF > plain UDF.
  • UDFs show as BatchEvalPython / ArrowEvalPython in plans.
  • Python memory lives in overhead, not heap.

Spark SQL, views and the Glue catalog

You can write any transformation as SQL instead of DataFrame code; both turn into the same plan. A temporary view gives a DataFrame a name for SQL queries in this session. A table is registered in a catalog, which on AWS is the Glue Data Catalog, so Athena, Redshift Spectrum and other jobs can find it too.

Mixing SQL and DataFramespyspark
orders.createOrReplaceTempView("orders")              # this SparkSession only
cust.createOrReplaceGlobalTempView("cust")            # all sessions in this app: global_temp.cust

top3 = spark.sql("""
  SELECT * FROM (
    SELECT c.city, o.customer_id, SUM(o.amount) AS revenue,
           ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY c.city ORDER BY SUM(o.amount) DESC) AS rn
    FROM orders o JOIN global_temp.cust c USING (customer_id)
    GROUP BY c.city, o.customer_id) t
  WHERE rn <= 3
""")

# Safe parameters (Spark 3.4+): no string formatting, no SQL injection
spark.sql("SELECT * FROM orders WHERE country = :c AND amount > :a", args={"c": "IN", "a": 100})
# Pass a DataFrame straight into SQL (3.3+)
spark.sql("SELECT country, COUNT(*) FROM {t} GROUP BY country", t=orders)
Catalog commandsspark sql
SHOW DATABASES;
SHOW TABLES IN sales_db;
DESCRIBE EXTENDED sales_db.orders;         -- columns, location, format, properties
SHOW PARTITIONS sales_db.orders;
REFRESH TABLE sales_db.orders;             -- after files changed outside Spark
ANALYZE TABLE sales_db.orders COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS customer_id, amount;
Glue Data Catalog as the metastore

In Glue jobs, turn on Use Glue Data Catalog as the Hive metastore (job parameter --enable-glue-datacatalog). On EMR, set the Hive metastore client factory to the Glue client in the spark-hive-site classification. Then spark.table("db.tbl") and saveAsTable use the same tables Athena sees. For Iceberg, you configure a separate Iceberg catalog backed by Glue (see the Iceberg setup lesson).

Use QUALIFY with caution: it works on Databricks and Snowflake but is not part of open-source Spark 3.5, so on Glue and EMR write the subquery shown above.

  • SQL and DataFrames produce identical plans.
  • Temp view = name for this session; table = registered in the Glue Data Catalog.
  • Use args= parameters instead of f-strings in SQL.

Catalyst: how Spark optimises your query

Catalyst is Spark's query optimiser. Whatever you write (DataFrame code or SQL), Catalyst turns it into a tree of operations, checks that every column exists, rewrites the tree to do less work, and then picks a concrete way to run each step. Tungsten is the execution engine underneath: it stores rows in a compact binary format and generates Java code for each stage so it runs close to hand-written speed.

You tell a satnav “take me to the airport, but stop for fuel”. You did not say which roads. The satnav checks the places exist (analysis), simplifies your request (“the fuel station on the airport road counts”, that is optimisation), then chooses actual roads based on traffic (physical planning). Catalyst is the satnav for your query.

See every phase yourself

orders.filter("amount > 100").select("order_id", "amount").explain(True)real output
== Parsed Logical Plan ==
'Project ['order_id, 'amount]
+- Filter (amount#28 > cast(cast(100 as decimal(3,0)) as decimal(12,2)))
   +- Relation [order_id#26,customer_id#27,amount#28,dt#29] parquet

== Analyzed Logical Plan ==
order_id: string, amount: decimal(12,2)
Project [order_id#26, amount#28]
+- Filter (amount#28 > cast(cast(100 as decimal(3,0)) as decimal(12,2)))
   +- Relation [order_id#26,customer_id#27,amount#28,dt#29] parquet

== Optimized Logical Plan ==
Project [order_id#26, amount#28]
+- Filter (isnotnull(amount#28) AND (amount#28 > 100.00))
   +- Relation [order_id#26,customer_id#27,amount#28,dt#29] parquet

== Physical Plan ==
*(1) Project [order_id#26, amount#28]
+- *(1) Filter (isnotnull(amount#28) AND (amount#28 > 100.00))
   +- *(1) ColumnarToRow
      +- FileScan parquet [order_id#26,amount#28,dt#29] ... PushedFilters: [IsNotNull(amount), GreaterThan(amount,100.00)], ReadSchema: struct<order_id:string,amount:decimal(12,2)>
  • Parsed: names start with ' (unresolved).
  • Analyzed: each column has an ID like amount#28 and a type.
  • Optimized: the double cast was folded into the constant 100.00, and Spark added isnotnull(amount) because a NULL can never be > 100.
  • Physical: the filter was pushed into the Parquet reader (PushedFilters) and only the columns needed are read (ReadSchema has two columns, even though the table has four).

What breaks optimisation

  • Python UDFs: Catalyst cannot look inside, so filters on UDF output cannot be pushed down.
  • Functions or casts on filter columns: cast(amount as string) = '300' is not pushed to the Parquet reader. On a partition column Spark still prunes listed folders, but a catalog table can no longer ask the Glue catalog for just the matching partitions. Write dt BETWEEN '2026-11-01' AND '2026-11-30' instead (see Partition pruning, DPP and projection).
  • Caching early: a filter applied after cache() filters the cached data, not the source.
  • Going through df.rdd: leaves the optimiser entirely.
  • CSV / JSON sources: filters can be applied during parsing, but there are no statistics to skip data.
Interview: Give a concrete example of Catalyst changing your code.

If I write orders.join(customers, "customer_id").filter(orders.country == "IN"), Catalyst pushes the filter below the join so only Indian orders are joined, then further into the Parquet scan as PushedFilters: [EqualTo(country,IN)]. If country is a partition column it becomes PartitionFilters and whole S3 folders are skipped. It also prunes columns: if I select 3 of 80 columns, only those 3 are read.

Interview: What is the difference between Catalyst and Tungsten?

Catalyst decides what to run: it analyses and optimises the query plan and chooses physical operators. Tungsten decides how efficiently it runs: compact binary row format (UnsafeRow), explicit memory management on and off heap, cache-friendly sorting, and whole-stage code generation.

  • Parsed → analyzed → optimized → physical → generated code; AQE adjusts at run time.
  • Pushdown and column pruning are the optimisations you will point at most.
  • UDFs, functions on filter columns and early caches block optimisation.

Reading explain() plans step by step

A physical plan is a tree printed upside down: the data sources are at the bottom and the final result is at the top. Read it from the bottom up, the same direction the data flows. Each line is an operator. Lines starting with Exchange are shuffles, and each one is a stage boundary. Being able to read a plan out loud is the clearest sign of a senior Spark engineer.

How to print planspyspark
df.explain()                 # physical plan only
df.explain(True)             # parsed, analyzed, optimized and physical ("extended")
df.explain("formatted")      # numbered operator tree + details per operator: easiest to read
df.explain("cost")           # with size statistics
spark.sql("EXPLAIN FORMATTED SELECT ...").show(truncate=False)

The query: revenue per country for orders on 1 November over 100, joined to customers. Orders is a Parquet table partitioned by dt; customers is small.

The querypyspark
orders = spark.read.parquet("s3://lake/orders/")          # partitioned by dt
cust   = spark.read.parquet("s3://lake/customers/")       # small table
res = (orders.filter((F.col("dt") == "2026-11-01") & (F.col("amount") > 100))
             .join(cust, "customer_id")
             .groupBy("country").agg(F.sum("amount").alias("rev")))
res.explain()

The final plan after running

Run an action (for example res.collect() or a write), then call res.explain() again. You now see what AQE actually did:

After execution (real output, trimmed)plan
AdaptiveSparkPlan isFinalPlan=true
+- == Final Plan ==
   *(3) HashAggregate(keys=[country#9], functions=[sum(amount#2)])
   +- AQEShuffleRead coalesced
      +- ShuffleQueryStage 1
         +- Exchange hashpartitioning(country#9, 200)
            +- *(2) HashAggregate(keys=[country#9], functions=[partial_sum(amount#2)])
               +- *(2) BroadcastHashJoin [customer_id#1], [customer_id#8], Inner, BuildRight
                     ...
                     +- BroadcastQueryStage 0
+- == Initial Plan ==
   ...

AQEShuffleRead coalesced means the 200 shuffle partitions were merged into a handful, because there are only three countries. ShuffleQueryStage / BroadcastQueryStage are the pieces AQE ran and measured before deciding the rest.

The same join when nothing can be broadcast

Sort-merge join shape (real output, broadcast disabled)plan
*(5) Project [customer_id#27, order_id#26, amount#28, dt#29, country#35, name#36]
+- *(5) SortMergeJoin [customer_id#27], [customer_id#34], Inner
   :- *(2) Sort [customer_id#27 ASC NULLS FIRST], false, 0
   :  +- Exchange hashpartitioning(customer_id#27, 200)
   :     +- *(1) Filter isnotnull(customer_id#27)
   :        +- *(1) ColumnarToRow
   :           +- FileScan parquet [order_id#26,customer_id#27,amount#28,dt#29] ...
   +- *(4) Sort [customer_id#34 ASC NULLS FIRST], false, 0
      +- Exchange hashpartitioning(customer_id#34, 200)
         +- *(3) Filter isnotnull(customer_id#34)
            +- *(3) ColumnarToRow
               +- FileScan parquet [customer_id#34,country#35,name#36] ...

Two Exchanges and two Sorts: both tables are shuffled by customer_id and sorted, then merged. The *(1) … *(5) numbers are whole-stage-codegen blocks. ColumnarToRow converts Parquet's column batches into rows for operators that work row by row.

Operator cheat sheet

OperatorMeaningWhat to check
FileScan / Scan parquetReading filesPartitionFilters present? PushedFilters? ReadSchema has only needed columns?
Exchange hashpartitioning(k, n)Shuffle by keyEach one = stage boundary + disk + network. Can you remove it?
Exchange rangepartitioningShuffle for orderByDo you really need a global sort?
Exchange RoundRobinPartitioningrepartition(n)Intentional?
Exchange SinglePartitionAll data to one taskWindow without partitionBy, global limit: red flag on big data
BroadcastExchange + BroadcastHashJoinBroadcast joinIs the small side really small?
SortMergeJoinShuffle + sort both sidesCould one side be broadcast? (skew=true) means AQE split skew
ShuffledHashJoinShuffle both, hash smaller side per partitionBuild side fits per partition?
BroadcastNestedLoopJoin, CartesianProductNo equality keyUsually a bug or a range join that needs a bucket key
HashAggregate / ObjectHashAggregate / SortAggregateAggregationSortAggregate is the slower fallback
WindowWindow functionPreceded by Exchange + Sort on partition keys
BatchEvalPython / ArrowEvalPythonPython UDF / pandas UDFCan a built-in replace it?
InMemoryTableScanReading a cacheProves the cache is used
ReusedExchangeShuffle output reusedGood: shared work in self-joins/unions
AQEShuffleRead coalesced / skewedAQE adjusted partitionsOnly in the final plan
dynamicpruningexpression(...)Dynamic partition pruningFact table pruned using a filtered dimension
Interview: You see BroadcastNestedLoopJoin in a plan. What do you do?

It means there is no equality condition Spark can hash on: a non-equi join, an OR across keys, or a forgotten key. First I check the condition. If a range join is intended (event time between start and end), I add an equality bucket key, for example the event date or floor(ts / 3600), exploding the range side into the buckets it covers, so Spark can hash-partition and compare only rows inside the same bucket.

Interview: How do you prove partition pruning happened?

In explain() the FileScan shows PartitionFilters: [...] (static pruning) or dynamicpruningexpression (dynamic pruning). In the Spark UI SQL tab, the scan node shows “number of partitions read” and “number of files read”, which should be a small fraction of the table.

I read plans bottom-up. At the scans I check partition filters, pushed filters and the read schema. Then I count Exchanges, because each is a shuffle and a stage boundary, and I look at the join strategy: broadcast hash, sort-merge or shuffled hash. I look for red flags like a single-partition exchange, nested loop joins and Python evaluation nodes. After execution I check the final adaptive plan to see whether AQE coalesced partitions, switched a join or split skewed partitions.

  • Bottom-up. Scans → filters → joins → aggregates → output.
  • Count Exchanges. Each is a shuffle.
  • Run an action, then explain() again for the final AQE plan.

Shuffle internals

During a shuffle, every task in the first stage splits its output into buckets, one per partition of the next stage, and writes them to its executor's local disk. Every task in the next stage then fetches its bucket from all of those files over the network. The executor's disk, the network and serialisation all add cost, and the second stage cannot start until the first has finished.

Shuffle metrics in the Spark UI

Shuffle Write
Bytes the map tasks wrote. Large = expensive boundary. Compare it with the input size: if a join writes 10× its input, rows exploded.
Shuffle Read
Bytes the reduce tasks fetched. Uneven read per task = skew.
Spill (Memory)
Size, in memory form, of data that did not fit and was moved to disk.
Spill (Disk)
The same data after serialisation on disk. Any spill means tasks had too little execution memory for their partition.
Shuffle Read Fetch Wait Time
Time reducers waited for blocks: slow network, busy or dead executors.
Shuffle Remote Reads
Bytes fetched from other machines (vs local reads on the same executor).
Who serves shuffle files when an executor is gone?

With dynamic allocation, an idle executor can be removed while its shuffle files are still needed. On EMR the external shuffle service (a process on each node, port 7337) keeps serving those files. Spark 3 can also track shuffle files and keep such executors alive (spark.dynamicAllocation.shuffleTracking.enabled) or migrate blocks during graceful decommissioning. If shuffle files are truly lost (node died, spot instance reclaimed), the reduce task fails with FetchFailedException and Spark re-runs the parent stage for the missing pieces.

Sizing spark.sql.shuffle.partitions

Aim for 100–200 MB per shuffle partition. Find the biggest Shuffle Write in the Stages tab and divide. With AQE you can set a higher starting number and let AQE merge small partitions.

Interview: How do you reduce shuffle in a job?
  • Filter and select needed columns before wide operations.
  • Broadcast small tables in joins.
  • Aggregate the fact table to the join grain before joining.
  • Reuse partitioning: data already hash-partitioned by a key, joined or aggregated again on the same key with the same partition count, skips the Exchange.
  • Bucketed or storage-partitioned tables for repeated joins on the same key.
  • Remove unnecessary distinct, orderBy and repartition.
  • Use approximate distinct counts where allowed.
  • Map side writes bucketed files to local disk; reduce side fetches its bucket from every map task.
  • Spill = not enough execution memory per task.
  • FetchFailedException = shuffle files lost; parent stage re-runs.

Executor memory model

An executor's memory is divided into regions with different jobs. Most of the JVM heap is a shared pool called unified memory, split dynamically between execution (shuffles, joins, sorts, aggregations) and storage (cached data and broadcast tables). The rest of the heap is user memory for your own objects. Outside the heap sits overhead, used by Python workers, network buffers and native code. Knowing which region ran out tells you how to fix an out-of-memory error.

Try it: how much memory does each task really get?

With a 16 GB heap and 4 cores, unified memory is about 9.4 GB, so each running task gets roughly 2.3 GB of execution memory when all cores are busy and nothing is cached. A task never gets less than 1/(2 × cores) or more than 1/cores of the execution pool. If one partition needs 6 GB to sort, it spills heavily or fails. That is why making partitions smaller (more of them) often fixes memory errors without adding a single gigabyte.

Which error means which region

Error messageRegionUsual causesFix first
java.lang.OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space (executor)HeapSkewed partition, huge window group, collect_list on a popular key, big broadcast tableFix skew, more partitions, fewer cores per executor; then more memory
Container killed by YARN for exceeding physical memory limitsOverheadPython UDFs/pandas, Arrow batches, many cores per executorRaise spark.executor.memoryOverhead, smaller Arrow batches, replace UDFs
Exit code 137 / “Killed” (Glue, EMR Serverless, Kubernetes)Whole containerSame as aboveSame; on Glue a bigger or R-type worker
GC overhead limit exceeded / red GC time in UIHeap churnMillions of small objects, UDFs, RDDsBuilt-ins, smaller partitions, more memory
Driver OutOfMemoryErrorDriver heapcollect(), toPandas(), broadcasting big tables, millions of filesRemove collect, lower broadcast threshold, raise driver memory
Large “Spill (Disk)”ExecutionPartitions too big for per-task memoryMore partitions; fewer cores per executor

Sizing executors on EMR

On Glue you choose a worker type and AWS sizes the executor. On EMR (and EMR Serverless) you set cores and memory. The classic method:

  1. Leave 1 core and about 1 GB per node for the operating system and YARN.
  2. Use about 5 cores per executor: enough parallelism, sane garbage collection, good S3 throughput.
  3. Divide the node's memory between its executors, keep about 10% for overhead.
  4. Leave one executor's worth for the driver / YARN application master.
On AWS

Glue: one executor per worker. G.1X = 4 vCPU / 16 GB, G.2X = 8 / 32, G.4X = 16 / 64, G.8X = 32 / 128, and since 2025 G.12X / G.16X for very large jobs and memory-optimised R.1X–R.8X (double memory per vCPU) for jobs that keep hitting OOM. EMR: the maximizeResourceAllocation setting does this sizing for you; otherwise set it yourself.

Interview: You doubled executor memory and the job still fails with OOM. Why?

Because the problem is probably one partition that is far bigger than the others (skew) or a single group that must be held in memory (a huge window partition or collect_list). More memory per executor shared by the same number of cores does not split that partition. I would check the Stages tab: if the max task's shuffle read is many times the median, it is skew, and I would fix it with broadcast, salting or AQE. If the message is a YARN container kill, the problem is overhead, not heap, so heap increases do not help at all.

  • Heap = reserved 300 MB + unified 60% (storage ↔ execution) + user 40%.
  • Overhead (off-heap) holds Python and network buffers.
  • Heap OOM → smaller/balanced tasks. Container killed → more overhead.

Join strategies: broadcast, sort-merge, hash

Spark has several physical ways to run the same join. If one table is small, it copies that table to every executor and joins locally without moving the big table: a broadcast hash join. If both are big, it shuffles both by the join key, sorts them and merges: a sort-merge join. Choosing correctly is often the difference between a 3-minute job and a 3-hour job.

StrategyChosen whenShufflesRisk
Broadcast hash joinEqui-join and one side under spark.sql.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold (10 MB, estimated) or a broadcast hint0Driver OOM when collecting; executor OOM if the “small” side is large in memory; 300 s broadcast timeout; hard limit 8 GB
Sort-merge joinDefault for large equi-joins2 (+ 2 sorts)Slow; skewed keys make giant tasks
Shuffled hash joinHint, or AQE converts SMJ when every partition's build side is small2 (no sort)OOM if a partition's build side does not fit
Broadcast nested loopNon-equi join with a small side0Compares every pair
Cartesian productNon-equi join, both large / crossJoin—Row explosion
Which side can be broadcast

In a left outer join only the right side can be broadcast, because every left row must be kept and checked. In a right outer join, only the left. A full outer join cannot use a broadcast hash join. Inner joins can broadcast either side.

Controlling the choicepyspark
from pyspark.sql.functions import broadcast

orders.join(broadcast(cust), "customer_id")             # force a broadcast
orders.join(cust.hint("merge"), "customer_id")           # force sort-merge
orders.join(cust.hint("shuffle_hash"), "customer_id")    # force shuffled hash
spark.sql("SELECT /*+ BROADCAST(c) */ * FROM orders o JOIN cust c USING (customer_id)")

spark.conf.set("spark.sql.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold", 64 * 1024 * 1024)   # 64 MB; -1 disables
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.broadcastTimeout", 600)                          # seconds (default 300)
Why a “9 MB” table can break a broadcast

The threshold is compared with Spark's estimate, which for Parquet is the compressed file size. Nine megabytes of snappy Parquet can be 80+ MB once decompressed into a hash table on every executor. If broadcasts cause driver or executor OOM, lower the threshold or remove the hint. AQE also has its own threshold, spark.sql.adaptive.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold, applied to real post-filter sizes at run time.

Avoiding the shuffle for repeated joins

Bucketing (Spark tables) and storage-partitioned joins (Iceberg)pyspark
# Spark bucketing: both tables written with the same bucket spec, joined without Exchange
orders.write.bucketBy(64, "customer_id").sortBy("customer_id").saveAsTable("db.orders_b")
cust.write.bucketBy(64, "customer_id").sortBy("customer_id").saveAsTable("db.cust_b")
spark.table("db.orders_b").join(spark.table("db.cust_b"), "customer_id")   # no Exchange

# Iceberg (Spark 3.4+): tables partitioned by bucket(64, customer_id) can join without a shuffle
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.sources.v2.bucketing.enabled", "true")             # storage-partitioned join
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.iceberg.planning.preserve-data-grouping", "true")   # keep Iceberg bucket grouping
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.requireAllClusterKeysForCoPartition", "false")

Spark bucketing only works through saveAsTable, is not understood by Athena, and can create many files. On AWS lakehouses, Iceberg's bucket(n, col) transform with storage-partitioned joins is the modern equivalent.

Interview: When is a broadcast join a bad idea?

When the “small” side is large in memory after decompression (driver must collect it and every executor must hold it), when it is the preserved side of an outer join (not allowed), when building it is slow and hits the broadcast timeout, or when executors are already memory-tight. In those cases a sort-merge join (with AQE skew handling) or a shuffled hash join is safer.

  • Small side → broadcast hash join, zero shuffles.
  • Both large → sort-merge join: 2 shuffles + 2 sorts.
  • Threshold compares estimated compressed size. AQE re-decides using real sizes.

Cost-based optimizer, statistics and join reorder

If Spark knows how many rows each table and filter produces, it can choose a better join order and join type.

What is it?

Catalyst normally uses rules (push filters down, prune columns). The cost-based optimizer (CBO) adds numbers: row counts, distinct values, min/max, nulls. With them Spark estimates the size of each step and picks the cheapest plan.

Why does it matter?

In a query joining a big fact table with several dimensions, the order matters: joining the most selective dimension first shrinks the data before the other joins. Without statistics, Spark joins in the order you wrote them.

How does it work?

ANALYZE TABLE computes statistics and stores them in the catalog. With spark.sql.cbo.enabled and spark.sql.cbo.joinReorder.enabled on, Spark uses them to reorder joins and estimate sizes.

How do I check it?

EXPLAIN COST <query> shows Statistics(sizeInBytes=…, rowCount=…) for every step, and you can see the join order.

Try it: three tables, before and after

We created sales (2 million rows), customers (50,000; only 1% in India) and products (1,000), and ran this query on Spark 3.5.6 (broadcast and AQE turned off to see the optimizer alone):

The queryspark sql
SELECT c.country, p.category, SUM(s.amount)
FROM sales s
JOIN products  p ON s.product_id  = p.product_id
JOIN customers c ON s.customer_id = c.customer_id
WHERE c.country = 'IN'
GROUP BY c.country, p.category
Before: no statistics, CBO offreal EXPLAIN COST, trimmed
Join Inner, (customer_id = customer_id), Statistics(sizeInBytes=17.1 PiB)
:- Join Inner, (product_id = product_id), Statistics(sizeInBytes=133.3 GiB)
:  :- Relation sales     Statistics(sizeInBytes=23.2 MiB)
:  +- Relation products  Statistics(sizeInBytes=5.7 KiB)
+- Filter (country = IN) Statistics(sizeInBytes=197.3 KiB)
   +- Relation customers

Spark joins in written order: sales × products first (all 2 million rows), then customers. Size estimates without row counts are wild guesses (17 petabytes!), which can also lead to wrong choices about broadcasting.

Collect statisticsreal output
ANALYZE TABLE sales     COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS customer_id, product_id;
ANALYZE TABLE customers COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS customer_id, country;
ANALYZE TABLE products  COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS product_id;
DESCRIBE EXTENDED customers country;
Statistics = 202068 bytes, 50000 rows          (table level)
num_nulls = 0   distinct_count = 2   avg_col_len = 2   max_col_len = 2   (column country)
After: statistics + CBO + join reorderreal EXPLAIN COST, trimmed
Join Inner, (product_id = product_id), Statistics(sizeInBytes=51.4 MiB, rowCount=9.98E+5)
:- Join Inner, (customer_id = customer_id), Statistics(rowCount=9.98E+5)
:  :- Relation sales                Statistics(rowCount=2.00E+6)
:  +- Filter (country = IN)       Statistics(rowCount=2.50E+4)
:     +- Relation customers         Statistics(rowCount=5.00E+4)
+- Relation products                Statistics(rowCount=1.00E+3)

What changed: Spark now joins sales with the filtered customers first, then products, and every estimate has a real row count. What's still off: it estimates 25,000 Indian customers (half, because there are 2 distinct countries) while the truth is 500. Turning on histograms (spark.sql.statistics.histogram.enabled=true before ANALYZE) improves estimates for skewed columns.

SettingDefault (3.5)Meaning
spark.sql.cbo.enabledfalseUse statistics to estimate plan costs
spark.sql.cbo.joinReorder.enabledfalseReorder multi-way inner joins by cost
spark.sql.statistics.histogram.enabledfalseCollect value histograms (better selectivity)
spark.sql.cbo.planStats.enabledfalseShow row counts from the catalog in the plan

CBO vs AQE

CBO: before running

Uses stored statistics from ANALYZE. Can reorder joins. Only as good as the statistics, which go stale when data changes.

AQE: while running

Uses real sizes measured after each shuffle. Switches to broadcast, coalesces partitions, splits skew. On by default. Cannot reorder joins that already ran.

In practice on AWS, most teams rely on AQE plus good filters and broadcast hints, and use CBO for big star-schema queries on stable catalog tables. Iceberg tables can also store column statistics in Puffin files (CALL ... system.compute_table_stats in recent Iceberg releases), and Glue Data Catalog can compute column statistics for Glue tables.

When it does not work

ProblemFix
Statistics computed once, never refreshed: estimates are wrong after growthRe-run ANALYZE after big loads (schedule it), or rely on AQE
Querying files by path (spark.read.parquet): there is no catalog to store statsUse catalog tables for CBO
Outer joins or joins with complex conditionsJoin reorder only reorders inner joins
Skewed column, estimate far from realityHistograms, or a broadcast hint where you know better

In the interview

By default Catalyst is rule-based. The cost-based optimizer uses table and column statistics from ANALYZE TABLE, like row counts and distinct values, to estimate the size of each step, choose join types and reorder multi-way inner joins so the most selective joins happen first. It's off by default, the statistics go stale, and it needs catalog tables, so in practice I rely on AQE at runtime and use CBO for large star-schema queries, checking the effect with EXPLAIN COST.

Q: CBO or AQE: which would you trust more?

AQE, because it uses actual sizes measured at runtime, while CBO uses stored statistics that may be stale. They complement each other: CBO can pick a good join order up front, which AQE can't change later, and AQE corrects size-based decisions as the job runs.

  • ANALYZE TABLE ... COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS → row counts, distinct values.
  • cbo.enabled + joinReorder.enabled → selective joins first. Check with EXPLAIN COST.
  • Stats go stale; AQE uses real runtime sizes.

Adaptive Query Execution (AQE)

Before a query runs, Spark only has estimates of data sizes, and estimates are often wrong. AQE lets Spark pause after each shuffle stage, look at the real sizes it just produced, and re-plan the rest of the query. It is on by default in Spark 3.2+ (so on Glue 5.x and EMR 7.x). It does three main things: merges tiny partitions, switches to a cheaper join, and splits oversized partitions in joins.

Key settings (Spark 3.5 defaults)

SettingDefaultMeaning
spark.sql.adaptive.enabledtrueTurns AQE on
spark.sql.adaptive.coalescePartitions.enabledtrueMerge small shuffle partitions
spark.sql.adaptive.advisoryPartitionSizeInBytes64 MBTarget size when merging / splitting
spark.sql.adaptive.coalescePartitions.parallelismFirsttrueWhen true, AQE ignores the advisory size and only respects a 1 MB minimum to keep parallelism high. Spark docs recommend false on busy clusters so partitions really grow to the advisory size
spark.sql.adaptive.coalescePartitions.initialPartitionNum(= shuffle.partitions)Starting count before merging; set high and let AQE merge
spark.sql.adaptive.skewJoin.enabledtrueSplit skewed join partitions
spark.sql.adaptive.skewJoin.skewedPartitionFactor5.0× median to count as skewed
spark.sql.adaptive.skewJoin.skewedPartitionThresholdInBytes256 MBMinimum size to count as skewed
spark.sql.adaptive.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold(= autoBroadcastJoinThreshold)Runtime broadcast limit
What AQE cannot do
  • Skew handling works for joins only, not for skewed groupBy or window partitions. Those still need salting or two-phase aggregation.
  • It cannot help the first stage (the file scan) because no shuffle has happened yet. One huge gzip file stays one task.
  • For outer joins it can only split the preserved side: the left side of a left join, the right side of a right join. Skew on the other side, or in a full outer join, is not split.
  • It does not apply to Structured Streaming queries.
Interview: AQE is on, but one task still runs for an hour. Why?

Possible reasons: the skew is in an aggregation or window, which AQE does not split; the slow partition is below the 256 MB threshold but each row is expensive (a UDF, a regex); the skew is in the scan stage (one huge unsplittable file); the skew is on the non-preserved side of an outer join (for example the right side of a left join), which AQE cannot split; or the thresholds do not match my data. I check the final plan or the SQL tab for skew=true. If AQE did not act, I lower the thresholds, broadcast, or salt.

AQE re-optimises the query at every shuffle boundary using actual statistics. It coalesces small shuffle partitions toward the advisory size, converts sort-merge joins to broadcast or shuffled hash joins when a side turns out small, and splits skewed join partitions that exceed both five times the median and 256 megabytes. It does not fix skew in aggregations or windows, and it cannot help the initial scan stage.

  • AQE = runtime re-planning after each shuffle.
  • Coalesce, switch join, split skew (joins only).
  • Skewed = > 5× median AND > 256 MB.

Fault tolerance, retries and speculation

Spark expects machines to fail. It remembers how every partition was produced (its lineage), so when a task or executor fails it re-runs only the missing work somewhere else. If the same task keeps failing, the job stops. Knowing the retry rules helps you read logs: a task that fails four times on different machines is a data problem, not a machine problem.

What failsWhat Spark doesSetting
One task attemptRetries it, possibly on another executorspark.task.maxFailures = 4
Same task fails 4 timesAborts the stage → job fails: Task 212 in stage 12.0 failed 4 times
Executor lostRe-runs its running tasks; recomputes lost cached blocks from lineage; lost shuffle output triggers parent-stage re-runs
Shuffle fetch failsMarks map output lost, re-runs the parent stage for missing partitions, then retriesspark.stage.maxConsecutiveAttempts = 4
Driver lostApplication fails. (Glue and Step Functions may retry the whole job.)Job-level retries

Speculative execution

With spark.speculation=true, Spark launches a second copy of tasks that are much slower than the median and keeps whichever finishes first. It helps when one machine is unhealthy. It does not help with skew (the copy has the same giant partition) and is dangerous with non-idempotent side effects, such as writing to a database from foreachPartition.

Checkpointing

df.checkpoint() writes the DataFrame to reliable storage (set spark.sparkContext.setCheckpointDir("s3://...")) and cuts the lineage, so recovery and planning no longer walk back through hundreds of steps. Use it in loops or iterative algorithms where the plan grows each iteration. localCheckpoint() is faster but stores data on executors, so it is not fault-tolerant.

Interview: How do you tell a data problem from an infrastructure problem in a failed job?

If the same task index fails on every attempt across different executors, and other tasks succeed, the partition itself is the problem: a skewed key, a poison record, a NULL reaching a UDF. If random tasks on one host fail, or executors are lost with exit codes from the cluster (spot reclaim, node failure, disk full), it is infrastructure or resources. The Executors tab and the failed-task error column show both patterns.

  • 4 attempts per task, then the job fails.
  • Same task index failing everywhere = data issue.
  • Speculation fixes bad nodes, not skew.

Spark UI tour: every tab

The Spark UI is a web page the driver serves while your application runs (port 4040). It shows every job, stage and task with timings, data sizes and errors. When the application ends, the live UI disappears; the History Server rebuilds the same pages from event logs the driver wrote. On AWS you usually open it from the Glue console, the EMR console or a self-hosted History Server.

Spark 3.5.6JobsStagesStorageEnvironmentExecutorsSQL / DataFrameStructured Streaming
orders-daily — Completed Jobs (3)
Job IdDescriptionSubmittedDurationStages: Succeeded/TotalTasks: Succeeded/Total
2parquet at orders_etl.py:8803:14:0238 min2/2 (1 skipped)600/600
1count at orders_etl.py:7103:09:404.3 min2/2601/601
0broadcast exchange03:09:318 s1/112/12

The description column shows the action and the line number in your script. Start here: which action is slow?

Click job 2 and you get its stages. One job splits into stages at every shuffle, and the shuffle columns show where data moved between them:

Spark 3.5.6JobsStagesStorageEnvironmentExecutorsSQL / DataFrame
Details for Job 2 — Completed Stages (2), Skipped Stages (1)
Stage IdDescriptionDurationTasks: Succeeded/TotalInputOutputShuffle ReadShuffle Write
9parquet at orders_etl.py:8836 min400/40058 GB61 GB
8parquet at orders_etl.py:882.1 min200/20096 GB61 GB
7count at orders_etl.py:71 (skipped)—0/12

Read it as a pipeline: stage 8 reads 96 GB from S3 and writes 61 GB of shuffle; stage 9 reads that 61 GB and writes the output. Stage 9 is where the time goes, so open it next. Stage 7 was skipped because its shuffle output already existed from job 1.

Connecting your code to the UI

Your code (line 88)→Physical plan (SQL tab)→Job→Stages (cut at Exchanges)→Tasks (one per partition)→Executors (where tasks ran)

Each Exchange in the SQL tab's plan becomes a stage boundary in the Stages tab. That is how you trace a slow stage back to the operator, and then to the line of code that created it.

There is no “Shuffle” tab

Some cheat sheets show a Shuffle tab, but the real Spark UI doesn't have one. Shuffle numbers appear in three places: the Shuffle Read and Shuffle Write columns of the Stages tab, per executor in the Executors tab, and on Exchange nodes in the SQL tab.

TabWhat it showsGo there when
JobsOne row per job with duration, stage and task counts; an event timeline of executors added and removedFind the slow action; see executors being lost mid-run
StagesEvery stage: duration, task count, input, output, shuffle read/write, spill. Click a stage for task-level detailMost debugging happens here: skew, spill, GC, slow or failing tasks
StorageCached DataFrames: fraction cached, size in memory and on diskCheck a cache is used and fully in memory
EnvironmentEvery Spark property, JVM option and classpath entryProve that a config you passed in Glue or EMR actually took effect
ExecutorsPer executor: cores, memory, tasks, failures, GC time, shuffle, log links, thread dumpsGC problems, dead executors, a bad host, logs of one executor
SQL / DataFrameEach query as a graph of physical operators with runtime metrics (rows, files, bytes, time) and the final AQE planWhich operator is slow, row explosion after a join, whether pruning and AQE happened
Structured StreamingInput rate, processing rate, batch duration, state rowsStreaming lag
Where to start every investigation
  1. Jobs: which job (line of code) takes the time?
  2. Stages: which stage inside it, and what do its task metrics say?
  3. SQL: which operator in the plan does that stage belong to?
  4. Executors and logs: errors, GC and lost executors.
  • Live UI on port 4040; after the job ends, use the History Server (event logs).
  • Jobs → Stages → SQL → Executors is the investigation order.
  • Environment tab proves configuration took effect.

Stages tab: diagnose from task metrics

Click a stage and Spark shows Summary Metrics for Completed Tasks: the minimum, 25th percentile, median, 75th percentile and maximum of each metric across all tasks in that stage. Comparing Max with Median is the fastest way to diagnose most performance problems. Below are the five patterns you will see again and again.

Pattern 1: healthy stage

SparkStages › Stage 4
MetricMin25thMedian75thMax
Duration11 s13 s14 s15 s19 s
GC Time0.1 s0.2 s0.2 s0.3 s0.5 s
Shuffle Read Size118 MB124 MB127 MB131 MB140 MB
Spill (Disk)00000

Max is close to median, partitions are about 128 MB, no spill, GC is tiny. Nothing to fix.

Pattern 2: data skew

SparkStages › Stage 12 (199/200 tasks)
MetricMin25thMedian75thMax
Duration2 s4 s5 s6 s38 min
GC Time0 ms40 ms60 ms90 ms2.1 min
Shuffle Read Size / Records12 MB / 90 K48 MB / 380 K52 MB / 410 K58 MB / 450 K41.3 GB / 310 M
Spill (Disk)000018.6 GB

One task reads 800× the median. The stage sits at 199/200 for half an hour. Diagnosis: skew on this stage's shuffle key. Next: SQL tab to see if it is a join or an aggregate, then df.groupBy(key).count().orderBy(F.desc("count")).show() to find the hot key. Fixes: Data skew.

Pattern 3: too few partitions

SparkStages › Stage 7 (8 tasks, cluster has 160 cores)
MetricMin25thMedian75thMax
Duration14 min15 min16 min17 min18 min
Input Size11 GB11 GB12 GB12 GB12 GB
Spill (Disk)9 GB10 GB11 GB11 GB12 GB

Every task is uniformly slow and spills; 152 cores are idle. Causes: 8 large gzip files, a coalesce(8) upstream, or a JDBC read without partitioning. Fix: splittable input, repartition to 2–3× cores, or a parallel JDBC read.

Pattern 4: thousands of tiny tasks

SparkStages › Stage 0 (48,212 tasks)
MetricMin25thMedian75thMax
Duration40 ms80 ms0.1 s0.2 s2 s
Scheduler Delay10 ms60 ms0.2 s0.4 s3 s
Input Size3 KB20 KB48 KB90 KB1 MB

Scheduler delay is larger than the work. Cause: small files (often from a streaming or Firehose writer) or far too many shuffle partitions. Fix: compact the source, raise maxPartitionBytes and lower openCostInBytes, let AQE coalesce. See Small files.

Pattern 5: memory pressure

SparkStages › Stage 9
MetricMin25thMedian75thMax
Duration3 min4 min4.5 min5 min7 min
GC Time1.1 min1.4 min1.6 min1.8 min2.6 min
Spill (Memory)4 GB5 GB5 GB6 GB7 GB

A third of every task's time is garbage collection. Causes: partitions too big for per-task memory, Python UDFs or collect_list building big objects, too many cores per executor. Fix: more, smaller partitions; built-ins instead of UDFs; more memory per core (bigger Glue worker or R-type).

Finding the straggler task

Below the summary, the Tasks table lists every task. Sort it by Duration, slowest first, and compare the slowest task's row with a normal one:

SparkStages › Stage 12 › Tasks (sorted by Duration)
IndexStatusExecutorDurationGC TimeShuffle Read Size / RecordsSpill (Disk)Errors
212RUNNING1438 min2.1 min41.3 GB / 310 M18.6 GB
87SUCCESS36 s90 ms58 MB / 450 K0
15SUCCESS95 s60 ms52 MB / 410 K0
140SUCCESS25 s55 ms51 MB / 400 K0

Task 212 reads about 700× more records than the others. That is a data problem (a hot key). If it were a machine problem, the slow task would have a normal record count and the slowness would follow one executor. Check the Executor column for that.

Other parts of the stage page

Aggregated Metrics by Executor
Is one executor doing all the work, or failing repeatedly? Repeated failures on one host point to a bad node or full disk.
Event Timeline
Coloured bars per task: scheduler delay, deserialisation, shuffle read, computing, shuffle write, result serialisation. Long green compute = CPU-bound. Long shuffle read = network or slow peers. Gaps = not enough tasks or waiting for executors.
DAG Visualization
The operators in this stage (e.g. Scan → Filter → Exchange). Links stages to your code.
Tasks table
Sort by Duration descending. For the slowest task check input/shuffle records, GC, spill, locality and the Errors column (full stack traces of failed attempts).
Locality Level
PROCESS_LOCAL (data in this JVM) > NODE_LOCAL > RACK_LOCAL > ANY. With S3 everything is effectively ANY; that is normal.
Interview: A stage is stuck at 199 of 200 tasks. What do you do?

That is the classic skew signature. I open the stage's summary metrics and compare max and median shuffle read: if the max is many times larger, one key dominates. I find which operator it is in the SQL tab, count rows per key to identify the hot key (often NULL, an empty string or a default ID), then choose a fix: filter or separately handle junk keys, broadcast the other side, confirm AQE skew join is splitting it, or salt the key. If max and median are similar but the last task is slow, I check that task's executor for GC or a bad node, and consider speculation.

  • Max ≫ median = skew. All tasks slow + few tasks = under-partitioned.
  • Tiny inputs + scheduler delay = small files / too many partitions.
  • High GC + spill = memory pressure per task.

SQL tab, Executors, Storage and Environment

The SQL / DataFrame tab shows the physical plan of each query as boxes with real numbers measured while it ran: how many rows came out of each operator, how many files were read, how long each block took. It is where you catch row explosions, missing pruning and AQE decisions. The Executors tab shows the health of each worker process.

Metrics worth looking for on SQL tab nodes

NodeMetricWhat it tells you
Scannumber of files read, size of files read, partitions read, metadata timePruning worked? Small-file problem? Slow S3 listing?
Filternumber of output rowsA filter keeping 99% of rows is not helping much
Joinnumber of output rowsRow explosion from duplicate keys
Exchangeshuffle bytes written, records, partitionsSize of each shuffle
AQEShuffleReadnumber of partitions, coalesced / skewed countsWhat AQE did
WholeStageCodegen (n)durationWhich fused block takes the time
BroadcastExchangedata size, time to collect, time to buildIs the broadcast actually small?

Executors tab: red flags

SparkExecutors
Executor IDAddressStatusCoresActive TasksFailed TasksTask Time (GC Time)Shuffle ReadLogs
driver10.0.1.12Active0000 (0)0stdout stderr
110.0.3.17Active4402.1 h (6 min)48 GBstdout stderr
210.0.5.9Active4402.0 h (38 min)51 GBstdout stderr
310.0.4.2Dead4071.2 h (12 min)22 GBstdout stderr

Executor 2: GC is 30% of task time (shown in red by the real UI above 10%). Executor 3: dead with 7 failed tasks; open its stderr to see why (OOM kill, spot reclaim, disk full).

Storage and Environment

  • Storage: each cached DataFrame with “Fraction Cached”. Below 100% means partitions were evicted or never computed, so some actions recompute from source. A cache you expected but cannot see was never filled by an action.
  • Environment: search for the property you set (spark.sql.shuffle.partitions, spark.executor.memoryOverhead). If it is missing, it never reached Spark, for example because a Glue --conf value was malformed or a resource setting was set with spark.conf.set after the session started.
  • SQL tab = plan with real numbers. Look for row explosions and pruning.
  • Executors tab = GC, failures, dead executors, log links.
  • Environment tab = proof of configuration.

History Server on Glue, EMR and EMR Serverless

While your job runs, the driver writes an event log: a file of JSON lines recording every job, stage and task start and end, their metrics, and the SQL plans. The Spark History Server reads those files and rebuilds the full Spark UI, so you can investigate a job that finished, or failed at 3 a.m., hours later. On AWS you mainly need to know where event logs go and how to open the UI.

AWS Glue

  1. Turn it on: in the job's Job details enable Spark UI and set an S3 path, or pass the job parameters --enable-spark-ui true and --spark-event-logs-path s3://my-bucket/sparkui-logs/.
  2. View it in the console: open the job run and choose Spark UI. It works while the job runs and after it ends, for Glue 3.0+ job runs that use the Standard log format (the default for newer jobs; runs before 20 November 2023 use a legacy format and are not supported). The IAM user needs the Glue Spark UI permissions (the UseGlueStudio managed permissions, or glue:RequestLogParsing plus the read APIs).
  3. Rolling logs: on Glue 4.0+ rolling event logs are supported, raising the viewable size to about 2 GB (0.5 GB without rolling). Streaming jobs roll by default.
  4. Limitation: the console's serverless Spark UI cannot read event logs in a bucket reachable only from your VPC. In that case, or for very old runs, launch your own History Server: AWS documents a Docker image and a CloudFormation template that point a History Server at the S3 event-log path.
Glue job parameters for observabilityaws cli
aws glue update-job --job-name orders-daily --job-update '{
  "Role": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/GlueJobRole",
  "Command": {"Name": "glueetl", "ScriptLocation": "s3://code/orders_etl.py"},
  "GlueVersion": "5.1",
  "DefaultArguments": {
    "--enable-spark-ui": "true",
    "--spark-event-logs-path": "s3://my-logs/sparkui/orders-daily/",
    "--enable-metrics": "true",
    "--enable-observability-metrics": "true",
    "--enable-job-insights": "true"
  }}'

Observability metrics publish CloudWatch metrics such as worker utilisation, per-stage skewness and memory usage. Job run insights analyses a failed run and writes a root-cause summary into the run's CloudWatch logs pointing at the failing line of your script. Both are cheap to enable and help in production on-call.

Amazon EMR on EC2

  • On the cluster: History Server at http://<primary-node>:18080, YARN ResourceManager at :8088 (reach them with an SSH tunnel or SSM port forwarding).
  • Persistent application UIs: in the EMR console, the cluster's Applications tab opens the Spark History Server hosted by AWS, even after the cluster has terminated, for a retention period. No tunnel needed.
  • By default EMR writes event logs to HDFS (/var/log/spark/apps), which disappears with the cluster. For long-term history, set spark.eventLog.dir to an S3 path and run a History Server against it.

EMR Serverless and EMR on EKS

  • EMR Serverless: each job run has a Spark UI link in EMR Studio / the console: live UI while it runs, History Server afterwards. Logs go to managed storage and, if configured, your S3 log URI.
  • EMR on EKS: persistent UIs from the virtual cluster's job runs, or a self-hosted History Server reading event logs from S3.
Open-source configs (EMR, Kubernetes, self-managed)spark conf
spark.eventLog.enabled           true
spark.eventLog.dir               s3://my-logs/spark-events/
spark.eventLog.rolling.enabled   true          # long-running apps: roll files
spark.eventLog.compress          true
spark.history.fs.logDirectory    s3://my-logs/spark-events/   # where the History Server reads
Interview: A job failed at 3 a.m. and the cluster is gone. How do you investigate?

First the orchestrator (Step Functions or Airflow) for the job run ID and the error message. Then the logs: on Glue the run's driver stream in the /aws-glue/jobs/error log group; on EMR the driver container's stderr under the cluster's S3 log URI. I look for the first exception and the last Caused by. Then the Spark UI from event logs (Glue console Spark UI, EMR persistent UI or a History Server) to find the failed stage, the failed task's error, executor-lost events and the SQL plan. Then CloudWatch metrics for memory and disk around the failure. If the cause is still unclear I reproduce on a sample with the same Glue version.

  • Glue: --enable-spark-ui + --spark-event-logs-path, view in the console.
  • EMR: persistent application UIs in the console, or History Server on :18080.
  • VPC-only buckets need a self-hosted History Server.

Where driver and executor logs live

Every Spark process writes two log streams: stdout (anything your code prints) and stderr (Spark's own log messages and stack traces). The driver has one pair; every executor has its own pair. Errors raised inside tasks appear first in the executor's stderr and are then summarised in the driver's log. Knowing where each platform stores them saves a lot of time during an incident.

AWS Glue 5.x

CloudWatch log groupContainsLog streams
/aws-glue/jobs/errorSpark's system and daemon logs (log4j), stack traces, and messages from the Glue logger (glue_context.get_logger())<jobRunId>-driver, <jobRunId>-<executorNumber>
/aws-glue/jobs/outputYour stdout and stderr (for example print() on the driver)Same naming
Progress bar streamStage progress every 5 seconds (jobs that create a GlueContext)<jobRunId>-progress-bar

In Glue 5.0 and later all jobs log in real time; you can change the group and stream names with --custom-logGroup-prefix and --custom-logStream-prefix. With a security configuration that encrypts logs, group names include the security configuration and role. In Glue 4.0 and earlier, real-time logs required enabling continuous logging, which writes to /aws-glue/jobs/logs-v2.

Amazon EMR on EC2

WhatWhere (under the cluster's S3 log URI)
Step output, client-mode drivers3://my-emr-logs/<cluster-id>/steps/<step-id>/stderr.gz
Cluster-mode driver (YARN ApplicationMaster).../containers/application_<id>/container_<id>_01_000001/stderr.gz
Executors.../containers/application_<id>/container_<id>_01_00000N/stderr.gz
On the clusteryarn logs -applicationId application_<id>

EMR pushes logs to S3 about every five minutes, so the very last lines of a crash may arrive late.

EMR Serverless

s3://my-logs/applications/<app-id>/jobs/<job-run-id>/SPARK_DRIVER/stderr.gz and .../SPARK_EXECUTOR/<n>/stderr.gz when an S3 log URI is configured; CloudWatch logging is also available.

print() inside a UDF

Code inside a UDF, foreach or mapPartitions runs on executors. Its print() output goes to that executor's stdout, never to the driver log. For debugging, write small diagnostic DataFrames or use accumulators instead.

Logging from your own codepyspark
spark.sparkContext.setLogLevel("WARN")          # quieter Spark INFO noise

# Glue: goes to the driver stream of /aws-glue/jobs/error in real time
logger = glue_context.get_logger()
logger.info(f"run_date={run_date} input_rows={n_in} output_rows={n_out}")

# Plain Python logging on the driver (EMR / local)
import logging
log = logging.getLogger("orders_etl"); log.setLevel(logging.INFO)
log.info("wrote %s rows to %s", n_out, target)
  • Glue 5.x: /aws-glue/jobs/error (Spark logs, traces) and /aws-glue/jobs/output (prints); streams named by job run ID.
  • EMR: container stderr under the S3 log URI; container 000001 is the driver in cluster mode.
  • Executor-side prints never reach the driver log.

Reading a PySpark error from top to root cause

A PySpark failure prints a long, scary stack trace. It has a fixed shape: a Python traceback that only tells you which action failed, then a Py4JJavaError wrapper, then the Java exception with the task and stage that failed, and finally one or more Caused by lines. The real cause is almost always the last Caused by.

A real error from Spark 3.5.6: bad input with FAILFAST

Driver stderr (trimmed)real log
26/11/03 19:01:30 ERROR Executor: Exception in task 0.0 in stage 1.0 (TID 1)
org.apache.spark.SparkException: [MALFORMED_RECORD_IN_PARSING.WITHOUT_SUGGESTION] Malformed records are
detected in record parsing: [o2,c2,null,20758].
Parse Mode: FAILFAST. To process malformed records as null result, try setting the option 'mode' as 'PERMISSIVE'.
	at org.apache.spark.sql.errors.QueryExecutionErrors$.malformedRecordsDetectedInRecordParsingError(...)
	at org.apache.spark.sql.catalyst.csv.UnivocityParser$.$anonfun$parseIterator$2(...)
	...
Caused by: org.apache.spark.sql.catalyst.util.BadRecordException: java.lang.NumberFormatException: Character a
is neither a decimal digit number, decimal point, nor "e" notation exponential mark.
	...
Caused by: java.lang.NumberFormatException: Character a is neither a decimal digit number ...
26/11/03 19:01:30 ERROR TaskSetManager: Task 0 in stage 1.0 failed 1 times; aborting job
Py4JJavaError An error occurred while calling o52.showString.

Spark 3.4+ errors start with an error class in square brackets ([MALFORMED_RECORD_IN_PARSING…]). Search for that class name; it is stable and well documented. The last Caused by names the exact problem: the letter a in a decimal column (our abc amount). The 20758 is the date stored as days since 1970.

A Python UDF crash

Executor stderrlog
org.apache.spark.api.python.PythonException: Traceback (most recent call last):
  File ".../pyspark/worker.py", line 1247, in main
  ...
  File "/tmp/orders_etl.py", line 41, in parse_sku
    return s.split("-")[1]
AttributeError: 'NoneType' object has no attribute 'split'

PythonException contains your function name and line. A NULL reached the UDF. Fix: handle None, or replace the UDF with F.split("sku", "-").getItem(1), which is NULL-safe and faster.

Search CloudWatch fast

CloudWatch Logs Insights on /aws-glue/jobs/errorlogs insights
fields @timestamp, @logStream, @message
| filter @logStream like /jr_8f2c1d/            # the job run ID
| filter @message like /Exception|ERROR|Caused by|Killed|exit code/
| sort @timestamp asc
| limit 200

Sort ascending: the first error is usually closest to the cause. Later errors are often consequences (tasks retried, stage aborted, job failed).

I read a PySpark failure bottom-up for the cause and top-down for the context. The Python traceback tells me which action failed; I skip the Py4J wrapper; the Spark exception tells me the stage, task and whether the same task failed on every attempt; the last Caused by is the root cause. Then I open that stage in the Spark UI and the executor's stderr around the first error.

  • Python line = the action, not the bug.
  • Same task failing 4 times = data. The last Caused by = cause.
  • Search the error class in square brackets; search logs for the first error.

Log line dictionary

These are the log lines you will meet most often in Glue and EMR jobs, with what each one means in plain words and where to look next.

Log lineMeaningNext step
WARN TaskSchedulerImpl: Initial job has not accepted any resourcesNo executors registered yetCluster full, requested more memory/cores than a node has, subnet/IP exhaustion
WARN WindowExec: No Partition Defined for Window operation!A window without partitionBy: all rows go to one taskAdd partitionBy, or accept it only for tiny data
