A method for tuning any Spark job

Tuning is not guessing configs. You measure where the time goes (Spark UI), explain why (plan and task metrics), change one thing, and measure again. Most wins come from doing less work: reading less data, shuffling less data, and spreading work evenly. Adding hardware comes last.

MeasureSpark UI: slowest job → stage → task metricsExplainplan + metrics: why is it slow?Change one thingcode or configMeasure again

The checklist, in order of payoff

#AreaQuestions to askLesson
1Read lessPartition pruning on the right column? Parquet/Iceberg? Only needed columns? Filters before joins? Pushdown visible in the plan?Pruning
2Shuffle lessBroadcast small tables? Aggregate before joining? Unneeded distinct / orderBy / repartition?Joins
3Balance the workSkewed keys? Partitions 100–200 MB? Enough tasks for all cores?Skew
4Stay in the JVMUDFs replaced by built-ins? No collect/toPandas on big data?UDFs
5Don't compute twiceDataFrame used by several actions → cache, then unpersistCaching
6Write sensiblyFile sizes 128 MB–1 GB, dynamic overwrite, no coalesce(1) on heavy workSmall files
7Right resourcesWorker type and count, memory overhead, AQE on, shuffle partitions sized from dataMemory
8Table layoutIceberg compaction, sort order, snapshot expiryIceberg maintenance

I tune in this order: read less, shuffle less, balance the work, keep logic in the JVM, avoid recomputation, write well-sized files, and only then size the cluster. I prove each change with the Spark UI: stage durations, shuffle bytes, max versus median task time, and the final plan.

  • Measure → explain → one change → measure.
  • Less data read and shuffled beats more machines.

Partitioning data on S3

When you write with partitionBy("dt"), Spark creates one folder per value: dt=2026-11-01/, dt=2026-11-02/. A later query with WHERE dt = '2026-11-02' reads only that folder and skips the rest. This is partition pruning, and choosing the right partition column is one of the most important design decisions in a data lake.

Choosing the partition column

  • Pick what most queries filter on. For event and transaction data, that is almost always a date.
  • Keep cardinality low to medium. Each partition should hold at least a few hundred MB. A month of daily partitions is fine; partitioning by customer_id or by timestamp is not.
  • Avoid deep nesting such as year/month/day/hour/country. It multiplies folders and tiny files and slows S3 listing.
  • Partition columns are strings in folder names; keep the format fixed (yyyy-MM-dd).
  • With Iceberg, use hidden partitioning transforms (days(event_ts), bucket(16, id)): users filter on the real column and still get pruning, and you can change partitioning later without rewriting data.
DataGood partitioningBad partitioning
Orders (10 GB/day)dt (day)dt/hour/country → 24 × 50 tiny folders per day
Clickstream (2 TB/day)dt + houruser_id
Customer dimension (5 GB total)None (or bucket by id in Iceberg)country with 1 country holding 80% of rows
Interview: What is the difference between repartition and partitionBy?

repartition changes how rows are split into in-memory partitions (tasks) during the job, and causes a shuffle. partitionBy on the writer decides the folder layout on storage. They are often used together: df.repartition("dt").write.partitionBy("dt") groups each date's rows into one task so each folder gets few, larger files.

  • Disk partitioning = folders; enables pruning.
  • Partition by what you filter on, usually a date; low cardinality.
  • Filter partition columns with plain comparisons or ranges; never OR them with data columns.

The small-file problem

Thousands of tiny files (a few KB or MB each) make everything slower: every file is a separate S3 request, a separate footer to read and often a separate task. A table with 2 million 50 KB files can take longer to list than to read. The cure is to write fewer, larger files (128 MB–1 GB) and to compact tables that already have the problem.

All the tools

Controlling output file sizepyspark
# 1. one task per partition value
df.repartition("dt").write.partitionBy("dt").parquet(out)

# 2. big partition values spread over N files each
df.repartition(F.col("dt"), (F.rand() * 8).cast("int")).write.partitionBy("dt").parquet(out)

# 3. cap rows per file (a task writes several files if needed)
df.write.option("maxRecordsPerFile", 2_000_000).partitionBy("dt").parquet(out)

# 4. let AQE grow the final shuffle partitions before a write
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.adaptive.coalescePartitions.parallelismFirst", "false")
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.adaptive.advisoryPartitionSizeInBytes", "256m")

# 5. Iceberg: hash-distribute writes by partition, compact on a schedule
spark.sql("ALTER TABLE glue_catalog.db.orders SET TBLPROPERTIES ('write.distribution-mode'='hash')")
spark.sql("CALL glue_catalog.system.rewrite_data_files(table => 'db.orders', options => map('target-file-size-bytes','536870912'))")

Reading small files you cannot fix yet

  • DataFrames: raise spark.sql.files.maxPartitionBytes (e.g. 256 MB) and lower spark.sql.files.openCostInBytes (e.g. 1 MB) so more files are packed into each task.
  • Glue DynamicFrames: connection_options={"groupFiles": "inPartition", "groupSize": "134217728"} groups small files into ~128 MB read tasks.
  • At the source: increase Kinesis Data Firehose buffer size/interval; use longer micro-batch triggers in streaming.
Interview: Your S3 table has 2 million 50 KB files. Fix it.

Short term, a compaction job that reads each partition and rewrites it into 256 MB–1 GB files, and swaps safely: write to a new prefix and repoint the Glue partition locations, or, if the table is Iceberg, use rewrite_data_files (or the Glue Data Catalog compaction optimizer), which commits atomically so readers never see a half-written state. Long term, fix the writer (repartition by the partition column before writing, AQE with a larger advisory size, longer streaming triggers or Firehose buffers) and schedule compaction and snapshot expiry.

  • Files written ≈ write tasks × partition values each task holds.
  • repartition(partition_col) before partitionBy(partition_col).
  • Target 128 MB–1 GB files; compact existing tables.

Data skew: find it and fix it

Skew means a few key values have far more rows than the rest, so after a shuffle a few tasks get most of the data. The stage waits for those few tasks while every other core sits idle. Typical culprits: NULL or empty keys, a placeholder like UNKNOWN or -1, one giant customer or tenant, a bot user in clickstream.

A supermarket opens 20 checkouts, but one customer arrives with 400 trolleys and must use a single checkout. Nineteen cashiers finish in minutes; the shop stays open for hours. Salting is splitting that customer's trolleys across many checkouts.

Find it

  • Stage stuck at 199/200; Max task duration and Shuffle Read many times the Median.
  • The same task index fails with OOM on every retry.
Find the hot keyspyspark
(df.groupBy("customer_id").count()
   .orderBy(F.desc("count")).show(10, truncate=False))

# rows per shuffle partition after hashing by the key (what Spark would see)
(df.repartition(200, "customer_id")
   .groupBy(F.spark_partition_id().alias("pid")).count()
   .orderBy(F.desc("count")).show(5))
Output from our skewed test data (80% of rows have key 0)
+---+-------+
|pid|  count|
+---+-------+
|  5|2430000|     <- one partition holds 2.43 M rows
|  7|  45000|
|  1|  45000|
| 15|  42000|
|  4|  39000|
+---+-------+

Fix it: cheapest option first

  1. Remove junk keys. Filter NULL / placeholder keys before the join, process them separately if needed, and union back.
  2. Broadcast the other side if it fits. A broadcast join has no shuffle, so no skew.
  3. Check AQE skew join (default on). Confirm skew=true in the final plan; lower skewedPartitionThresholdInBytes / skewedPartitionFactor if your skewed partitions are smaller than 256 MB.
  4. Salt the key for joins AQE cannot handle.
  5. Two-phase aggregation for skewed groupBy (AQE does not split aggregations).
  6. Isolate hot keys: join the top N hot keys with a broadcast join and everything else normally.
Salted joinpyspark
N = 16                                                    # pick from the skew ratio

facts_s = facts.withColumn("salt", (F.rand(seed=42) * N).cast("int"))
dim_s   = dim.withColumn("salt", F.explode(F.array([F.lit(i) for i in range(N)])))

joined = facts_s.join(dim_s, ["customer_id", "salt"]).drop("salt")

# Better: salt only the known hot keys, so the dimension grows only for them
hot = ["c1", "UNKNOWN"]
facts_s = facts.withColumn("salt", F.when(F.col("customer_id").isin(hot), (F.rand(42) * N).cast("int"))
                                    .otherwise(F.lit(0)))
dim_s = (dim.filter(~F.col("customer_id").isin(hot)).withColumn("salt", F.lit(0))            # normal keys: salt 0
         .unionByName(dim.filter(F.col("customer_id").isin(hot))                               # hot keys: N copies
                         .withColumn("salt", F.explode(F.array([F.lit(i) for i in range(N)])))))
joined = facts_s.join(dim_s, ["customer_id", "salt"]).drop("salt")

The hot-key version is built as a union because Spark does not allow a generator such as explode inside when(). Only the hot keys are copied N times, so the dimension barely grows.

Two-phase (salted) aggregation for skewed groupBypyspark
N = 32
partial = (events.withColumn("salt", (F.rand() * N).cast("int"))
                 .groupBy("page_id", "salt")                         # phase 1: spread hot pages
                 .agg(F.sum("views").alias("v"), F.count("*").alias("c")))
final = (partial.groupBy("page_id")                                  # phase 2: small input
                .agg(F.sum("v").alias("views"), F.sum("c").alias("events")))
# works for sum / count / min / max; for avg carry sum and count; distinct counts need sketches
Interview: Skew in a window function partitioned by user_id. Options?

AQE does not split window partitions. Options: remove bot, test or NULL users; add a second partition key if the logic allows (user and day, when the window never crosses days); pre-aggregate to (user, day) first and run the window on the much smaller table, then join back; handle the few hot users with separate logic; and as a last resort give executors more memory per core so the large partition spills rather than fails.

I confirm skew from the stage's max versus median shuffle read, then find the hot keys with a count by key. Often they are NULLs or placeholders that I can filter. If the other side is small I broadcast; otherwise I check AQE split the partitions. When AQE cannot help, for example skewed aggregations or windows, I salt: random salt on the skewed side, replicate the other side across salt values, or a two-phase aggregation.

  • Skew = few keys, most rows → one slow task.
  • Filter junk keys → broadcast → AQE → salt → two-phase aggregate.
  • AQE only splits skewed joins.

Caching and persistence

cache() asks Spark to keep a DataFrame in executor memory (and disk if needed) after the first time it is computed, so later actions reuse it instead of re-reading and recomputing from the source. It helps only when the same DataFrame is used by two or more actions. Caching something used once just wastes memory.

Cache what is reused, then release itpyspark
from pyspark import StorageLevel

clean = raw.filter("amount > 0").dropDuplicates(["order_id"])   # expensive
clean.persist(StorageLevel.MEMORY_AND_DISK)                    # same as clean.cache() for DataFrames
clean.count()                                                  # fills the cache (optional, makes timing clear)

clean.filter("country = 'IN'").write.parquet(path_in)          # reads from cache
clean.groupBy("dt").count().write.parquet(path_counts)         # reads from cache
clean.unpersist()                                              # free memory for the rest of the job
Storage levelBehaviour
MEMORY_AND_DISKDataFrame default. Partitions that do not fit go to local disk
MEMORY_ONLYPartitions that do not fit are recomputed when needed
DISK_ONLYLocal disk only
*_2 (e.g. MEMORY_AND_DISK_2)Two copies on different executors: faster recovery, double memory
OFF_HEAPTungsten off-heap memory (needs off-heap enabled)
When caching hurts
  • The DataFrame is used once: pure overhead.
  • You cache the raw scan before filters: you store everything, and later filters no longer push down to the files.
  • The cache is larger than memory and keeps getting evicted: re-reading Parquet from S3 can be faster.
  • You forget unpersist() in a loop: storage memory fills and squeezes execution memory, causing spills.

cache vs checkpoint

cache / persist

Keeps the lineage. If an executor dies, the lost partitions are recomputed from the source. Lives only for the application.

checkpoint

Writes to reliable storage (S3) and cuts the lineage. Use when the plan has become huge (loops, iterative algorithms). localCheckpoint is faster but not fault tolerant.

Interview: Does df.cache() cache the data immediately?

No. It is lazy: it only marks the plan. The first action fills the cache, and only for the partitions that action computes. take(5) may cache one partition. That is why people call count() once to fully materialise it. The Storage tab shows the fraction cached, and the plan shows InMemoryTableScan when the cache is used.

  • Cache only DataFrames used by 2+ actions; unpersist after.
  • cache is lazy; the first action fills it.
  • checkpoint cuts lineage; cache keeps it.

Pushdown, pruning and dynamic partition pruning

The cheapest data is data you never read. Spark has four ways to avoid reading: skip columns you don't select, skip folders that don't match a partition filter, skip row groups inside Parquet files whose statistics cannot match, and skip fact partitions using values found in a filtered dimension table at run time.

This lesson is the summary. The Reading less data chapter goes deep on every term (projection pushdown, nested pruning, partition projection, bloom filters, Glue catalog predicates) with real Spark 3.5.6 plans.

TechniqueSkipsShows in plan asBreaks when
Column pruningUnused columnsReadSchema with few columnsselect("*") carried to the end; CSV/JSON
Partition pruningWhole foldersPartitionFilters: [...]OR with a data column; catalog-side pruning lost with functions
Predicate pushdownParquet row groups / pagesPushedFilters: [...]Unsorted data (wide min/max), UDF filters
Dynamic partition pruningFact folders, using a filtered dimensiondynamicpruningexpression(...)Join key not the fact's partition column; dimension filter not selective
Make row-group skipping effectivepyspark
# Sort within each output file by a high-cardinality column you filter on
(df.repartition("dt").sortWithinPartitions("customer_id")
   .write.partitionBy("dt").parquet(out))

# Iceberg: declare a write order (and compact with sort or z-order)
spark.sql("ALTER TABLE glue_catalog.db.orders WRITE ORDERED BY customer_id")

Unsorted data gives every row group a min/max covering almost all customer IDs, so pushdown skips nothing. Sorted data gives each row group a narrow range, so a lookup of one customer reads a tiny fraction of the file.

  • Column pruning, partition pruning, pushdown, dynamic pruning: check all four in the plan.
  • Sorted data makes pushdown work.
  • DPP needs the join key to be the fact table's partition column.

Configs that matter (Spark 3.5 defaults)

Hundreds of Spark settings exist; about twenty matter in daily work. SQL settings can be changed in code with spark.conf.set. Resource settings (executor memory and cores, driver memory) must be fixed before the session starts: in spark-submit, EMR configuration classifications, EMR Serverless submit parameters, or Glue job parameters (Glue sizes memory through the worker type).

SettingDefaultWhy you touch it
spark.sql.shuffle.partitions200Size shuffles to 100–200 MB per partition
spark.sql.adaptive.enabledtrueKeep on
spark.sql.adaptive.coalescePartitions.parallelismFirsttrueSet false so AQE merges up to the advisory size
spark.sql.adaptive.advisoryPartitionSizeInBytes64 MB128–256 MB for fewer, larger output files
spark.sql.autoBroadcastJoinThreshold10 MBRaise for medium dims; -1 to disable when broadcasts OOM
spark.sql.broadcastTimeout300 sSlow-to-build broadcast sides
spark.sql.files.maxPartitionBytes128 MBRead task size
spark.sql.files.openCostInBytes4 MBLower to pack more small files per task
spark.sql.sources.partitionOverwriteModestaticSet dynamic
spark.sql.session.timeZoneJVM zoneSet UTC explicitly
spark.sql.ansi.enabledfalse (true in Spark 4)Strict errors instead of silent NULLs
spark.executor.memory / spark.executor.cores1g / 1 on YARNExecutor sizing (EMR)
spark.executor.memoryOverheadmax(384 MB, 10%)Python/Arrow heavy jobs, YARN kills
spark.driver.memory / spark.driver.maxResultSize1g / 1gBig broadcasts, many tasks, collects
spark.dynamicAllocation.enabledfalse (true on EMR)Scale executors with load
spark.speculationfalseStraggler copies on noisy clusters
spark.sql.execution.arrow.pyspark.enabledfalseFast toPandas() / createDataFrame(pandas_df)
spark.sql.execution.arrow.maxRecordsPerBatch10,000Lower for wide rows in pandas UDFs
spark.task.maxFailures4Task retries before failing the job
spark.serializerJavaKryo helps RDD-heavy code
Passing configs on Glue and EMRexamples
# Glue job parameter (one key, several confs chained)
--conf  spark.sql.shuffle.partitions=600 --conf spark.sql.sources.partitionOverwriteMode=dynamic

# EMR configuration classification
[{"Classification": "spark-defaults",
  "Properties": {"spark.sql.adaptive.coalescePartitions.parallelismFirst": "false",
                 "spark.executor.memoryOverhead": "3g"}}]

Start here: why reading less data matters

Most slow or expensive Spark jobs have one simple problem: they read far more data than they need. Reading from S3 is the slowest part of a job, and on Athena you pay for every byte scanned. So the first question for any slow job is: “Is it reading only what it needs?”

Spark has several tricks to skip data. Each trick has a name (column pruning, predicate pushdown, partition pruning…). This chapter takes them one at a time. Every lesson answers the same questions: what is it, why does it matter, how does it work, how do I check it, when does it fail, and what do I say in the interview.

You need one bill from a big office archive. A smart clerk does not open every box. They go only to the November room, open only the cabinet whose label says it can hold your bill number, and photocopy only the two pages you need. Each skip saves work before any reading happens. Spark does the same with your data.

The five ways to skip data

Think of a table as folders, files inside folders, blocks inside files, and columns inside blocks. Each trick skips one of these.

Real numbers from our own test

We wrote 2 million orders (10 days, 6 columns, two of them long text) as Parquet and measured the bytes Spark actually read, using the Spark UI's “Input” numbers, on Spark 3.5.6.

414 MBAll columns, all days
20 MBOnly 2 columns (column pruning)
2 MB2 columns, 1 day (+ partition pruning)
414 MBSame query, but after cache() on the full table

Same result, 200 times less data, just by reading smart. And the last box shows how easily one line of code can throw that away.

The three lines you will check every time

Every trick leaves a mark in the plan. Run df.explain() and find the FileScan line. Three fields tell you almost everything:

Field in the planQuestion it answersGood sign
PartitionFiltersIs Spark skipping folders?Shows your filter on the date/partition column, not []
PushedFiltersIs the file reader skipping blocks?Shows your filters, e.g. GreaterThan(amount,100.00)
ReadSchemaIs Spark reading only the columns needed?Lists only the columns you use

Names you will hear, and where each is explained

NameWhat it skips (in simple words)Lesson
Column pruning / projection pushdownColumns you don't useColumn pruning
Predicate pushdown / filter pushdownBlocks inside files that can't match your filterPredicate pushdown
Partition pruningFolders that can't match your filterPartition pruning
Dynamic partition pruning (DPP)Folders, using values found in another table during the joinDPP
push_down_predicate, catalogPartitionPredicate, partition indexesGlue's ways to fetch fewer partitions from the catalogGlue partition filters
Partition projectionAthena's way to skip the catalog lookup entirelyPartition projection
Data skipping, sorting, z-order, bloom filtersMaking block and file skipping actually workData skipping
Vectorized reader, aggregate/limit pushdown, JDBC pushdown, runtime bloom filterSmaller extrasOther read tricks

Start with the next lesson. It explains how a Parquet file is built inside, because almost every trick depends on it.

  • Reading less data is the first fix for slow or costly jobs, and on Athena it is directly money.
  • Skip folders → files → blocks → columns → rows, in that order.
  • Check PartitionFilters, PushedFilters, ReadSchema in explain().

Inside a Parquet file (the key to everything)

A Parquet file stores data in blocks of rows, and inside each block it stores every column separately, with a small summary (min, max) for each column.

A CSV file is written row by row: to read one column, you must read every line. Parquet is built differently, and that design is what lets Spark skip data. Once you see these three ideas, column pruning and predicate pushdown become obvious.

1. Row groups

The file is cut into blocks of rows, called row groups (Spark's default target is 128 MB each). Think of them as chapters of a book.

2. Column chunks

Inside a row group, each column is stored on its own, one after another. All amount values sit together, all notes values sit together.

3. Footer summary

At the end of the file is a footer: the schema, plus for every row group and every column the min, max and number of nulls.

Two more details worth knowing

Pages
Each column chunk is split into small pages (about 1 MB). Newer Parquet files also store min/max per page (the “column index”), so a reader can skip pages too.
Dictionary encoding
When a column has few distinct values (like country), Parquet stores a small list of the distinct values plus short codes. A reader can check that list to know a value is not in a row group at all.
Why this matters for you

Column pruning works because columns are stored separately. Predicate pushdown works because of the min/max summaries. And the summaries are only useful if similar values sit together, which is why sorting data when you write it makes reads faster (see Data skipping). ORC files work the same way with “stripes” instead of row groups. CSV and JSON have none of this, which is why they are slow to query.

Interview: Why is Parquet faster than CSV for analytics?

Parquet is columnar: each column is stored separately, so a query reads only the columns it needs. It keeps min/max statistics per row group in the footer, so the reader can skip blocks that cannot match a filter. It is compressed and encoded per column, which compresses much better than rows. CSV must be read and parsed fully for every query.

  • File → row groups → column chunks → pages, with a footer of min/max stats.
  • Columns stored separately → column pruning. Min/max stats → predicate pushdown.

Column pruning (projection pushdown)

Read only the columns your query actually uses, and leave the rest on S3.

What is it?

Spark looks at your whole query, works out which columns are really used, and tells the Parquet reader to fetch only those. “Projection” is the database word for “the list of selected columns”, so projection pushdown means the same thing seen from the reader's side.

Why does it matter?

Tables are often wide (50–200 columns) and you need a handful. In our test, reading 2 of 6 columns cut the read from 414 MB to 20 MB. On Athena, fewer bytes scanned means a smaller bill.

How does it work?

Parquet stores each column separately (previous lesson). Spark's optimiser (Catalyst) removes unused columns from the plan, then the reader skips those column chunks.

How do I check it?

In explain(), look at ReadSchema. It should list only the columns you use.

Try it yourself

Without select: every column is readreal plan, Spark 3.5.6
orders.filter("amount > 100").explain()

FileScan parquet [order_id,customer_id,amount,notes,address,dt] ...
  ReadSchema: struct<order_id:string,customer_id:string,amount:decimal(12,2),notes:string,address:struct<...>>
With select: only two columns are readreal plan, Spark 3.5.6
orders.select("order_id", "amount").filter("amount > 100").explain()

FileScan parquet [order_id#24,amount#26,dt#29] ...
  ReadSchema: struct<order_id:string,amount:decimal(12,2)>

What to notice: the select is written before the filter in the code, but it doesn't matter. Spark optimises the whole plan, so the order in your code does not change pruning. dt appears in the list because it is a partition column taken from the folder name, not read from the file.

Nested columns are pruned too

orders.select("order_id", "address.city")real plan
ReadSchema: struct<order_id:string,address:struct<city:string>>

Only city is read from the address struct, not the other fields. This is called nested schema pruning, on by default (spark.sql.optimizer.nestedSchemaPruning.enabled). Very useful for JSON-like data such as clickstream events.

When it does not work

✗ Cache the whole table first

c = orders.cache()
c.select("order_id","amount").count()

The cache is built from all columns. Our test: 414 MB read instead of 2 MB.

✓ Select and filter, then cache

c = (orders.filter("dt = '2026-11-03'")
       .select("order_id","amount").cache())

Only the needed data is read and kept in memory.

Other things that break itWhy
select("*") kept to the end, writing every columnYour output needs all columns, so all are read
A Python UDF that takes a whole row or structSpark must send every field to Python
CSV or JSON filesStored row by row: the whole line must be read anyway
df.rdd or toPandas() before selectingSpark's optimiser can no longer see what you use

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

A Glue job reads a 120-column raw table but the curated output needs 15 columns: put the select early so every later step (joins, shuffles) also carries less. Athena dashboards that run SELECT * on wide Parquet tables cost far more than ones that list four columns. When designing curated tables, keep them narrow for their consumers, because every reader pays for every column.

In the interview

Column pruning means Spark reads only the columns a query actually uses. It works because Parquet and ORC store columns separately. Projection pushdown is the same idea from the reader's side: the needed column list is pushed down to the source. I check it with ReadSchema in the plan. It breaks with SELECT *, caching the full DataFrame before selecting, UDFs on whole rows, and row formats like CSV.

Q: Is column pruning the same as projection pushdown?

Practically yes. Column pruning is the optimiser removing unused columns from the plan; projection pushdown is handing that column list to the data source so it reads only those. For a JDBC source it means the SQL sent to the database lists only those columns.

Q: Does the order of select and filter in my code matter?

No. Catalyst optimises the whole query, so it prunes columns and pushes filters regardless of the order you wrote them. What does matter is putting a cache, a UDF or an RDD conversion in between, because those block the optimiser.

Q: Why doesn't column pruning help a CSV source?

CSV stores data row by row. To get one column, the reader must still read and parse every full line. Pruning saves real bytes only for columnar formats like Parquet and ORC.

  • Read only used columns. Check ReadSchema.
  • Works for nested struct fields too.
  • Broken by cache-before-select, SELECT *, whole-row UDFs, CSV/JSON.

Predicate pushdown (filter pushdown)

Hand your filter to the file reader, so it can skip whole blocks of rows that cannot match.

What is it?

A predicate is just a filter condition, like amount > 2000. Pushdown means Spark gives that condition to the data source instead of reading everything and filtering afterwards.

Why does it matter?

The reader can skip row groups (and pages) whose min/max show they cannot match. For a database over JDBC, the filter becomes part of the SQL WHERE, so only matching rows travel over the network.

How does it work?

The Parquet reader compares your filter with each row group's footer summary. If max(amount) is 900, the condition amount > 2000 can't be true there, so the block is skipped. Spark still checks the remaining rows one by one.

How do I check it?

In explain(), look at PushedFilters. Your condition should appear there, e.g. GreaterThan(amount,2000).

Try it yourself: what gets pushed

All of these are real Spark 3.5.6 plans on the same table. Read the PushedFilters column.

Your filterPushedFilters in the planResult
amount > 100IsNotNull(amount), GreaterThan(amount,100.00)Pushed
customer_id.isin("1","2","3")In(customer_id, [1,2,3])Pushed
order_id.startswith("o12")StringStartsWith(order_id,o12)Pushed
amount.cast("string") == "300.00"IsNotNull(amount) onlyNot pushed: a cast/function on the column
Python UDF big(amount)[]Not pushed: Spark can't see inside a UDF
(dt == '2026-11-02') | (amount > 2900)[], and PartitionFilters also []Nothing pushed or pruned: whole table read
How to read the three filter lists

DataFilters = what Spark wants to push. PushedFilters = what the reader accepted. If your condition is in DataFilters but missing from PushedFilters, it was not pushed. PartitionFilters is separate: it's for folder skipping (next lesson).

Quick game: will it push down?

Tap your answer for each filter.

col("order_date") >= "2026-11-01"

Plain column compared with a literal: pushed.

date_format("order_date","yyyy-MM") == "2026-11"

A function wraps the column. Rewrite as a range: order_date >= '2026-11-01' AND order_date < '2026-12-01'.

col("status").isNull()

IsNull is pushed; the footer stores null counts.

upper(col("country")) == "IN"

upper() on the column blocks it. Store values in one case when writing, then filter country == "IN".

col("region").isin("south","west")

IN lists are pushed as In(...).

my_python_udf(col("amount")) == True

Spark can't look inside a Python UDF. Use built-in functions where possible.

When it does not work

✗ Function on the column

df.filter(F.year("order_date") == 2026)

Not pushed: every row group is read.

✓ Range on the plain column

df.filter((F.col("order_date") >= "2026-01-01") &
          (F.col("order_date") <  "2027-01-01"))

Pushed: row groups outside 2026 are skipped.

Pushed but still slow?

Pushdown can only skip a block if its min/max range excludes your value. If the data is in random order, every block covers almost every value, so nothing is skipped even though the filter shows as pushed. The fix is sorting when you write: see Data skipping, where sorting cut a lookup from 30 MB to 0.5 MB.

Pushdown to databases (JDBC)

When Spark reads from PostgreSQL, MySQL or Redshift over JDBC, pushdown means the database does the filtering, and only matching rows are sent over the network.

Let the database do the workpyspark
orders = (spark.read.format("jdbc")
    .option("url", url).option("dbtable", "public.orders")
    .option("user", user).option("password", password)
    .load()
    .filter("order_date >= '2026-11-01'")   # becomes WHERE in the SQL sent to the database
    .select("order_id", "amount"))         # becomes SELECT order_id, amount

# For anything complex, send your own SQL: it runs fully inside the database
spark.read.format("jdbc").option("url", url).option("query",
  "SELECT customer_id, SUM(amount) AS total FROM orders "
  "WHERE order_date >= DATE '2026-11-01' GROUP BY customer_id").load()

Filters (pushDownPredicate) are pushed by default. pushDownAggregate (Spark 3.2+) and pushDownLimit (3.3+) push simple aggregates and LIMIT when you turn them on.

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

A Glue job that filters orders with year(order_ts) == 2026 reads the whole table; rewriting it as a timestamp range lets Parquet skip row groups. A nightly job that pulls “changed rows” from RDS should push updated_at > last_run into the database rather than pulling the full table into Spark.

In the interview

Predicate pushdown means Spark passes filter conditions to the data source. For Parquet, the reader compares them with each row group's min/max statistics and skips blocks that can't match; for JDBC, the filter becomes part of the SQL WHERE. I check PushedFilters in the plan. It fails when the filter wraps the column in a function or cast, uses a UDF, or ORs with something that can't be pushed. And even when pushed, it only helps if the data is sorted or clustered on that column.

Q: What's the difference between predicate pushdown and partition pruning?

Partition pruning skips whole folders using filters on partition columns, before any file is opened (PartitionFilters). Predicate pushdown skips blocks inside files using filters on normal columns and the file's min/max statistics (PushedFilters). They work at different levels and together.

Q: My filter is in DataFilters but not in PushedFilters. Why?

The reader couldn't translate it: a function or cast on the column, a Python UDF, a type mismatch that forces a cast, or an OR with a part that can't be pushed. I rewrite it as the plain column compared with a literal of the same type.

Q: If the filter is pushed down, why does Spark still show a Filter step?

Pushdown skips whole blocks only. The blocks that are read still contain non-matching rows, so Spark applies the filter to every row to get the exact result.

  • Filter goes to the reader; it skips row groups using min/max. Check PushedFilters.
  • Write column op literal. No functions, casts or UDFs on the filtered column.
  • Pushdown only skips data when the data is sorted or clustered.

Partition pruning

Data is stored in one folder per value (for example one folder per day); a filter on that column lets Spark open only the matching folders.

What is it?

When you write with partitionBy("dt"), Spark creates folders like dt=2026-11-01/, dt=2026-11-02/. A filter on dt lets Spark skip every other folder. This is static partition pruning: the values are known when the query is planned.

Why does it matter?

It is the biggest saving of all. A daily job on 3 years of data should read 1 folder out of about 1,100. It also avoids listing files in S3 folders you don't need, which is slow with many files.

How does it work?

Spark (or the Glue catalog) knows the list of partition values. Before reading any file it keeps only the folders whose value matches your filter.

How do I check it?

In explain(), PartitionFilters should show your filter. In the SQL tab of the Spark UI, “number of partitions read” should be small.

Try it yourself

orders.filter(F.col("dt") == "2026-11-02").select("order_id")real plan
FileScan parquet [order_id#24,dt#29] ... PartitionFilters: [isnotnull(dt#29), (dt#29 = 2026-11-02)], PushedFilters: [], ReadSchema: struct<order_id:string>

In our measurement, adding this one filter took a 2-column read from 20 MB to 2 MB (1 of 10 days).

Common doubt: a function on the partition column

orders.filter(F.substring("dt", 1, 7) == "2026-11")real plan
PartitionFilters: [isnotnull(dt#29), (substring(cast(dt#29 as string), 1, 7) = 2026-11)]

This still prunes in Spark. Many blogs say it doesn't, but Spark can run any normal function on the folder values. Where it does hurt: for tables in the Glue Data Catalog with many partitions, Spark can only ask the catalog for “just the matching partitions” when the filter is a simple comparison. With a function, it downloads the list of all partitions first and filters them itself, which can take minutes on huge tables. So the advice stays the same: use plain ranges.

When it does not work

✗ OR with a normal column

df.filter((F.col("dt") == "2026-11-02") |
          (F.col("amount") > 2900))

Real plan: PartitionFilters: []. Rows with a big amount can be in any folder, so every folder must be read.

✓ AND, or split into two reads

day  = df.filter(F.col("dt") == "2026-11-02")
late = df.filter((F.col("dt") >= "2026-10-26") &
                 (F.col("amount") > 2900))
day.unionByName(late)  # dedupe if they can overlap

Each part prunes. Often the business rule allows a bounded date range.

Other problemFix
No filter on the partition column at all (filtering on order_ts while the table is partitioned by dt)Add the matching dt filter too, or use Iceberg hidden partitioning (days(order_ts)), which prunes from a filter on order_ts itself
Partitioned by the wrong column (by customer_id: millions of tiny folders)Partition by what most queries filter on, usually a date; sort by the rest
Comparing in the wrong formatSpark infers dt=2026-11-02 as a date column; compare with the same yyyy-MM-dd format

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

Every daily Glue job should read with a filter on the run date. If a job's runtime keeps growing a little every day, the first thing to check is whether PartitionFilters is empty: then it is reading all history every day. Athena works the same: a query without a partition filter scans (and bills) the whole table.

In the interview

Partition pruning skips whole partition folders using a filter on the partition column, before any file is opened. For example, a table partitioned by day, filtered on one day, reads one folder. I check PartitionFilters in the plan and partitions read in the SQL tab. It fails when there is no filter on the partition column or when the partition filter is ORed with a normal column. I prefer plain range filters because they can also be pushed to the Glue catalog.

Q: How do you choose a partition column?

The column most queries filter on, with low to medium cardinality, so each partition holds at least a few hundred MB. Usually a date. Never a high-cardinality ID: that creates millions of tiny files. For filters on high-cardinality columns I sort or z-order within partitions instead.

Q: What's the difference between repartition() and partitionBy()?

repartition splits data into in-memory partitions (tasks) during the job and causes a shuffle. partitionBy on the writer decides the folder layout on storage, which is what partition pruning uses later.

  • One folder per value; filter on it → only matching folders are read.
  • Check PartitionFilters. An OR with a normal column kills it.
  • Prefer plain ranges so the Glue catalog can filter too.

Dynamic partition pruning (DPP)

When you join a big partitioned table with a small filtered table, Spark first finds the matching keys in the small table and then reads only those partitions of the big one.

What is it?

Normal partition pruning needs the value in your query (dt = '2026-11-02'). Often you don't know the dates: you know “holiday = Diwali”, and the dates live in a dim_date table. DPP lets Spark work out those dates while the query runs (“dynamic”) and use them to prune the fact table.

Why does it matter?

Star-schema queries (fact joined to dimensions) are everywhere in reporting. Without DPP, Spark reads every partition of the fact table and throws most rows away in the join.

How does it work?

Spark runs the small side first, collects the join-key values (the dates), broadcasts them, and adds them as an extra partition filter on the fact table's scan.

How do I check it?

In the plan, the fact table's PartitionFilters contains dynamicpruningexpression(...).

Try it yourself

SELECT ... FROM orders o JOIN dim_date d ON o.dt = d.dt WHERE d.holiday = 'Diwali'real plan, Spark 3.5.6
FileScan parquet [amount#11,dt#14] ... PartitionFilters: [isnotnull(dt#14), dynamicpruningexpression(dt#14 IN dynamicpruning#29)]
   +- SubqueryBroadcast dynamicpruning#29, 0, [dt#21]
        +- ReusedExchange [dt#21], BroadcastExchange ...

How to read it: dynamicpruningexpression is the run-time filter on the fact table. SubqueryBroadcast is the step that collects the dates. ReusedExchange means Spark reuses the same broadcast for the join itself, so DPP costs almost nothing extra.

When does DPP happen? (all must be true)

ConditionWhy
The big table is partitioned by the join columnDPP prunes partitions; if dt isn't the partition column there is nothing to prune
The small side has a selective filter (=, IN, a range)Without a filter all keys match, so pruning gains nothing
It is an equi-join (ON a.dt = b.dt)Spark needs exact key values to filter by
spark.sql.optimizer.dynamicPartitionPruning.enabled = trueOn by default in Spark 3.x
What we saw in testing

With WHERE d.holiday = 'Diwali' DPP appeared. With a bare true/false column (WHERE d.is_holiday) it did not, even though the query was similar. Spark only adds DPP when it estimates a benefit. Never assume: always check the plan.

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

A Glue reporting job joins fact_sales (partitioned by sale_date) with dim_date filtered on “last fiscal quarter” or dim_store filtered on a region. With DPP, only the right dates are read. If the fact table is partitioned by something else, join on the partition column where possible, or add an explicit date filter to the fact table yourself. Iceberg tables get the same effect through Spark's runtime filtering.

In the interview

Dynamic partition pruning is partition pruning where the values come from another table at run time. When a partitioned fact table joins a filtered dimension on the partition column, Spark evaluates the dimension first, broadcasts the matching keys, and uses them as a partition filter on the fact scan. It needs the join key to be the fact's partition column and a selective filter on the dimension. In the plan it shows as dynamicpruningexpression.

Q: How is DPP different from a broadcast join?

A broadcast join copies the small table to every executor so the big table isn't shuffled, but the big table is still fully read. DPP reduces what is read from the big table, using the small table's keys. They usually work together: the same broadcast is reused for both.

Q: Your star-schema query reads the whole fact table. What do you check?

Whether the fact table is partitioned by the join key, whether the dimension side has a selective filter, whether the join is an equi-join on that column, and whether DPP is enabled. If DPP can't apply, I add an explicit filter on the fact's partition column derived from the dimension (for example a date range).

  • DPP = partition values discovered at run time from a filtered dimension.
  • Needs: fact partitioned by the join key + selective dimension filter + equi-join.
  • Check for dynamicpruningexpression.

Glue: push_down_predicate, catalog predicates, partition indexes

Before Spark can skip folders, it has to get the list of partitions from the Glue Data Catalog. These options make that list small.

When a table is in the Glue Data Catalog, the catalog keeps one entry per partition (per folder). A table with 5 years of hourly data has about 44,000 partitions. If a job first downloads all 44,000 entries just to use 24 of them, that alone can take minutes. Glue gives you three tools to avoid that.

push_down_predicate

A filter you pass when reading a DynamicFrame. Glue gets the full partition list from the catalog, filters it, and then lists S3 files only for the partitions left. Uses normal Spark SQL syntax.

catalogPartitionPredicate

A filter evaluated inside the Glue catalog, so the catalog returns only matching partitions. Much faster for tables with very many partitions. Simpler syntax, and it relies on partition indexes.

Partition indexes

An index you create on a table's partition keys in the catalog (like a database index). It lets the catalog find matching partitions quickly instead of scanning all of them. Athena can use them too.

Try it yourself

Reading in a Glue jobaws glue
dyf = glue_context.create_dynamic_frame.from_catalog(
    database="sales", table_name="orders",
    # full Spark SQL syntax, applied after the catalog returns partitions
    push_down_predicate="dt = '2026-11-17' AND region IN ('south','west')",
    # simpler syntax, applied inside the catalog (uses partition indexes)
    additional_options={"catalogPartitionPredicate": "dt = '2026-11-17'"})

Use both together: the catalog predicate for the simple, coarse part and push_down_predicate for anything more complex. Note the two use different syntax: the first is Spark SQL, the second uses a simpler parser.

Create a partition index (once per table)aws cli
aws glue create-partition-index --database-name sales --table-name orders \
  --partition-index Keys=dt,IndexName=idx_dt
With DataFrames instead of DynamicFramespyspark
spark.table("sales.orders").filter("dt = '2026-11-17'")   # Glue catalog as metastore

With a plain comparison like this, Spark asks the catalog only for matching partitions (metastore partition pruning). With a function wrapped around dt, it may fetch all partitions first, which is why plain ranges are the safe habit.

When it does not work

ProblemFix
Reading with from_catalog and no predicate at allAlways pass a predicate on the partition columns
Filtering the DynamicFrame after reading (Filter.apply)That filters rows after reading everything; use the predicate in the read
Catalog predicate with syntax it doesn't supportKeep it simple (=, >, <, AND); put the rest in push_down_predicate
New partitions written to S3 but not registered in the catalogRegister them (enableUpdateCatalog in the writer, a crawler, or ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION), or use Iceberg

In the interview

In Glue, push_down_predicate filters partitions after Glue lists them all from the catalog, and before it lists S3 files. For tables with very many partitions that listing is slow, so I add a catalogPartitionPredicate, which the Glue catalog evaluates on its side using partition indexes, and returns only the matching partitions. I use both: a coarse catalog predicate and a detailed push-down predicate.

Q: Is push_down_predicate the same as Spark's predicate pushdown?

No, despite the name. Glue's push_down_predicate is partition pruning: it decides which partitions (folders) to read. Spark's predicate pushdown skips row groups inside Parquet files using filters on normal columns.

Q: A Glue job spends 5 minutes before the first task starts. Why?

Often partition listing: a table with hundreds of thousands of partitions and a read that fetches every partition from the catalog, or many small files to list in S3. Fixes: a catalogPartitionPredicate with a partition index, plain partition filters, fewer and larger partitions, compaction, or Iceberg, which plans from its own metadata.

  • push_down_predicate: Glue gets all partitions, filters them, then lists S3.
  • catalogPartitionPredicate + partition index: the catalog filters, returns few.
  • Both are partition pruning, not Parquet predicate pushdown.

Athena partition projection

Instead of looking up partitions in the Glue catalog, Athena calculates the S3 folder paths from rules you give it. Only Athena does this.

What is it?

You describe the partition values as rules in the table's properties: “dt is a date from 2024-01-01 until today, one per day, and the folder is s3://…/dt=${dt}/”. Athena then works out the folder for your query by itself.

Why does it matter?

Two pains go away. No one needs to keep adding partitions to the catalog (no crawlers, no MSCK REPAIR TABLE). And queries on tables with huge numbers of partitions plan faster, because there is no catalog lookup.

How does it work?

Athena reads the projection rules, combines them with your WHERE filter, builds the list of S3 paths in memory, and reads them.

The big catch

Only Athena uses projection. Spark on Glue or EMR and Redshift Spectrum still read the normal partition list in the catalog.

Try it yourself

Turn on projection for a date + region tableathena sql
ALTER TABLE logs.app_events SET TBLPROPERTIES (
  'projection.enabled'          = 'true',
  'projection.dt.type'          = 'date',
  'projection.dt.format'        = 'yyyy-MM-dd',
  'projection.dt.range'         = '2024-01-01,NOW',
  'projection.dt.interval'      = '1',
  'projection.dt.interval.unit' = 'DAYS',
  'projection.region.type'      = 'enum',
  'projection.region.values'    = 'south,north,east,west',
  'storage.location.template'   = 's3://lake/app_events/dt=${dt}/region=${region}/'
);

The four projection types: date (date/time ranges), integer (number ranges, like hour 0–23), enum (a fixed list), and injected (any value, but every query must give it in the WHERE clause, useful for IDs). The location template must have a placeholder for every partition column.

When to use it, and when not

✓ Good fit

  • Logs and events partitioned by date/hour, queried only in Athena
  • AWS service logs (CloudTrail, ALB, VPC flow logs)
  • Tables with very many partitions and predictable values

✗ Poor fit

  • Tables also read by Glue/EMR Spark or Redshift Spectrum (they need registered partitions)
  • Unpredictable partition values
  • Queries without a filter on the projected column (Athena then generates every possible value)
  • Iceberg tables: they keep partitions in their own metadata, so projection isn't needed

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

A team writes clickstream events by date and hour, and analysts query them in Athena. A crawler ran every hour to add partitions, and fresh data was missing until it finished. Switching the table to partition projection removed the crawler and made new hours queryable immediately. But a Glue job that also read this table started returning no rows for new hours: the fix was to keep registering partitions from the writer job (or move the table to Iceberg).

In the interview

Partition projection is an Athena feature: instead of looking up partitions in the Glue catalog, Athena calculates them from rules in the table properties, like a date range and a path template. It removes partition maintenance and speeds up planning for highly partitioned tables. The important limitation is that only Athena uses it; Glue Spark jobs, EMR and Redshift Spectrum still need partitions registered in the catalog. For tables shared across engines I keep registering partitions or use Iceberg.

Q: Partition pruning vs partition projection?

Partition pruning is skipping partitions that don't match the filter; every engine does it. Partition projection is how Athena finds out which partitions exist: from rules instead of from the catalog. After projecting, Athena still prunes using your filter.

Q: New data lands in S3 but doesn't show up in Athena until the crawler runs. How do you fix it?

Options: add partitions directly from the writer (Glue enableUpdateCatalog or ALTER TABLE ADD PARTITION), use partition projection if only Athena reads the table, or use Iceberg, where a commit makes the data visible immediately.

  • Projection = Athena computes partition paths from rules; no catalog lookups, no crawlers.
  • Types: date, integer, enum, injected. Template needs every partition column.
  • Only Athena uses it. Spark and Spectrum still need registered partitions.

Data skipping: sorting, z-order, bloom filters, Iceberg

Pushdown can only skip a block if its min/max range excludes your value, so write similar values together.

Every row group stores the min and max of each column. Imagine customer IDs written in random order: each block holds customers from 0 to 50,000, so for any customer, every block “might” contain it, and nothing is skipped. Now sort by customer ID before writing: block 1 holds 0–1,400, block 2 holds 1,400–2,800, and so on. A lookup for one customer now matches one block. How you write data decides how fast it can be read.

Real numbers from our test

2 million rows written to one Parquet file twice, once as-is and once sorted by customer_id; query customer_id = '4242'; bytes read measured in the Spark UI (Spark 3.5.6).

30.4 MBUnsorted: 2,000,000 rows read
0.55 MBSorted: 20,000 rows read

Same data, same query, same pushed filter: about 55 times less data read. In a smaller test with 12 row groups, the unsorted file needed all 12, the sorted one needed 1.

Your four tools

1. Sort within files

Best when queries filter on one main column (customer_id).

(df.repartition("dt")
   .sortWithinPartitions("customer_id")
   .write.partitionBy("dt").parquet(path))

2. Z-order

Best when queries filter on two or three columns, sometimes one, sometimes another. It interleaves them so each gets decent skipping (but less than a pure sort on one).

CALL glue_catalog.system.rewrite_data_files(
  table => 'sales.orders', strategy => 'sort',
  sort_order => 'zorder(customer_id, product_id)')

3. Bloom filters

Best for exact-match lookups (=, IN) on columns with many distinct values, like email or device ID, where even sorted ranges overlap. A bloom filter answers “definitely not in this block” or “maybe”.

ALTER TABLE glue_catalog.crm.customers SET TBLPROPERTIES (
 'write.parquet.bloom-filter-enabled.column.email'='true')

4. Iceberg file pruning

Iceberg's manifest files store min/max of every column for every data file. Spark reads these at planning time and drops whole files before opening them. It works even on columns that aren't the partition column, if the data is sorted.

For plain Parquet (not Iceberg), bloom filters are a writer option: .option("parquet.bloom.filter.enabled#email", "true"). In Iceberg, set the write order once with ALTER TABLE ... WRITE ORDERED BY customer_id and let compaction keep it sorted.

Which one should I use?

Queries mostly filter on…Do this
A datePartition by date
Date + one ID (customer_id)Partition by date, sort by the ID
Date + any of 2–3 columnsPartition by date, z-order those columns
Exact lookups on email / UUIDAdd bloom filters on that column
Joins and lookups on one keyIceberg bucket(16, customer_id) partition transform

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

Support analysts look up one customer's events in Athena, and each query scans a whole day of data. The table is partitioned by date, which is right, but files are written in arrival order. Setting the Iceberg write order to customer_id and running a sort compaction on recent partitions makes Iceberg skip most files and Parquet skip most row groups, so each lookup scans a small fraction of the day.

In the interview

Predicate pushdown only skips data if the min/max statistics are narrow, which depends on how data is written. I partition by the main date filter, sort within partitions by the most common other filter, z-order when two or three columns are used, and add bloom filters for exact lookups on high-cardinality columns. With Iceberg I set a write order and run sort compaction, and Iceberg prunes whole files from manifest statistics before opening them.

Q: The filter shows in PushedFilters but the job still reads almost everything. Why?

The data isn't sorted or clustered on that column, so every row group's min/max covers the value and none can be skipped. Fix the write: sort within partitions, z-order, add bloom filters, or with Iceberg set a write order and compact.

Q: Table is partitioned by date but queries filter by customer_id. Repartition by customer_id?

No: that would create millions of tiny folders. Keep the date partition and sort or z-order by customer_id inside it, optionally add a bloom filter, or use an Iceberg bucket transform on customer_id.

  • Pushdown is only as good as the min/max ranges; sorting makes them narrow.
  • Sort for one column, z-order for a few, bloom filters for exact lookups.
  • Iceberg skips whole files using manifest stats.

Other read tricks

A few more terms come up in senior interviews. They matter less day to day, but you should be able to explain each in two sentences. Same format for each: what it is, why, how to check, and the short interview answer.

1. Vectorized reader

What

Spark's Parquet reader decodes values in batches of about 4,096 rows per column, instead of one row at a time.

Why

Batch decoding is several times faster on CPU. It doesn't skip data; it reads the data it does read faster.

Check

Batched: true in the FileScan line. On by default (spark.sql.parquet.enableVectorizedReader).

The vectorized reader decodes Parquet columns in batches rather than row by row, which makes scans much faster. It's on by default; I see it as Batched: true in the plan.

2. Aggregate pushdown

What

For MIN, MAX and COUNT with no filter, the answer can come straight from Parquet footers, without reading any rows.

Why

“What's the latest date in this table?” can be answered from metadata in seconds.

Check

PushedAggregation in the plan. Off by default (spark.sql.parquet.aggregatePushdown=false) and only works with the DataSource V2 Parquet reader. In our test with the default reader, turning it on changed nothing.

Aggregate pushdown answers simple MIN, MAX and COUNT from file or source metadata instead of scanning rows. In Spark it's off by default for Parquet and limited to the V2 reader; JDBC sources and Iceberg metadata tables are where I actually use the idea.

3. Limit pushdown

What

LIMIT 100 is sent to the source, so it returns only 100 rows.

Why

Matters for databases over JDBC: without it, Spark might pull far more rows over the network than needed.

Check

JDBC option pushDownLimit=true (Spark 3.3+). For files, Spark already stops reading once it has enough rows for a simple show() or limit().

4. JDBC pushdown options (summary)

OptionDefaultPushes
pushDownPredicatetrueFilters become SQL WHERE
pushDownAggregatefalse (Spark 3.2+)Simple GROUP BY, SUM, COUNT…
pushDownLimitfalse (Spark 3.3+)LIMIT
query option–Your whole SQL runs in the database

Selected columns are always pushed (projection pushdown). For Redshift, prefer the Redshift connector, which unloads to S3 in parallel, over plain JDBC for large reads.

5. Runtime bloom filter join

What

When a big table joins a much smaller, filtered table in a shuffle join, Spark builds a bloom filter of the small side's join keys and applies it to the big side before the shuffle.

Why

Rows of the big table that can't possibly find a match are dropped early, so far less data is shuffled across the network. Helps when the small side is too big to broadcast.

Check

A might_contain(...) filter on the big side in the plan. On by default in Spark 3.5 (spark.sql.optimizer.runtime.bloomFilter.enabled=true), applied when the big side is large enough.

orders (big) JOIN customers WHERE segment = 7, not broadcastreal plan, Spark 3.5.6
+- Filter (isnotnull(customer_id#22) AND might_contain(Subquery scalar-subquery#268, ..., xxhash64(customer_id#22, 42)))
   :  +- Subquery ...
   :     +- ObjectHashAggregate(functions=[bloom_filter_agg(xxhash64(customer_id#252, 42), ...)])
   :          +- Filter (segment#253L = 7) ...  FileScan parquet [customer_id,segment]
   +- FileScan parquet [order_id#21,customer_id#22,amount#23,dt#26]

How to read it: the subquery builds a bloom filter (bloom_filter_agg) from customers in segment 7. The orders side keeps only rows where might_contain is true, before the Exchange (shuffle). We lowered the size threshold to make it appear on test data; on real large tables it kicks in by itself.

A runtime bloom filter is built from the join keys of the small, filtered side and applied to the big side before the shuffle, so rows that can't match are dropped early. It's on by default in Spark 3.5 and shows as might_contain in the plan. It's similar in spirit to DPP, but works on any join key, not just partition columns.

  • Vectorized reader: faster decoding (Batched: true).
  • Aggregate pushdown: MIN/MAX/COUNT from metadata; off by default for Parquet.
  • JDBC: predicate on by default; aggregate and limit opt-in; or send your own query.
  • Runtime bloom filter: shrink the big side before a shuffle join (might_contain).

Cheat sheet, project stories and interview drill

This lesson pulls the chapter together for revision: the confusing pairs interviewers love, one table with every term, a 5-step audit you can run on any job, two project stories, and a practice drill.

The confusing pairs

PairThe difference in one line
Column pruning vs projection pushdownSame thing: Spark drops unused columns; the reader is told to read only the rest.
Predicate pushdown vs partition pruningPushdown skips blocks inside files (normal columns); pruning skips folders (partition columns).
Partition pruning vs partition projectionPruning skips partitions (every engine); projection is Athena calculating which partitions exist instead of asking the catalog.
Static vs dynamic partition pruningStatic: the value is in your query. Dynamic: the values come from another table during the join.
Glue push_down_predicate vs Spark predicate pushdownGlue's is partition pruning on the catalog; Spark's skips Parquet row groups.
push_down_predicate vs catalogPartitionPredicateFirst: Glue gets all partitions then filters. Second: the catalog filters, using partition indexes.
DPP vs runtime bloom filterDPP prunes partitions by partition column; bloom filter drops rows by any join key before a shuffle.
Broadcast join vs DPPBroadcast avoids shuffling the big side; DPP avoids reading parts of it.

Everything on one page

TermSkipsSee it inBreaks whenFix
Column pruningColumnsReadSchemaSELECT *, cache first, CSVSelect early, cache after
Predicate pushdownRow groups, pagesPushedFiltersFunction/cast/UDF on column, mixed ORPlain col op literal
Data skippingActually skipping themBytes read in SQL tabUnsorted dataSort, z-order, bloom
Partition pruningFoldersPartitionFiltersNo partition filter, OR with data columnFilter on partition column
DPPFolders via a joindynamicpruningexpressionJoin key not partition col, no dim filterJoin on partition column
Glue catalog predicateCatalog partition listingPlanning timeNo index, unsupported syntaxPartition index + simple predicate
Partition projectionCatalog lookups (Athena)Table propertiesOther engines read the tableRegister partitions or Iceberg
Iceberg file pruningFilesFiles read in SQL tabUnsorted dataWrite order + sort compaction
Runtime bloom filterRows before shufflemight_containSmall side not filteredFilter the small side

Audit any job in five steps

1. explain() each read→2. PartitionFilters not empty?→3. PushedFilters = DataFilters?→4. ReadSchema only needed columns?→5. SQL tab: files and bytes read vs table size

Then fix what failed: add plain partition filters, remove mixed ORs and casts, select early, cache after filtering, sort data on filter columns, compact small files.

Project story 1: the slow daily Glue job

Situation
A daily Glue job enriched clickstream events from an Iceberg table (about 2 TB a month, 80 columns, partitioned by event day). It took 55 minutes and the runtime crept up every week.
What I found
The first stage read 60 GB for one day of data. In explain(), PartitionFilters was empty because the filter was to_date(event_ts) = run_date OR is_late = true: an OR mixing the partition source column with a normal column. ReadSchema showed all 80 columns, because a full DataFrame was cached before the select.
What I changed
Split the read: one read for the run date, one bounded read (last 7 days) for late events, then union. Moved the select before the cache.
Result
First stage read 3.1 GB instead of 60 GB; the job dropped to 14 minutes and stopped growing.

Project story 2: the expensive Athena dashboard

Situation
Support analysts looked up single customers in Athena on an events table; costs rose every month.
What I found
Queries filtered on date and customer_id, but files were in arrival order, so Parquet statistics couldn't skip anything. Some dashboards used SELECT * on a wide table.
What I changed
Set the Iceberg write order to customer_id and ran sort compaction on recent partitions; changed dashboard queries to list their columns.
Result
Bytes scanned per lookup fell by about 85%, and so did the Athena bill for that dashboard.
Make the stories yours

Use your own tables and numbers. Interviewers believe stories that name the evidence (“PartitionFilters was empty”, “ReadSchema had 80 columns”) and give a before/after number.

Drill: which trick is this?

Tap the right name for each situation.

Spark reads only 3 of 60 columns from Parquet.

Skipping columns = column pruning (projection pushdown). Seen in ReadSchema.

WHERE dt = '2026-11-17' reads one folder of 1,000.

Skipping folders by a partition column = partition pruning (PartitionFilters).

The reader skips row groups whose max(amount) is below 2000.

Using footer min/max to skip blocks = predicate pushdown (PushedFilters).

Fact partitions are chosen from a filtered dim_date during the join.

Partition values found at run time from another table = dynamic partition pruning.

Athena works out S3 paths from a date range in table properties.

Rules instead of catalog lookups = partition projection (Athena only).

The Glue catalog returns only 24 of 44,000 partitions using an index.

Server-side filtering in the catalog with partition indexes = catalogPartitionPredicate.

Big-side rows are dropped before a shuffle join using might_contain.

A bloom filter of the small side's keys applied before the shuffle = runtime bloom filter join.

Rapid interview questions

Explain column pruning, predicate pushdown and partition pruning in one answer.

All three reduce what Spark reads, at different levels. Partition pruning skips whole folders using filters on partition columns. Predicate pushdown sends filters on normal columns to the Parquet reader, which skips row groups using min/max statistics. Column pruning reads only the columns the query uses, because Parquet stores columns separately. I check them in the plan as PartitionFilters, PushedFilters and ReadSchema.

How do you make Athena queries cheaper on data your Spark jobs write?

Write Parquet or Iceberg, partition by the main date filter, sort or z-order on other common filters, keep files 128 MB–1 GB with compaction, and keep curated tables narrow. Consumers should select only needed columns and always filter on the partition column. Athena bills by bytes scanned, so every skip is money saved.

Why might a filter after cache() be slow?

The cache was built from a full scan with every column and no filter. The later filter runs on cached rows, so no partition pruning, pushdown or column pruning happens. Filter and select before caching. In our test this was 414 MB vs 2 MB.

How do you prove a read optimisation worked?

Before and after: PartitionFilters, PushedFilters and ReadSchema in the plan; files read, partitions read and size of files read in the SQL tab; stage input size and duration; and for Athena, data scanned per query.

Does Spark use Athena partition projection?

No. Only Athena does. Glue/EMR Spark and Redshift Spectrum use the partitions registered in the catalog, so a projected table with unregistered partitions reads as empty from Spark.

  • Know the pairs: pruning vs pushdown, pruning vs projection, static vs dynamic.
  • Audit: PartitionFilters → PushedFilters → ReadSchema → bytes read.
  • Tell stories with plan evidence and before/after numbers.

Playbook: the job that got slow

“Our daily Glue job used to take 40 minutes, now it takes 3 hours.” This is the most common senior interview scenario and the most common real incident. Answer it as an investigation, not a list of guesses: find what changed, find where the time goes, explain why, fix one thing.

SymptomLikely causeFix
199/200 tasks done, last one runs foreverSkewBroadcast, AQE skew join, salting, filter NULL keys
Thousands of 100 ms tasksSmall files / too many partitionsCompact, raise maxPartitionBytes, AQE coalesce
Few long tasks, idle coresToo few partitions, gzip input, coalesce upstream, single-partition JDBCrepartition, splittable formats, parallel JDBC
Long wait before the first taskDriver listing millions of S3 objects, schema inference, huge planPartition pruning, explicit schema, Iceberg metadata, checkpoint long lineage
Gaps between jobs with nothing runningDriver-side Python (loops, pandas, collect), slow S3 commitMove logic into Spark; S3-optimised committer / Iceberg
High GC timeBig objects, UDFs, too many cores per executorSmaller partitions, built-ins, more memory per core
Slower on every loop iterationLineage grows in a loopcheckpoint or write and re-read each iteration
Stages retried repeatedlyExecutors lost (OOM, spot), fetch failuresMemory overhead, on-demand core nodes, smaller tasks

Playbook: out-of-memory errors

Two questions decide the fix. Driver or executor? The log says which process died. Heap or overhead? “Java heap space” means the JVM heap; “Container killed … exceeding memory limits” or exit code 137 means the container's total, usually overhead. Then fix the cause (oversized tasks, collects, UDFs) before adding memory.

Driver OOM

  • collect(), toPandas(), take(huge) → write to S3 or aggregate first.
  • Broadcasting a large table → lower the threshold, remove the hint.
  • Millions of files or partitions → compact, prune, use Iceberg.
  • Huge plans from loops of withColumn/union → one select, checkpoint.
  • Total size of serialized results ... bigger than spark.driver.maxResultSize → fix the collect; raising the limit is last resort.

Executor OOM

  • Same task fails every time → skew or a giant group: fix skew, more partitions.
  • collect_list / window on a hot key → restructure.
  • Container killed / 137 → raise memoryOverhead, smaller Arrow batches, replace UDFs.
  • Many cores share one heap → fewer cores per executor.
  • Glue: G.1X → G.2X, or R-type workers for memory-heavy jobs.

Before adding memory, I make tasks smaller and balanced, because doubling memory on a skewed key only delays the failure. I pick heap versus overhead from the actual error message: Java heap space means more partitions, fixing skew or fewer cores per executor; a YARN container kill means overhead, usually from Python UDFs or Arrow, so I raise memoryOverhead or remove the UDF.

Error catalog: message → cause → fix

ErrorCauseFix
[UNRESOLVED_COLUMN.WITH_SUGGESTION]Typo, dropped column, upstream schema change, wrong nested pathprintSchema(); backticks for names with dots or spaces; schema contract check
[AMBIGUOUS_REFERENCE]Same column name from both join sidesJoin on names, alias DataFrames, drop one copy
Cannot convert column into boolPython and/or/not on Columns& | ~ with brackets

FileAlreadyExistsExceptionDefault save mode, or a retried task wrote the same fileCorrect mode; avoid speculation with custom writers

No space left on deviceShuffle/spill fills local diskShuffle less; bigger Glue worker; bigger EBS
StackOverflowError during planningVery deep plan (loops)checkpoint; fewer chained operations
CommitFailedException (Iceberg)Two writers committed to the same tableCommit retries; serialise writers; isolate by partition
Glue Command failed with exit code 1GenericOpen the run's error log stream; find the first traceback

Silent data-correctness bugs

A job that crashes gets fixed the same day. A job that quietly writes wrong numbers can go unnoticed for months. Senior interviewers probe these bugs because they show whether you protect the business, not just the cluster.

BugHow it happensGuard
Row explosionDuplicate keys on the lookup side of a joinAssert key uniqueness before joins
Dropped rowsInner join where a left join was needed; NULLs removed by != / ~isinRow-count reconciliation in → out
Non-deterministic dedupdropDuplicates keeps a random rowrow_number with a tie-breaker
Wiped partitionsStatic overwrite modeDynamic overwrite or Iceberg
Duplicates on retryappend then a retryIdempotent overwrite / MERGE
Wrong business dateSession time zones differSet time zone; convert explicitly
Money driftDoubles for currencyDECIMAL
Silent NULLsFailed casts (ANSI off), schema driftNULL-rate alerts; ANSI on for critical jobs
Missed late dataDaily job reads only today's folderLookback window + MERGE
A one-pass quality gatepyspark
def assert_quality(df, key_cols, not_null_cols, min_rows):
    stats = df.agg(
        F.count("*").alias("rows"),
        F.countDistinct(*key_cols).alias("distinct_keys"),
        *[F.sum(F.col(c).isNull().cast("int")).alias(f"null_{c}") for c in not_null_cols],
    ).first().asDict()                       # one small row comes back to the driver
    problems = []
    if stats["rows"] < min_rows:
        problems.append(f"only {stats['rows']} rows (expected ≥ {min_rows})")
    if stats["distinct_keys"] != stats["rows"]:
        problems.append(f"{stats['rows'] - stats['distinct_keys']} duplicate keys")
    problems += [f"{k} = {v}" for k, v in stats.items() if k.startswith("null_") and v]
    if problems:
        raise ValueError("Data quality failed: " + "; ".join(problems))
    return stats

On AWS you can also use AWS Glue Data Quality (rules written in DQDL, evaluated inside the job or on catalog tables) or Deequ / PyDeequ on EMR.

Three incidents, solved end to end

These are realistic incidents written the way you would explain them in a project deep-dive: symptom, evidence, root cause, fix, and how you prevented it from happening again. Practise telling each in under two minutes.

Incident 1: month-end OOM in a Glue job

Symptom
A daily Glue 5.1 job (10 × G.1X) failed only on the 1st of each month with ExecutorLostFailure … Container killed … exceeding physical memory limits.
Evidence
Spark UI from event logs: stage 14, the same task index failed four times. Its shuffle read was 9 GB vs a median of 120 MB. SQL tab: the stage was a window partitionBy("account_id") computing running balances. Month-end loads the full month for one large corporate account.
Root cause
A window partition for one account was far larger than one task's memory. AQE does not split window partitions.
Fix
Pre-aggregated transactions to (account, day) before the window, cutting that account's rows from 40 million to 31; computed running balances on the daily table and joined back to transactions for intra-day detail with a second window partitioned by (account, day). Runtime dropped from failing at 52 minutes to 14 minutes on the same workers.
Prevention
Added a CloudWatch alarm on Glue observability skewness metrics and a pre-check that logs the top 5 keys by row count.

Incident 2: a join that suddenly took 3 hours

Symptom
Nightly enrichment went from 25 minutes to 3 hours with no code change.
Evidence
Plan diff: BroadcastHashJoin had become SortMergeJoin. The product dimension had grown from 8 MB to 14 MB of Parquet, crossing the 10 MB threshold. The new SMJ also showed skew on a popular product.
Fix
Selected only the 4 needed columns from the dimension (3 MB), restoring the broadcast; kept an explicit broadcast() hint with a size check that falls back if the dimension exceeds 200 MB.
Prevention
Recorded the plan's join strategies in the job's run log; alert when a strategy changes.

Incident 3: Athena queries slowed down over six months

Symptom
Analysts complained that Athena queries on an Iceberg table went from seconds to minutes.
Evidence
The files metadata table showed 1.8 million data files averaging 3 MB, plus thousands of delete files from hourly MERGE jobs (merge-on-read). The snapshots table had 4,000 snapshots and was never expired.
Fix
Ran rewrite_data_files with sort order on the most filtered column, expire_snapshots keeping 7 days, remove_orphan_files, and rewrite_manifests. Then enabled Glue Data Catalog table optimizers (compaction, snapshot retention, orphan file deletion) so it stays healthy.
Result
Files fell to 21,000 and median query time returned to under 10 seconds; S3 storage dropped 40% after snapshot expiry.
How to tell your own story

Use the same five headings for your real incidents. Numbers make answers credible: data volume, runtime before and after, cost per run, failure rate. Keep client-specific details at the level you are allowed to share.

Glue, EMR, EMR Serverless: which one

All four AWS options run the same Spark. They differ in how much you manage and how much you can configure. Glue is serverless ETL with the Data Catalog and job bookmarks built in. EMR on EC2 gives you a full cluster you control. EMR Serverless runs Spark without a cluster but with more Spark control than Glue. EMR on EKS runs Spark on your Kubernetes cluster.

AWS GlueEMR on EC2EMR ServerlessEMR on EKS
You manageWorker type and countInstances, cluster lifecycle, YARNApplication limitsEKS cluster
Spark version (Nov 2026)3.5.6 (5.1), 4.1 (6.0)3.5.6–3.5.8 (7.11–7.14)Same EMR release labelsSame EMR release labels
StartupSeconds to ~1 minMinutes (or long-running cluster)Seconds with pre-initialised capacitySeconds–minutes
BillingDPU-hours, per secondEC2 + EMR fee; spot possiblevCPU, memory, storage per secondEKS + EMR fee
ExtrasData Catalog, bookmarks, connectors, Data Quality, StudioAny app (Hive, Trino, HBase), custom AMIs, full configNo cluster, full Spark configsShares K8s with other workloads
Best forScheduled ETL; most teamsHeavy sustained workloads, custom stacks, cost control at scaleSpiky jobs needing Spark tuningKubernetes-first organisations

For typical scheduled ETL I choose Glue: serverless, integrated with the Data Catalog and bookmarks, and per-second billing. I move to EMR when I need full control of Spark and hardware, spot instances for cost at large sustained scale, custom libraries or other engines on the same cluster. EMR Serverless sits between them: no cluster to manage but more Spark configurability than Glue.

  • Same Spark everywhere; difference is control vs convenience.
  • Glue for most ETL; EMR for control and cost at scale; Serverless in between.

AWS Glue for Spark in depth

A Glue Spark job is a Python script that Glue runs on a cluster it creates for you. You choose the Glue version (which fixes the Spark version), the worker type (CPU and memory per worker) and how many workers. One worker hosts the driver; the others host one executor each. You pay per DPU-hour, by the second.

Worker types

WorkerDPUvCPUMemoryDisk (free)Use for
G.025X0.2524 GB—Low-volume streaming jobs
G.1X1416 GB94 GB (44)Default for most batch jobs
G.2X2832 GB138 GB (78)Heavier transforms, ML, UDFs
G.4X / G.8X4 / 816 / 3264 / 128 GB256 / 512 GBLarge joins and aggregations, heavy shuffle
G.12X / G.16X12 / 1648 / 64192 / 256 GB768 / 1024 GBVery large workloads (higher start-up latency)
R.1X – R.8X1–8 M-DPU4–3232–256 GB94–512 GBMemory-optimised: jobs with frequent OOM

1 DPU = 4 vCPU + 16 GB. R workers use M-DPUs with double memory (32 GB per 4 vCPU). Glue 6.0 lowered the DPU price by about 30%, but only for jobs on 6.0.

Cost and scaling options

  • Auto Scaling (--enable-auto-scaling true): Glue adds or removes workers up to your maximum based on pending tasks. Use it by default.
  • Flex execution: runs non-urgent jobs on spare capacity at a lower price; start time can be delayed.
  • Timeout: set your own, a little above the normal runtime. A hung job keeps burning DPUs until it reaches its timeout, and the default is many hours.

DynamicFrame or DataFrame?

DynamicFrame (Glue)DataFrame (Spark)
SchemaPer record; a column can be “choice” of types (sometimes string, sometimes int)One fixed schema
Good atMessy sources, bookmarks, Glue connectors, ResolveChoice, Relationalize, catalog partition predicatesEvery Spark API, Catalyst optimisation, windows, SQL
Typical useRead and writeAll the transformation logic in between
The common Glue patternaws glue
