    t_cols = [F.col("customer_id")] + [F.col(f"t.{c}") for c in dim.columns if c != "customer_id"]
    s_cols = [F.col("customer_id")] + [F.col(f"s.{c}") for c in tracked + ["row_hash"]]

    in_dim, in_src = F.col("t.customer_sk").isNotNull(), F.col("s.row_hash").isNotNull()
    same = F.col("t.row_hash") == F.col("s.row_hash")

    unchanged = j.filter(in_dim & (~in_src | same)).select(*t_cols)        # keep as is
    changed   = j.filter(in_dim & in_src & ~same)
    expired   = (changed.select(*t_cols)                                   # close old version
                 .withColumn("valid_to", load_date)
                 .withColumn("is_current", F.lit(False)))
    new_rows  = (changed.select(*s_cols)                                   # new version of changed
                 .unionByName(j.filter(~in_dim).select(*s_cols))           # + brand-new customers
                 .withColumn("valid_from", load_date)
                 .withColumn("valid_to", HIGH)
                 .withColumn("is_current", F.lit(True))
                 .withColumn("customer_sk", F.xxhash64("customer_id", "valid_from"))
                 .select(*dim.columns))
    return hist.unionByName(unchanged).unionByName(expired).unionByName(new_rows)

result = scd2(dim, stg, F.to_date(F.lit("2026-11-03")))
result.drop("row_hash").orderBy("customer_id", "valid_from").show()
Output
+--------------------+-----------+-----+---------+-------+----------+----------+----------+
|         customer_sk|customer_id| name|     city|segment|valid_from|  valid_to|is_current|
+--------------------+-----------+-----+---------+-------+----------+----------+----------+
|-5488638099003664039|          1| Asha|Hyderabad|   GOLD|2026-01-01|9999-12-31|      true|
|-7015704917477310995|          2| Ravi|     Pune| SILVER|2026-01-01|2026-11-03|     false|
|-2170294953861443125|          2| Ravi|   Mumbai| SILVER|2026-11-03|9999-12-31|      true|
| 6601879412621348927|          3|Meena|  Chennai| SILVER|2026-01-01|2026-11-03|     false|
|-1415422703402219002|          3|Meena|  Chennai|   GOLD|2026-11-03|9999-12-31|      true|
| 6531721166160098237|          4| Omar|    Dubai| BRONZE|2026-11-03|9999-12-31|      true|
+--------------------+-----------+-----+---------+-------+----------+----------+----------+

Asha is unchanged. Ravi and Meena each have a closed old row and a new current row. Omar is new. We then ran the same function again with the same input: the row count stayed at 6, because the new current rows now match the source hashes. That is idempotency: a retried job does not create duplicate versions.

Writing the result

Plain Parquet / Hive table

The result is the whole new table. Write it to a new location (e.g. .../dim_customer/v=20261103/), validate, then point the Glue table at it. Overwriting the path you read from in the same job can delete input files mid-read. Fine for dimensions up to a few hundred million rows.

Iceberg table

Don't rewrite the table. Feed only the changed and new rows into a MERGE (next lesson), so Iceberg rewrites only affected files, in one atomic commit. Compute the source DataFrame with this code if the logic is complex, register it as a temp view, MERGE it.

Interview: How do you make SCD2 idempotent?

Change detection is based on comparing the source hash with the current row's hash, and effective dates come from the batch's business date, not the wall clock. If the batch is re-run, the current rows already match the source, so no new versions are created and nothing is closed twice. Surrogate keys are deterministic (hash of natural key and valid_from), so even a partial re-run produces the same keys. With Iceberg the MERGE is atomic, so a failure mid-way leaves the table unchanged.

  • Split current/history → full join current with source → classify → union.
  • Changed = two rows: closed old + new version.
  • Re-running the same batch must add zero rows.

Type 2 in Spark SQL and Iceberg MERGE

There are three SQL ways to do Type 2. On a plain Parquet table you rebuild the table with CTEs and INSERT OVERWRITE into a new location. On Iceberg you use one MERGE INTO with a well-known trick: each changed customer appears twice in the source, once to close the old row and once, with a NULL key, to insert the new row. A third option uses two statements, which is simpler but not atomic.

Option A: plain Spark SQL rebuild (no table format needed)

CTE rebuild — real output belowspark sql
WITH stg AS (
  SELECT *, sha2(concat_ws('||', coalesce(name,'~'), coalesce(city,'~'), coalesce(segment,'~')), 256) AS row_hash
  FROM stg_customer),
cur AS (SELECT * FROM dim_customer WHERE is_current),
changed AS (                                   -- customers whose tracked values differ
  SELECT s.* FROM stg s JOIN cur c ON s.customer_id = c.customer_id WHERE s.row_hash <> c.row_hash),
brand_new AS (                                 -- customers not in the dimension yet
  SELECT s.* FROM stg s LEFT ANTI JOIN cur c ON s.customer_id = c.customer_id)
-- 1. all existing rows; close the current row of changed customers
SELECT d.customer_sk, d.customer_id, d.name, d.city, d.segment, d.valid_from,
       CASE WHEN ch.customer_id IS NOT NULL AND d.is_current THEN DATE'2026-11-03' ELSE d.valid_to END AS valid_to,
       CASE WHEN ch.customer_id IS NOT NULL AND d.is_current THEN false ELSE d.is_current END AS is_current,
       d.row_hash
FROM dim_customer d LEFT JOIN changed ch ON d.customer_id = ch.customer_id
UNION ALL
-- 2. new versions for changed customers and first versions for new customers
SELECT xxhash64(customer_id, DATE'2026-11-03'), customer_id, name, city, segment,
       DATE'2026-11-03', DATE'9999-12-31', true, row_hash
FROM (SELECT * FROM changed UNION ALL SELECT * FROM brand_new)
Output: identical to the DataFrame version
|1|Asha |Hyderabad|GOLD  |2026-01-01|9999-12-31|true |
|2|Ravi |Pune     |SILVER|2026-01-01|2026-11-03|false|
|2|Ravi |Mumbai   |SILVER|2026-11-03|9999-12-31|true |
|3|Meena|Chennai  |SILVER|2026-01-01|2026-11-03|false|
|3|Meena|Chennai  |GOLD  |2026-11-03|9999-12-31|true |
|4|Omar |Dubai    |BRONZE|2026-11-03|9999-12-31|true |

Wrap it in INSERT OVERWRITE TABLE dw.dim_customer_next ... (a different table or location), validate, and swap. The date literal would come from a job parameter, e.g. spark.sql(query, args={"load_date": run_date}) with :load_date in the SQL.

Option B: one Iceberg MERGE (the production pattern)

SCD2 MERGE on Icebergspark sql
MERGE INTO glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer t
USING (
  -- part 1: match every staging row against its current version
  SELECT s.customer_id AS merge_key, s.* FROM stg_hashed s
  UNION ALL
  -- part 2: changed customers again with a NULL key, so they INSERT as new versions
  SELECT NULL AS merge_key, s.*
  FROM stg_hashed s
  JOIN glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer d
    ON d.customer_id = s.customer_id AND d.is_current AND d.row_hash <> s.row_hash
) u
ON t.customer_id = u.merge_key AND t.is_current
WHEN MATCHED AND t.row_hash <> u.row_hash THEN
  UPDATE SET t.valid_to = DATE'2026-11-03', t.is_current = false
WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN
  INSERT (customer_sk, customer_id, name, city, segment, valid_from, valid_to, is_current, row_hash)
  VALUES (xxhash64(u.customer_id, DATE'2026-11-03'), u.customer_id, u.name, u.city, u.segment,
          DATE'2026-11-03', DATE'9999-12-31', true, u.row_hash)

stg_hashed is the staging view with row_hash added and deduplicated to one row per customer (MERGE fails if two source rows match one target row). Expected result: the same six rows as the DataFrame version. Re-running it changes nothing: part 2 is empty because hashes now match, and part 1 matches with equal hashes. Run it on Glue 5.x with --datalake-formats iceberg; Athena can run the same MERGE on Iceberg tables.

Option C: two statements (simple, not atomic)

Close, then insertspark sql
-- 1. close changed current rows
MERGE INTO glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer t USING stg_hashed s
ON t.customer_id = s.customer_id AND t.is_current
WHEN MATCHED AND t.row_hash <> s.row_hash THEN UPDATE SET valid_to = DATE'2026-11-03', is_current = false;

-- 2. insert a current row for every staging customer that has no current row now
INSERT INTO glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer
SELECT xxhash64(s.customer_id, DATE'2026-11-03'), s.customer_id, s.name, s.city, s.segment,
       DATE'2026-11-03', DATE'9999-12-31', true, s.row_hash
FROM stg_hashed s
LEFT ANTI JOIN (SELECT customer_id FROM glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer WHERE is_current) c
  ON s.customer_id = c.customer_id;

Easier to read, and safe to re-run (step 2 only inserts customers without a current row). But between the two commits, readers see changed customers with no current row. Use it when nobody queries the table during the load, or use a branch (write-audit-publish) to hide the intermediate state.

Spark 4: MERGE from the DataFrame API

Spark 4.0 adds DataFrame.mergeInto(table, condition) with .whenMatched().update(...), .whenNotMatched().insertAll() and .merge(), so the MERGE can be written without SQL text. On Glue 5.x / EMR 7.x (Spark 3.5) MERGE is SQL only. Spark 4 only

  • Plain tables: CTE rebuild into a new location.
  • Iceberg: one MERGE with the NULL merge-key trick; atomic and idempotent.
  • Two-statement version is simpler but exposes an in-between state.

Type 2 edge cases: late, multiple, deleted

The basic merge assumes one clean snapshot per day. Real CDC feeds are messier: a customer can change twice in one batch, a change can arrive late and belong between two versions you already have, the same change can be sent twice, an “update” can carry no real change, and customers can be deleted. A senior answer handles all five. The robust approach is to rebuild the full version chain for just the affected customers.

Rebuild the chain for affected customers only (tested)pyspark
w = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy("valid_from")
keys = cdc.select("customer_id").distinct()                          # customers touched by this batch

untouched = hist.join(keys, "customer_id", "left_anti")             # everyone else: leave alone
affected  = (hist.join(keys, "customer_id", "left_semi")            # their existing versions
               .unionByName(cdc.withColumnRenamed("changed_at", "valid_from"))   # + new changes
               .dropDuplicates(["customer_id", "valid_from"]))      # same change sent twice

# drop "changes" that don't change anything compared with the previous version
affected = (affected.withColumn("h", row_hash(affected))
                    .withColumn("prev_h", F.lag("h").over(w))
                    .filter(F.col("prev_h").isNull() | (F.col("h") != F.col("prev_h")))
                    .drop("h", "prev_h"))

def close(df):                                                      # end = next version's start
    nxt = F.lead("valid_from").over(w)
    return (df.withColumn("valid_to", F.coalesce(nxt, F.to_timestamp(F.lit("9999-12-31 00:00:00"))))
              .withColumn("is_current", nxt.isNull()))

result = close(affected).unionByName(close(untouched))
Output: batch had a late change, two changes for customer 2, a duplicate for 3 and a no-op for 1
+-----------+---------+-------+-------------------+-------------------+----------+
|customer_id|city     |segment|valid_from         |valid_to           |is_current|
+-----------+---------+-------+-------------------+-------------------+----------+
|1          |Hyderabad|GOLD   |2026-01-01 00:00:00|9999-12-31 00:00:00|true      |   <- no-op update ignored
|2          |Pune     |SILVER |2026-01-01 00:00:00|2026-06-15 12:00:00|false     |   <- end moved back
|2          |Hyderabad|SILVER |2026-06-15 12:00:00|2026-11-02 09:00:00|false     |   <- late change inserted
|2          |Mumbai   |SILVER |2026-11-02 09:00:00|2026-11-03 18:00:00|false     |
|2          |Bengaluru|SILVER |2026-11-03 18:00:00|9999-12-31 00:00:00|true      |   <- second change in batch
|3          |Chennai  |SILVER |2026-01-01 00:00:00|2026-11-03 10:00:00|false     |
|3          |Chennai  |GOLD   |2026-11-03 10:00:00|9999-12-31 00:00:00|true      |   <- duplicate removed
+-----------+---------+-------+-------------------+-------------------+----------+

Applying the rebuilt chain to Iceberg

MERGE on (natural key, valid_from)spark sql
MERGE INTO glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer t
USING rebuilt_chain s                                   -- affected customers only
ON t.customer_id = s.customer_id AND t.valid_from = s.valid_from
WHEN MATCHED AND (t.valid_to <> s.valid_to OR t.is_current <> s.is_current) THEN
  UPDATE SET t.valid_to = s.valid_to, t.is_current = s.is_current      -- existing versions re-dated
WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN INSERT *                                          -- late and new versions

Each version is identified by (customer_id, valid_from), so the MERGE is idempotent. Facts already loaded with the old Pune surrogate key for orders between 15 June and 2 November now point to the wrong version: a late-arriving dimension change may require re-keying those facts. Mention that trade-off; many teams accept it or restate only recent partitions.

Deletes

Soft delete: close the row and flag itpyspark
deleted = spark.createDataFrame([(3,)], "customer_id int").withColumn("del", F.lit(True))
sd = (dim.join(deleted, "customer_id", "left")
         .withColumn("valid_to",   F.when(F.col("del") & F.col("is_current"), F.to_date(F.lit("2026-11-04"))).otherwise(F.col("valid_to")))
         .withColumn("is_deleted", F.coalesce(F.col("del") & F.col("is_current"), F.lit(False)))
         .withColumn("is_current", F.when(F.col("del"), F.lit(False)).otherwise(F.col("is_current")))
         .drop("del"))
Output for customer 3
|3|Chennai|SILVER|2026-01-01|2026-11-03|false|false|
|3|Chennai|GOLD  |2026-11-03|2026-11-04|false|true |   <- closed and flagged; no current row left

Never hard-delete Type 2 rows: facts still reference them. How you learn about deletes depends on the source: CDC sends an explicit D operation; a full snapshot means “missing today = deleted” (left anti join of current rows against the snapshot); a partial feed cannot detect deletes at all. GDPR erasure is the exception: then you anonymise the attributes in every version.

Point-in-time joins: using the dimension

Attach the version that was true on the order date (tested)pyspark
pit = (facts.alias("f").join(dim.alias("d"),
          (F.col("f.customer_id") == F.col("d.customer_id")) &
          (F.col("f.order_date") >= F.col("d.valid_from")) &
          (F.col("f.order_date") <  F.col("d.valid_to")))         # half-open interval
       .select("f.order_id", "f.order_date", "f.amount", "d.customer_sk", "d.city", "d.segment"))
Output
+--------+----------+------+--------------------+-------+-------+
|order_id|order_date|amount|         customer_sk|   city|segment|
+--------+----------+------+--------------------+-------+-------+
|      o1|2026-10-15| 500.0|-7015704917477310995|   Pune| SILVER|   <- October order → Pune version
|      o2|2026-11-05| 700.0|-2170294953861443125| Mumbai| SILVER|
|      o3|2026-11-04|  90.0|-1415422703402219002|Chennai|   GOLD|
+--------+----------+------+--------------------+-------+-------+

Do this lookup when loading facts and store customer_sk in the fact table; reports then join on the surrogate key only. The range condition is a non-equi join, but the equality on customer_id lets Spark hash-partition it; broadcast the dimension if it is small.

Quality checks every SCD2 table should pass

Three checks (all returned 0 on our result)pyspark
w = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy("valid_from")
one_current = dim.groupBy("customer_id").agg(F.sum(F.col("is_current").cast("int")).alias("c")).filter("c != 1")
gaps_or_overlaps = (dim.withColumn("nxt", F.lead("valid_from").over(w))
                       .filter(F.col("nxt").isNotNull() & (F.col("nxt") != F.col("valid_to"))))
dup_sk = dim.groupBy("customer_sk").count().filter("count > 1")
for name, df in [("current_rows", one_current), ("gaps_overlaps", gaps_or_overlaps), ("dup_sk", dup_sk)]:
    assert df.limit(1).count() == 0, f"SCD2 check failed: {name}"

Soft-deleted customers legitimately have zero current rows; exclude is_deleted keys from the first check.

  • Messy CDC → rebuild the version chain for affected keys with lag/lead.
  • Deduplicate, drop no-op changes, sort by business change time.
  • Soft-delete, never hard-delete. Point-in-time join with half-open ranges.
  • Check: one current row, no gaps/overlaps, unique surrogate keys.

Type 3, Type 4 and Type 6

These three are variations you will be asked to explain and occasionally build. Type 3 adds a “previous value” column. Type 4 keeps the main table small (current values only) and moves history to a separate table. Type 6 is Type 2 plus a column with today's value on every version, so analysts can report both “as it was” and “as it is now” without a self-join.

Type 3: previous value column

Keep current and previous city (tested)pyspark
moved = F.col("t.city").isNotNull() & F.col("s.city").isNotNull() & ~F.col("t.city").eqNullSafe(F.col("s.city"))
t3 = (dim.alias("t").join(stg.alias("s"), "customer_id", "full")
         .select("customer_id",
                 F.coalesce("s.name", "t.name").alias("name"),
                 F.coalesce("s.city", "t.city").alias("current_city"),
                 F.when(moved, F.col("t.city")).alias("previous_city"),
                 F.when(moved, F.lit("2026-11-03").cast("date")).alias("city_changed_on")))
Output
+-----------+-----+------------+-------------+---------------+
|customer_id| name|current_city|previous_city|city_changed_on|
+-----------+-----+------------+-------------+---------------+
|          1| Asha|   Hyderabad|         NULL|           NULL|
|          2| Ravi|      Mumbai|         Pune|     2026-11-03|
|          3|Meena|     Chennai|         NULL|           NULL|
|          4| Omar|       Dubai|         NULL|           NULL|
+-----------+-----+------------+-------------+---------------+

In a real Type 3 table, customers who did not move keep their existing previous_city from earlier runs; take t.previous_city in the otherwise branch. Iceberg version: MERGE ... WHEN MATCHED AND t.current_city <> s.city THEN UPDATE SET previous_city = t.current_city, current_city = s.city, city_changed_on = current_date(). In a MERGE, the right-hand side reads the old target values, so the swap works in one statement.

Type 4: current table + history table

Implementation: run the Type 1 MERGE on the current table and the Type 2 logic (or a simple append of changed rows with timestamps) on the history table in the same job. With Iceberg, use a branch or run the history write first, so a failure never leaves current updated without history. Facts can store the current table's key; as-of analysis joins to history by date.

Type 6: Type 2 plus current values everywhere

Add current_city to every version (tested)pyspark
latest = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy(F.desc("valid_from"))
t6 = scd2_result.withColumn("current_city", F.first("city").over(latest))
Output
+-----------+-------+------------+----------+----------+----------+
|customer_id|   city|current_city|valid_from|  valid_to|is_current|
+-----------+-------+------------+----------+----------+----------+
|          2|   Pune|      Mumbai|2026-01-01|2026-11-03|     false|   <- historical row knows today's city
|          2| Mumbai|      Mumbai|2026-11-03|9999-12-31|      true|
+-----------+-------+------------+----------+----------+----------+

Now one query can report “October revenue by the city at the time” (city) and “by the customer's city today” (current_city). The cost: every change updates current_city on all of that customer's historical rows. In Iceberg: after the Type 2 MERGE, run MERGE ... ON t.customer_id = s.customer_id WHEN MATCHED AND t.current_city <> s.city THEN UPDATE SET current_city = s.city with the current rows as source.

  • Type 3: one previous value; MERGE swaps columns in one statement.
  • Type 4: small current table + separate history table.
  • Type 6: Type 2 + current_* columns updated on every version.

PySpark or Spark SQL: which and when

Both produce the same plan, so the choice is about the target table and about maintainability, not speed. On Iceberg, the actual write should be a SQL MERGE, because it is atomic and rewrites only affected files. How you prepare the MERGE's source can be DataFrame code (testable, reusable, config-driven) or SQL (readable for analysts). Most production teams combine them.

SituationRecommendedWhy
Target is Iceberg (or Delta/Hudi), standard SCD1/SCD2Spark SQL MERGEAtomic, row-level, only touches changed files, short and readable, same SQL works in Athena
Target is plain Parquet / HiveDataFrame API (or CTE rebuild) + write to a new locationNo MERGE/UPDATE available; you must rebuild the table anyway
Many dimensions, same logic, columns from configDataFrame function producing the source, then MERGEOne tested function, parameterised by table name, keys and tracked columns
Messy CDC: late, multiple, duplicate changesDataFrame (lag/lead chain rebuild) → MERGE on (key, valid_from)Window logic is clearer step by step and easy to unit-test
Team mostly SQL, simple logic, small dimsSpark SQL end to endReadability for the people who maintain it
Spark 4 (Glue 6.0)Either; DataFrame.mergeInto availableMERGE without SQL strings Spark 4 only

The hybrid pattern most teams use

Reusable SCD2 job skeletonpyspark
def apply_scd2(spark, table, stg, key, tracked, load_date):
    """Prepare in DataFrame code, write with one Iceberg MERGE."""
    w = W.partitionBy(key).orderBy(F.desc("updated_at"))
    s = (stg.withColumn("rn", F.row_number().over(w)).filter("rn = 1").drop("rn")      # 1 row per key
            .withColumn("row_hash", F.sha2(F.concat_ws("||",
                 *[F.coalesce(F.col(c).cast("string"), F.lit("~")) for c in tracked]), 256)))
    s.createOrReplaceTempView("stg_hashed")
    cols = ", ".join(tracked)
    spark.sql(f"""
      MERGE INTO {table} t
      USING (SELECT {key} AS merge_key, * FROM stg_hashed
             UNION ALL
             SELECT NULL AS merge_key, s.* FROM stg_hashed s
             JOIN {table} d ON d.{key} = s.{key} AND d.is_current AND d.row_hash <> s.row_hash) u
      ON t.{key} = u.merge_key AND t.is_current
      WHEN MATCHED AND t.row_hash <> u.row_hash THEN
        UPDATE SET t.valid_to = DATE'{load_date}', t.is_current = false
      WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN INSERT ({key}_sk, {key}, {cols}, valid_from, valid_to, is_current, row_hash)
        VALUES (xxhash64(u.{key}, DATE'{load_date}'), u.{key}, {", ".join("u." + c for c in tracked)},
                DATE'{load_date}', DATE'9999-12-31', true, u.row_hash)""")

apply_scd2(spark, "glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer", stg_df, "customer_id", ["name", "city", "segment"], run_date)

Table and column names come from your own config, never from user input. load_date is the batch's business date passed as a Glue job argument, which keeps re-runs idempotent.

Performance at scale

  • Only touch what changed. Filter staging to changed keys (hash compare) before the MERGE; most days only a small fraction of customers change.
  • Help the MERGE find files. Iceberg must find target rows for each key. Partition or bucket the dimension by the natural key (bucket(32, customer_id)) or sort it by the key, so the MERGE reads few files. Don't partition by is_current: every change would move rows between partitions.
  • Merge-on-read for frequent (hourly) SCD loads, with scheduled compaction.
  • Broadcast small staging data in the DataFrame joins.
  • Dimensions are usually small (millions of rows). A full DataFrame rebuild of a 5-million-row dimension is seconds on Glue; don't over-engineer.
  • Very large, fast-changing attributes (a customer's “last login”) don't belong in an SCD2 dimension. Keep them as facts or in a mini-dimension.

SCD interview questions

Explain SCD Type 1, 2 and 3 with an example.

Using a customer who moves from Pune to Mumbai: Type 1 overwrites the city, so all past orders now show Mumbai. Type 2 closes the Pune row with an end date and inserts a new Mumbai row with its own surrogate key, so orders keep pointing to the version that was true when they happened. Type 3 keeps current_city = Mumbai and previous_city = Pune in one row, so it only remembers one step back.

How do you implement SCD2 in PySpark on AWS?

On Glue 5.x with Iceberg in the Glue Data Catalog: read the day's source, deduplicate to one row per key, compute a hash of the tracked columns, and run one MERGE that closes the current row of changed keys and inserts new versions, using the NULL merge-key trick so it's a single atomic commit. Effective dates come from the batch's business date or the source change timestamp, surrogate keys are a hash of key and valid_from, so re-runs are idempotent. On plain Parquet I compute the full new dimension with a full outer join and union, write it to a new location and swap the table location.

How do you handle late-arriving or out-of-order changes in SCD2?

For the affected keys only, I union existing versions with the new changes, deduplicate, drop changes that don't differ from the previous version, sort by business change time and recompute valid_to with lead(). The late version lands in the middle and its neighbours are re-dated. I apply that with a MERGE keyed on natural key plus valid_from. Facts already linked to the re-dated version may need re-keying, so I discuss with the business whether to restate them.

How do you detect changes efficiently?

A hash (sha2 or xxhash64) of the tracked columns, computed NULL-safely with coalesce before concat_ws, stored on each row. Comparing one hash is cheap and avoids long OR conditions. I normalise values first (trim, case) to avoid false changes, and exclude Type 1 columns from the hash.

Why not just use Iceberg time travel instead of SCD2?

Time travel tracks load time, not business time, and snapshots are expired after days for cost and GDPR. SCD2 tracks when a value was true in the business, keeps it for years, supports late corrections, and gives facts a stable surrogate key to join on.

How do facts get the right surrogate key?

At fact load time, join the fact's natural key and event date to the dimension with a point-in-time condition (event_date ≥ valid_from and < valid_to) and store the surrogate key in the fact. If no version exists yet (an early-arriving fact), insert an “inferred member” row with the natural key and unknown attributes, and let the next dimension load fill it in with Type 1.

What can go wrong in an SCD2 pipeline?

Two current rows for one key (a non-atomic load or a bad re-run), gaps or overlaps in validity ranges, new versions created by noise (trailing spaces, case), duplicates in the source making MERGE fail, NULLs making change detection wrong, and clock-based effective dates making re-runs non-idempotent. I guard with dedup, normalisation, NULL-safe hashing, business dates as parameters, atomic MERGE, and three DQ checks after each load.

For SCD2 on our Iceberg tables I prepare the source in PySpark, deduplicating and hashing the tracked columns, then apply it with a single Spark SQL MERGE using the NULL merge-key pattern, so closing old versions and inserting new ones is one atomic commit and safe to re-run. On plain Parquet there's no MERGE, so I rebuild the dimension with DataFrame joins and swap locations. I choose SQL for the write because it's atomic and readable, and DataFrame code for preparation because it's testable and reusable across dimensions.

  • Iceberg write → SQL MERGE. Plain Parquet → DataFrame rebuild + swap.
  • Prepare in DataFrame code (dedup, hash, chains), apply with MERGE.
  • Bucket/sort the dimension by natural key; don't partition by is_current.

SCD practice problems

Write each solution in a blank editor before opening it. All outputs come from Spark 3.5.6. These problems appear in live PySpark rounds for senior data engineering roles because they combine joins, windows and NULL handling in one task.

1. Type 1 upsert where NULL in the source must overwrite

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
in_src = F.col("s.customer_id").isNotNull()
t1 = (stg.alias("s").join(dim.alias("t"), "customer_id", "full")
         .select("customer_id", *[F.when(in_src, F.col(f"s.{c}")).otherwise(F.col(f"t.{c}")).alias(c)
                                  for c in ["name", "city", "segment"]]))

coalesce would keep the old value when the source sends NULL; testing whether the row exists in the source does not.

2. Build SCD2 ranges from daily full snapshots

You have one row per customer per day (a daily snapshot table). Produce one row per continuous period with the same city. Note customer 1 moves back to Hyderabad.

Show solution
Gaps and islands on attribute changespyspark
w = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy("snap_date")
g = (snaps.withColumn("chg", F.when(F.lag("city").over(w).eqNullSafe(F.col("city")), 0).otherwise(1))
          .withColumn("version", F.sum("chg").over(w.rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0))))
r = g.groupBy("customer_id", "version", "city").agg(F.min("snap_date").alias("valid_from"))
wv = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy("valid_from")
r = (r.withColumn("valid_to", F.coalesce(F.lead("valid_from").over(wv), F.to_date(F.lit("9999-12-31"))))
      .withColumn("is_current", F.lead("valid_from").over(wv).isNull()))
Output
+-----------+-------+---------+----------+----------+----------+
|customer_id|version|     city|valid_from|  valid_to|is_current|
+-----------+-------+---------+----------+----------+----------+
|          1|      1|Hyderabad|2026-11-01|2026-11-03|     false|
|          1|      2|    Delhi|2026-11-03|2026-11-05|     false|
|          1|      3|Hyderabad|2026-11-05|9999-12-31|      true|
|          2|      1|     Pune|2026-11-01|9999-12-31|      true|
+-----------+-------+---------+----------+----------+----------+

Grouping by city alone would merge the two Hyderabad periods into one wrong range (1 to 5 November). The running version counter keeps them apart. This is the most common SCD trick question.

3. Customers who changed city more than twice in a year

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
(dim.filter(F.year("valid_from") == 2026)
    .groupBy("customer_id").agg(F.countDistinct("customer_sk").alias("versions"))
    .filter("versions > 3"))          # first version of the year + more than two changes

Clarify with the interviewer whether a version created on 1 January counts as a change, and whether to count changes of city only (then compare city with lag(city) instead of counting versions).

4. Find broken SCD2 rows: overlaps and multiple current rows

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy("valid_from")
overlaps = (dim.withColumn("next_from", F.lead("valid_from").over(w))
               .filter(F.col("next_from") < F.col("valid_to")))
multi_current = (dim.groupBy("customer_id")
                    .agg(F.sum(F.col("is_current").cast("int")).alias("current_rows"))
                    .filter("current_rows != 1"))
Output on a deliberately broken table
overlaps:                                         multi_current:
|7|A|2026-01-01|2026-05-01|next 2026-04-01|        |8|2|
|8|C|2026-01-01|9999-12-31|next 2026-03-01|

5. Attach the correct dimension version to each fact

See the point-in-time join with output in Type 2 edge cases. Interviewers often follow up: “What if the fact's date is before the customer's first version?” Answer: the inner join drops it; use a left join and map unmatched rows to an unknown member surrogate key (for example -1) so revenue is not lost, then investigate.

6. Full snapshot input: detect deletes as part of SCD2

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
deleted_keys = (dim.filter("is_current").select("customer_id")
                   .join(stg.select("customer_id"), "customer_id", "left_anti"))   # current but absent today
# then apply the soft-delete logic to these keys, and the normal SCD2 merge for the rest

Only valid when the source is a complete snapshot. Add a safety check: if more than a few percent of customers “disappear” in one day, the extract was probably truncated; stop instead of deleting half the dimension.

7. Talk-through questions to prepare

  • Your SCD2 MERGE failed with “matched multiple source rows”. Why, and what is the fix? (duplicates per key in staging; deduplicate with row_number on the latest change)
  • The table suddenly has two current rows for some customers. What happened? (a non-atomic two-step load interrupted and re-run without the anti-join guard, or concurrent jobs)
  • A source sends “updates” every day for every customer, though nothing changed. Effect? (without hash comparison, a new version per customer per day; dimension explodes)
  • How would you backfill SCD2 history from three years of daily snapshots? (problem 2, run once over all snapshots, then switch to incremental)

Streaming as an ever-growing table

Structured Streaming treats a stream (Kafka, Kinesis, new files landing in S3) as a table that keeps getting new rows. You write the same DataFrame code as for batch. Spark runs it again and again on just the new rows in small micro-batches, and remembers where it got to in a checkpoint, so after a restart it continues exactly where it stopped.

Kafka (Amazon MSK) → Icebergpyspark
raw = (spark.readStream.format("kafka")
       .option("kafka.bootstrap.servers", brokers)
       .option("subscribe", "orders")
       .option("startingOffsets", "latest")          # only for the very first run; later runs use the checkpoint
       .option("maxOffsetsPerTrigger", 500_000)      # cap batch size
       .load())

orders = (raw.select(F.from_json(F.col("value").cast("string"), order_schema).alias("o"))
             .select("o.*"))

query = (orders.writeStream
         .format("iceberg").outputMode("append")
         .option("checkpointLocation", "s3://chk/orders_stream/")   # one per query, never shared
         .trigger(processingTime="1 minute")
         .toTable("glue_catalog.sales.orders_raw"))
query.awaitTermination()

Triggers

TriggerBehaviourUse
default (none)Next batch as soon as the previous finishesLowest latency in micro-batch
processingTime="1 minute"One batch per intervalMost streaming jobs
availableNow=TrueProcess everything available now in several batches, then stopIncremental batch on a schedule (cheap “streaming” on Glue)
once=TrueOne batch then stopDeprecated; use availableNow
Real-time mode Spark 4.1Millisecond-latency for stateless queriesGlue 6.0 / Spark 4.1 only

Output modes

  • append: only new final rows. For aggregations this needs a watermark, because Spark must know when a window is complete.
  • update: rows that changed in this batch.
  • complete: the whole result table every batch; only for small aggregates.
  • Stream = unbounded table processed in micro-batches.
  • Checkpoint = offsets + state + commits; one per query.
  • availableNow = scheduled incremental batch.

Event time, windows and watermarks

Event time is when something happened (a click at 10:03); processing time is when Spark sees it (maybe 10:09, because a phone was offline). To count clicks per 5-minute window correctly, Spark must keep windows open for late events. A watermark tells Spark how late events may be: “wait up to 10 minutes, then close the window, write it out and forget its state”.

Windowed counts with a watermarkpyspark
views = (events
         .withWatermark("event_ts", "10 minutes")                       # how late data may be
         .groupBy(F.window("event_ts", "5 minutes"), "page_id")          # tumbling 5-minute windows
         .agg(F.count("*").alias("views")))
# sliding windows: F.window("event_ts", "10 minutes", "5 minutes")
# session windows: F.session_window("event_ts", "30 minutes")

# streaming dedup within the watermark (Spark 3.5)
deduped = events.withWatermark("event_ts", "1 hour").dropDuplicatesWithinWatermark(["event_id"])
Without a watermark

An aggregation keeps state for every window forever; memory grows until the job fails. Always set a watermark on stateful streaming queries (aggregations, dedup, stream-stream joins).

  • Event time ≠ processing time. Windows use event time.
  • Watermark = max event time − allowed lateness; older state is dropped.
  • Later-than-watermark events are discarded.

Exactly-once, foreachBatch and AWS streaming

“Exactly once” means every input event affects the output once, even after crashes and retries. Spark gets there with three ingredients: a replayable source (Kafka/Kinesis offsets), the checkpoint recording which offsets each batch covered, and an idempotent or transactional sink (Iceberg commits, or a MERGE keyed on the data). If the sink is a plain append to files or a database insert, a replayed batch can duplicate rows.

foreachBatch: run batch code (like MERGE) per micro-batchpyspark
def upsert(batch_df, batch_id):
    (batch_df.withColumn("rn", F.row_number().over(
         W.partitionBy("user_id").orderBy(F.desc("updated_at"))))
      .filter("rn = 1").drop("rn")
      .createOrReplaceTempView("updates"))
    batch_df.sparkSession.sql("""
        MERGE INTO glue_catalog.crm.users t USING updates s ON t.user_id = s.user_id
        WHEN MATCHED AND s.updated_at > t.updated_at THEN UPDATE SET *
        WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN INSERT *""")

(cdc_stream.writeStream
   .foreachBatch(upsert)
   .option("checkpointLocation", "s3://chk/users_upsert/")
   .trigger(availableNow=True)          # run as a scheduled incremental job
   .start().awaitTermination())

A replayed batch runs the same MERGE again; because it is keyed and guarded by updated_at, the result is the same. That is exactly-once in effect. batch_id can also be stored in the target to skip a batch already applied.

Streaming on AWS

  • Glue streaming jobs read Kinesis Data Streams and Amazon MSK with built-in connectors; G.025X workers for small streams; glue_context.forEachBatch helper with checkpointing.
  • EMR / EMR Serverless run Structured Streaming with the Kafka source; Kinesis needs a connector.
  • S3 file source: spark.readStream.schema(s).json("s3://landing/") picks up new files; combine with availableNow for cheap incremental ETL.
  • Many teams do not need true streaming: an availableNow job every 15 minutes gives near-real-time data at batch cost.
Spark 4 streaming additions

transformWithState (a new, more flexible arbitrary-state API with timers and TTL) and a State Data Source to read checkpoint state for debugging arrived in Spark 4.0; real-time mode in 4.1. On Spark 3.5 use applyInPandasWithState for custom state in Python. Spark 4 only

  • Exactly-once = replayable source + checkpoint + idempotent sink.
  • foreachBatch + MERGE is the standard upsert pattern.
  • availableNow on a schedule is often enough.

Kinesis and MSK sources in depth

Kinesis splits a stream into shards and MSK (Kafka) into partitions; Spark reads them in parallel and remembers its position in the checkpoint.

Kinesis Data Streams

AWS's own stream. Data is split into shards; each shard accepts about 1 MB/s or 1,000 records/s of writes and gives 2 MB/s of reads. Position = sequence number. Read in Spark via Glue's Kinesis connector (Glue) or a Kinesis connector package (EMR).

Amazon MSK (Kafka)

Managed Apache Kafka. Data is split into partitions of a topic; position = offset. Spark's built-in Kafka source reads it (Glue has a Kafka connection type too). Auth is usually IAM or SASL/SCRAM over TLS.

Why does it matter?

Parallelism, throughput limits, where a new job starts reading, and how much it reads per batch decide whether a streaming job keeps up or falls further behind every minute.

Try it yourself

MSK with IAM auth (EMR or Glue, Spark's Kafka source)pyspark
events = (spark.readStream.format("kafka")
    .option("kafka.bootstrap.servers", "b-1.msk.xxxx.kafka.ap-south-1.amazonaws.com:9098")
    .option("subscribe", "orders")
    .option("startingOffsets", "latest")             # first start only; afterwards the checkpoint wins
    .option("maxOffsetsPerTrigger", 500000)          # cap per micro-batch
    .option("kafka.security.protocol", "SASL_SSL")
    .option("kafka.sasl.mechanism", "AWS_MSK_IAM")
    .option("kafka.sasl.jaas.config", "software.amazon.msk.auth.iam.IAMLoginModule required;")
    .option("kafka.sasl.client.callback.handler.class",
            "software.amazon.msk.auth.iam.IAMClientCallbackHandler")
    .load())
# value is binary: decode it
orders = events.select(F.from_json(F.col("value").cast("string"), order_schema).alias("o"), "partition", "offset").select("o.*", "partition", "offset")

IAM auth needs the aws-msk-iam-auth library on the classpath. startingOffsets applies only on the very first run; once a checkpoint exists, Spark always resumes from it. Keep partition and offset for debugging and deduplication.

Kinesis in a Glue streaming jobaws glue
df = glueContext.create_data_frame.from_options(
    connection_type="kinesis",
    connection_options={
        "streamARN": "arn:aws:kinesis:ap-south-1:123:stream/orders",
        "startingPosition": "TRIM_HORIZON",            # or LATEST, or a timestamp
        "classification": "json", "inferSchema": "false",
        "maxFetchRecordsPerShard": "100000"})          # cap per shard per batch

glueContext.forEachBatch(frame=df, batch_function=process_batch,
    options={"windowSize": "60 seconds", "checkpointLocation": "s3://lake/_chk/orders/"})

Keeping up: the numbers that matter

SignalKinesisMSK / KafkaMeaning
LagGetRecords.IteratorAgeMilliseconds (CloudWatch)Consumer lag (offsets behind)Growing = the job can't keep up
ParallelismOne reader task per shardOne task per partitionMore shards/partitions = more parallel reads
Read limit2 MB/s per shard, shared by consumers (enhanced fan-out gives each consumer its own)Broker throughputSeveral consumers on one Kinesis stream compete
Batch size controlmaxFetchRecordsPerShardmaxOffsetsPerTriggerSteady batches, no giant catch-up batch
Retention24 h default, extendableTopic retention settingJob down longer than retention = data lost

When it does not work

ProblemFix
Lag grows every hourBatch duration > trigger interval. Speed up the sink (fewer small files, MERGE on fewer partitions), add workers, add shards/partitions
First batch after a long stop is huge and fails with OOMSet maxOffsetsPerTrigger / maxFetchRecordsPerShard
Changed the query and restarted: errors or reprocessingSome changes are incompatible with the old checkpoint; start a new checkpoint from a chosen position and make the sink idempotent
One hot shard/partitionProducers use a skewed key; choose a better partition key
Thousands of tiny filesLonger trigger, write to Iceberg and compact

In the interview

Kinesis splits data into shards and Kafka into partitions; Spark reads each in parallel and stores the positions, sequence numbers or offsets, in the checkpoint, which always wins over the starting position after the first run. I cap each micro-batch with maxOffsetsPerTrigger or records per shard, watch iterator age or consumer lag, and scale with more shards or partitions plus a faster sink. On MSK I use IAM auth; on Glue I use the built-in Kinesis and Kafka connectors with forEachBatch.

Q: Kinesis or MSK: how do you choose?

Kinesis is serverless-ish and simplest on AWS, scaled by shards, integrated with Firehose and Lambda. MSK fits when teams already use Kafka, need Kafka's ecosystem (Connect, Debezium CDC, schema registry tooling), longer retention or higher throughput per partition, at the cost of running brokers (or MSK Serverless).

  • Shards (Kinesis) / partitions (Kafka) = parallel read units; checkpoint holds positions.
  • Cap batches; watch iterator age or consumer lag.
  • Starting position only applies to the first run.

Avro, Protobuf and Glue Schema Registry

Producers send compact binary messages; a schema registry keeps the schema in one place so producers and consumers agree on what the bytes mean.

Avro and Protobuf

Binary formats for messages. Much smaller and faster than JSON, and every message follows a declared schema (field names and types). Avro is common with Kafka; Protobuf is common in microservices.

Schema registry

A service that stores schemas and their versions. AWS Glue Schema Registry is free, supports Avro, JSON Schema and Protobuf, and enforces compatibility rules (for example BACKWARD) so a producer can't publish a breaking change.

Why does it matter?

Without a contract, a producer renaming a field silently breaks every consumer. With a registry, the breaking change is rejected at the producer, and consumers can read old and new versions.

Try it yourself

Plain Avro messages (schema known to the job)pyspark
from pyspark.sql.avro.functions import from_avro      # needs the spark-avro package on EMR/local
order_schema_json = open("order_v2.avsc").read()

decoded = events.select(from_avro(F.col("value"), order_schema_json, {"mode": "PERMISSIVE"}).alias("o")).select("o.*")
Messages written with the Glue Schema Registry serializerpyspark
# The GSR serializer prefixes each message with a small header:
# 1 byte version + 1 byte compression + 16 bytes schema version UUID, then the Avro bytes.
payload = F.expr("substring(value, 19)")              # skip the 18-byte header (substring is 1-based)
decoded = events.select(from_avro(payload, order_schema_json).alias("o")).select("o.*")

Plain from_avro doesn't understand the registry's header, so feeding it raw messages fails or produces garbage. Either strip the header and use a known schema (as here), fetch the schema version from the registry with boto3 (get_schema_version) at start-up, or in Glue streaming point the source at a Data Catalog table whose schema comes from the registry. If the producer turned on compression in the serializer, the bytes after the header are zlib-compressed and must be decompressed first.

Protobuf (Spark 3.4+, spark-protobuf package)pyspark
from pyspark.sql.protobuf.functions import from_protobuf
decoded = events.select(from_protobuf("value", "Order", descFilePath="s3://code/order.desc").alias("o")).select("o.*")

Compatibility modes in plain words

ModeMeansSafe changes
BACKWARD (common default)New consumers can read old dataAdd optional fields (with defaults), remove fields
FORWARDOld consumers can read new dataAdd fields, remove optional fields
FULLBothAdd or remove optional fields only
NONENo checksAnything (risky)

In the interview

For streaming I prefer Avro or Protobuf with AWS Glue Schema Registry: messages are compact and the registry enforces compatibility, usually BACKWARD, so producers can't publish breaking changes. In Spark I decode with from_avro or from_protobuf; messages from the Glue serializer carry a header with the schema version ID, so I either strip it and use the registered schema, or let Glue streaming resolve the schema through a catalog table. Schema changes then flow into Iceberg with schema evolution.

  • Avro/Protobuf = compact, typed messages. Registry = versioned contract.
  • Glue Schema Registry: Avro, JSON, Protobuf; compatibility modes; free.
  • GSR messages have a header: strip it or let Glue resolve the schema.

Developing Glue jobs: interactive sessions, notebooks, Docker

Don't develop by editing a script, running the whole job and waiting minutes for an error: use an interactive session or a local Glue container to try code cell by cell.

Interactive sessions

A Glue Spark backend you connect to from a notebook (Glue Studio notebooks, SageMaker Unified Studio, or Jupyter/VS Code on your laptop). You run code cell by cell against real data with the job's IAM role. You pay per DPU-hour while the session is up.

Local Docker image

AWS publishes the Glue runtime as a container (public.ecr.aws/glue/aws-glue-libs:5 for Glue 5.0). It has Spark, the Glue libraries and Python, so you can run and unit test Glue scripts on your laptop or in CI for free.

Why does it matter?

Fast feedback means fewer broken deployments. Interviewers ask “how do you develop and test Glue jobs?” to see if you have a real workflow, not just console clicking.

Try it: an interactive session

First notebook cell: configure, then codeglue notebook
%glue_version 5.0
%worker_type G.1X
%number_of_workers 2
%idle_timeout 30                         # minutes; the default is 2880 (48 hours!)
%additional_python_modules great_expectations==1.3.0
%%configure
{ "--enable-glue-datacatalog": "true", "--datalake-formats": "iceberg" }
df = spark.table("sales.orders").filter("dt = '2026-11-17'").limit(1000)
df.printSchema(); df.show(5)

Set %idle_timeout low: a forgotten session otherwise keeps billing for two days. Use few workers and sample data while exploring. %stop_session ends it. Magics must come before the first code cell that starts the session.

Try it: the local Docker image

Run a Glue script and its tests locallyshell
docker pull public.ecr.aws/glue/aws-glue-libs:5

# run a job script
docker run -it --rm -v ~/.aws:/home/hadoop/.aws -v $PWD:/home/hadoop/workspace \
  -e AWS_PROFILE=dev public.ecr.aws/glue/aws-glue-libs:5 \
  spark-submit /home/hadoop/workspace/src/orders_job.py --JOB_NAME local_test

# run unit tests in the same image (also what CI runs)
docker run --rm -v $PWD:/home/hadoop/workspace public.ecr.aws/glue/aws-glue-libs:5 \
  -c "cd /home/hadoop/workspace && python3 -m pytest tests/"

The Glue 5.0 image is a single image for batch and streaming and runs on x86 and ARM (Apple Silicon). Some features only exist in the real service: job bookmarks and the Glue Parquet writer don't work locally, so test those in a dev account.

OptionReal data?CostBest for
Interactive session (notebook)Yes, with IAM roleDPU-hours while runningExploring data, debugging a prod issue
Local DockerSamples or S3 with your credentialsFreeUnit tests, CI, offline work
Plain local PySpark (pip)SamplesFreePure Spark logic without Glue APIs
Dev Glue job runYesFull job costIntegration test before prod
Structure the code so it can be tested

Keep transformations as functions that take and return DataFrames (def clean_orders(df) -> DataFrame) in a Python package; the Glue script only reads, calls them and writes. Then tests can call the functions on tiny DataFrames in Docker without S3. See Testing PySpark code.

In the interview

I explore with Glue interactive sessions from a notebook with a short idle timeout and few workers, then move the logic into tested functions in a package. Unit tests run locally and in CI inside the official Glue Docker image, so the Spark and library versions match the service. Features like job bookmarks only work in the service, so a dev job run is the last integration step before deploying the same script to prod with infrastructure as code.

  • Interactive sessions: magics first, low %idle_timeout, small workers.
  • Docker image aws-glue-libs:5: local runs, pytest, CI. No bookmarks locally.
  • Transformations as testable functions; the script is thin.

Which config wins? Spark configuration precedence

When the same setting is set in several places, the one closest to your code usually wins, and some settings can't be changed once the job has started.

What is it?

A Spark setting can come from cluster defaults (spark-defaults.conf, EMR classifications), from spark-submit --conf or Glue job parameters, from the session builder in code, or from spark.conf.set at runtime.

Why does it matter?

“I set executor memory to 8g but the job still uses 4g” is a classic. Knowing the order and which settings are fixed at start-up saves hours.

Two kinds of setting

Runtime SQL settings (spark.sql.shuffle.partitions, AQE…) can change any time. Start-up settings (executor memory and cores, driver memory, spark.sql.warehouse.dir, extensions, catalogs) are fixed when the SparkContext starts.

Try it: real behaviour on Spark 3.5.6

Changing settings after the session startedreal output
spark.conf.set("spark.executor.memory", "8g")
AnalysisException: [CANNOT_MODIFY_CONFIG] Cannot modify the value of the Spark config: "spark.executor.memory".
spark.conf.set("spark.sql.warehouse.dir", "/tmp/x")
AnalysisException: Cannot modify the value of a static config: spark.sql.warehouse.dir.
s2 = SparkSession.builder.config("spark.sql.shuffle.partitions", "99").getOrCreate()
s3 = SparkSession.builder.config("spark.executor.memory", "16g").getOrCreate()
print(s2 is spark, spark.conf.get("spark.sql.shuffle.partitions"))
print(spark.sparkContext.getConf().get("spark.executor.memory", "(not set)"))
True 99
(not set)

What to notice: getOrCreate() returns the existing session. It applies runtime SQL settings from the builder (shuffle partitions became 99) but silently ignores start-up settings like executor memory. In Glue, the session already exists when your script runs, so memory must be set by the worker type or job parameters, not in code.

Where to set what on AWS

PlatformStart-up settings (memory, cores, catalogs)Runtime SQL settings
GlueWorker type and count; job parameters (--conf, --datalake-formats); run arguments override the job's default argumentsspark.conf.set in the script
EMR on EC2spark-defaults classification for the cluster; --conf per step overrides itspark.conf.set or --conf
EMR ServerlessApplication default runtime config; job sparkSubmitParameters override itspark.conf.set
Local / testsSparkSession.builder.config(...) before the first getOrCreate()spark.conf.set

To check what actually took effect, look at the Environment tab of the Spark UI (or History Server): it lists the final value of every property.

In the interview

Spark settings layer up: cluster defaults, then spark-submit or job parameters, then values set in code, and runtime SQL settings can be changed with spark.conf.set at any point. Start-up settings like executor memory, driver memory, warehouse dir and catalog extensions are fixed when the context starts, so setting them in a running Glue script does nothing or fails. I set those through worker types and job parameters, and confirm the final values in the Environment tab.

  • Code > submit/job params > cluster defaults (same key).
  • Memory, cores, catalogs = start-up only. SQL settings = any time.
  • getOrCreate() on an existing session ignores start-up settings. Check the Environment tab.

Testing PySpark code

Spark code is easy to test if you write transformations as pure functions: a function takes DataFrames and returns a DataFrame, with no reading or writing inside. Then a test builds a tiny input DataFrame by hand, calls the function, and compares the result with the expected rows. Spark 3.5 ships assertDataFrameEqual for exactly this.

Project layouttree
orders_pipeline/
├── jobs/orders_daily.py          # thin entry point: parse args, read, call transforms, write
├── orders_pipeline/
│   ├── transforms.py             # pure functions: DataFrame in, DataFrame out
│   └── quality.py
├── tests/
│   ├── conftest.py               # one SparkSession for the whole test run
│   └── test_transforms.py
└── pyproject.toml
transforms.pypyspark
from pyspark.sql import DataFrame, functions as F, Window as W

def latest_per_order(cdc: DataFrame) -> DataFrame:
    w = W.partitionBy("order_id").orderBy(F.desc("updated_at"), F.desc("seq"))
    return cdc.withColumn("rn", F.row_number().over(w)).filter("rn = 1").drop("rn")
conftest.py and a testpytest
# tests/conftest.py
import pytest
from pyspark.sql import SparkSession

@pytest.fixture(scope="session")
def spark():
    s = (SparkSession.builder.master("local[2]")
         .config("spark.sql.shuffle.partitions", "2")      # tiny data: keep tests fast
         .config("spark.sql.session.timeZone", "UTC")
         .getOrCreate())
    yield s
    s.stop()

# tests/test_transforms.py
from pyspark.testing import assertDataFrameEqual            # Spark 3.5+
from orders_pipeline.transforms import latest_per_order

def test_keeps_latest_and_breaks_ties(spark):
    cdc = spark.createDataFrame(
        [("o1", "PAID", "2026-11-01 10:00:00", 1), ("o1", "SHIPPED", "2026-11-01 12:00:00", 2),
         ("o2", "CREATED", "2026-11-01 11:00:00", 3), ("o2", "CANCELLED", "2026-11-01 11:00:00", 4)],
        "order_id string, status string, updated_at string, seq long")
    expected = spark.createDataFrame(
        [("o1", "SHIPPED", "2026-11-01 12:00:00", 2), ("o2", "CANCELLED", "2026-11-01 11:00:00", 4)],
        cdc.schema)
    assertDataFrameEqual(latest_per_order(cdc), expected)   # order-insensitive by default
  • Test edge cases: NULL keys, duplicates, empty input, late data, time-zone boundaries.
  • Integration test the real Glue job on a small sample in a dev account before promoting.
  • Older Spark versions: the chispa library offers similar assertions.
  • Pure transform functions → easy unit tests.
  • Session-scoped SparkSession fixture, few shuffle partitions.
  • pyspark.testing.assertDataFrameEqual in 3.5.

Deploying Spark jobs: CI/CD and config

Senior interviews ask “how does your code get to production?”. A good answer: code in Git, tests in CI, packaged artifacts uploaded to S3, Glue jobs and workflows defined as infrastructure-as-code, promoted through dev → test → prod with environment-specific parameters, never edited by hand in the console.

Pull requestlint + pytestMerge to mainbuild wheel/zipUpload to S3versioned pathDeploy IaCCDK / CloudFormation / TerraformPromotedev → test → prod
Glue job in AWS CDK (Python)cdk
glue.CfnJob(self, "OrdersDaily",
    name=f"orders-daily-{env}",
    role=job_role.role_arn,
    glue_version="5.1",
    worker_type="G.1X", number_of_workers=10,
    timeout=120,                                            # minutes
    command=glue.CfnJob.JobCommandProperty(
        name="glueetl", python_version="3",
        script_location=f"s3://{artifacts}/{version}/jobs/orders_daily.py"),
    default_arguments={
        "--extra-py-files": f"s3://{artifacts}/{version}/orders_pipeline.zip",
        "--enable-auto-scaling": "true",
        "--enable-spark-ui": "true",
        "--spark-event-logs-path": f"s3://{logs}/sparkui/orders-daily/",
        "--datalake-formats": "iceberg",
        "--env": env,
    })
  • Keep environment differences (bucket names, worker counts) in parameters or config files, not in code.
  • Version every artifact path so rollback is a redeploy of the previous version.
  • Record the Glue version and Spark configs in code review, because they change behaviour.
  • Git → CI tests → versioned S3 artifacts → IaC → promotion.
  • No console edits in production.

PII: find it, mask it, hash it, encrypt it, delete it

Personal data (names, emails, phone numbers, IDs) must be found, protected in a way that still lets analysts work, and deletable on request.

What is PII?

Personally identifiable information: anything that identifies a person, alone or combined (email, phone, PAN/SSN, address, date of birth). Health data (PHI) and card data have extra rules (HIPAA, PCI).

Why does it matter?

Laws like GDPR and India's DPDP Act require minimum access, protection and the right to be deleted. A leaked analytics table with raw emails is a serious incident; for healthcare and pharma clients it's a contract breach.

The five moves

Find it (classify), mask it for display, hash it to keep joins working, encrypt it when someone must read the original, and delete it everywhere when asked.

Pick the right technique

TechniqueReversible?Still joinable?Use for
Drop the column–NoData nobody downstream needs (the best option)
Mask (a*******@gmail.com)NoNoShowing in reports and support tools
Salted hash (pseudonymise)NoYes, same input = same hashCounting and joining customers across tables
Encrypt (AES / KMS)Yes, with the keyOnly if deterministicFields a few authorised people must read
Tokenise (vault lookup)Yes, via the vaultYesCard numbers, IDs in regulated systems
Restrict access (Lake Formation column/row/cell filters)––Same table, different views per role

Try it: mask, hash and encrypt in Spark

Built-in functions onlyreal output, Spark 3.5.6
salt = get_secret("pii/salt")                  # from Secrets Manager, never in code
key  = get_secret("pii/aes-key")               # 16/24/32-byte key, ideally a KMS data key

protected = people.select(
    F.sha2(F.concat(F.lower("email"), F.lit(salt)), 256).alias("email_hash"),
    F.regexp_replace("email", r"(?<=.).(?=[^@]*@)", "*").alias("email_masked"),
    F.concat(F.lit("******"), F.substring("phone", -4, 4)).alias("phone_masked"),
    F.base64(F.expr(f"aes_encrypt(pan, '{key}', 'GCM')")).alias("pan_encrypted"))
protected.show(truncate=False)
+----------------------------------------------------------------+------------------+------------+----------------------------------------------------+
|email_hash                                                      |email_masked      |phone_masked|pan_encrypted                                       |
+----------------------------------------------------------------+------------------+------------+----------------------------------------------------+
|e659d00aa8f1491d29f00f2d78b5e89a4fe7ad81213d494260aed9038431ab38|a*******@gmail.com|******3210  |+Ai5U2cy8+xt0bk28tCJ7D39qZnnmb1OfBkZU7lMCAE2CHsqgDU=|
+----------------------------------------------------------------+------------------+------------+----------------------------------------------------+
protected.select(F.expr(f"cast(aes_decrypt(unbase64(pan_encrypted), '{key}', 'GCM') as string)")).show()
ABCPK1234Q

Why salt the hash: an unsalted SHA-256 of a phone number can be reversed by hashing all possible phone numbers. Normalise before hashing (lower-case, trim) so the same email always gives the same hash. GCM encryption uses a random IV, so the same PAN encrypts differently each time: secure, but not joinable.

Finding PII you didn't know about

Glue's sensitive data detection inside a jobaws glue
from awsglueml.transforms import EntityDetector
detector = EntityDetector()
found = detector.classify_columns(dyf, ["EMAIL", "PHONE_NUMBER", "PERSON_NAME"], 0.1, 0.8)
# → {"contact": ["EMAIL"], "notes": ["PHONE_NUMBER"]}  columns whose sampled values match, above the threshold

Glue offers classify_columns() (which columns contain PII) and detect() (per row, with actions to mask or redact) for its managed entity types plus custom ones. For scanning whole S3 buckets, Amazon Macie finds PII across objects. Free-text columns like notes are where PII hides.

The hard part: deleting someone everywhere

An Iceberg DELETE only creates a new snapshot; time travel can still read the person's data until those snapshots are expired and the files removed. Keep the raw zone short-lived or encrypted per customer, and keep a list of every system that holds the data.

In a real AWS project

Where it shows up

In a pharma or healthcare data platform, raw files land in a restricted bucket with short retention. The Glue curation job drops unneeded identifiers, hashes patient and HCP IDs with a salt from Secrets Manager (so analysts can still join and count), encrypts the few fields that support needs with a KMS key, and registers tables in Lake Formation with column-level permissions. Deletion requests run a scheduled job that deletes by hashed ID across tables, then expires snapshots.

In the interview

I first minimise: drop PII nobody needs. For identifiers analysts must join on, I use a salted SHA-256 hash with the salt in Secrets Manager. For display I mask. For fields some roles must read, I encrypt with KMS-managed keys and control access with Lake Formation column and row filters. I discover unknown PII with Glue's sensitive data detection or Macie. For deletion requests in Iceberg, I delete the rows and then expire snapshots and remove orphan files, because old snapshots still contain the data.

Q: Why isn't hashing alone enough to protect a phone number?

Phone numbers have a small set of possible values, so an attacker can hash all of them and look up matches. A secret salt (or a keyed HMAC) prevents that, and the salt must be stored and rotated securely.

  • Minimise → mask → salted hash (joinable) → encrypt (readable with key) → restrict access.
  • Find PII with Glue sensitive data detection or Macie.
  • Iceberg delete + expire snapshots + remove orphans = really gone.

Data lineage: where did this number come from?

Lineage is a map of which jobs read which tables and wrote which tables (and columns), so you can trace any number back to its source.

What is it?

A graph: datasets are nodes, jobs are the arrows between them. Column-level lineage goes further: gold.revenue.net_amount came from silver.orders.amount minus silver.refunds.amount.

Why does it matter?

Three daily questions: “This dashboard number looks wrong: where does it come from?” (debugging), “If I change this column, what breaks?” (impact analysis), and “Show the auditor how this report is built” (compliance).

How does it work?

OpenLineage is an open standard. A Spark listener watches each job, reads the plans, and emits events: inputs, outputs, schema and column mappings. A backend collects them into a graph.

On AWS today

Glue 5.0 and later include the OpenLineage libraries for Spark (DataFrame jobs; DynamicFrame operations are not captured). EMR's Spark also includes them. Events go to Amazon SageMaker Unified Studio (built on DataZone), which shows the lineage graph, including column-level lineage, next to the catalog.

Turn on lineage for a Glue 5 job (job parameter --conf)aws glue
spark.extraListeners=io.openlineage.spark.agent.OpenLineageSparkListener
--conf spark.openlineage.transport.type=amazon_datazone_api
--conf spark.openlineage.transport.domainId=dzd_abc123
--conf spark.glue.accountId=123456789012

Use DataFrame or Spark SQL reads and writes on catalog tables (not raw rdd or DynamicFrame tricks) so the listener can see clear inputs and outputs. Outside AWS's tools, the same OpenLineage events can go to Marquez or other backends.

Lightweight lineage you control

Even without a lineage tool, record each run's inputs and outputs. Iceberg makes it easy: tag every commit with the run, and log which snapshots you read.

Run metadata on every Iceberg commitpyspark + iceberg
(df.writeTo("glue_catalog.gold.revenue")
   .option("snapshot-property.job_name", "gold_revenue")
   .option("snapshot-property.job_run_id", run_id)
   .option("snapshot-property.source_snapshots", f"silver.orders:{orders_snap}")
   .overwritePartitions())

# later: which run produced the current data, from which inputs?
spark.sql("SELECT committed_at, summary['job_run_id'], summary['source_snapshots'] FROM glue_catalog.gold.revenue.snapshots ORDER BY committed_at DESC LIMIT 5").show(truncate=False)

With input and output snapshot IDs recorded, you can reproduce any output exactly (time travel to the input snapshots and rerun), which is what auditors in regulated industries want.

In the interview

Lineage tells us which jobs read and write which tables and columns, for debugging wrong numbers, impact analysis before schema changes, and audits. On AWS, Glue 5 and EMR ship the OpenLineage Spark listener, and SageMaker Unified Studio collects the events into a lineage graph with column-level detail. I also stamp every Iceberg commit with the job run ID and the input snapshot IDs, so any output can be traced and reproduced exactly.

  • Lineage = graph of datasets and jobs; column-level for impact analysis.
  • OpenLineage listener (Glue 5+, EMR) → SageMaker Unified Studio.
  • Iceberg snapshot properties: run ID + input snapshots = traceable, reproducible.

Spark 4: what changes and what to say

Spark 4.0 was released in 2025 and 4.1 followed; AWS offers it on Glue 6.0 (Spark 4.1.1, Python 3.13) since August 2026. Most production code on Glue 5.x and EMR 7.x is still Spark 3.5. Your DataFrame code mostly works unchanged, but some defaults change behaviour, so migrations are deliberate projects. Everything on this page is Spark 4 only unless noted.

The changes that matter

FeatureWhat it meansImpact on your jobs
ANSI SQL mode on by defaultInvalid casts, overflows and divide-by-zero raise errors instead of returning NULLHidden bad data now fails jobs. Use try_cast, try_divide, or set spark.sql.ansi.enabled=false temporarily
VARIANT typeStore semi-structured JSON efficiently without a fixed schema; parse_json, variant_getReplaces JSON-as-string columns; with Iceberg v3 on Glue 6.0, variant shredding speeds reads
Spark Connect (much more complete)Thin client (pyspark-client, ~1.5 MB) talks to a remote Spark server over gRPC; spark.api.mode settingNotebooks and apps without a local JVM; some RDD/SparkContext APIs are not available in Connect mode
Python Data Source APIWrite custom readers/writers in pure PythonConnect to APIs or niche systems without Java
SQL pipe syntaxFROM orders |> WHERE amount > 100 |> AGGREGATE SUM(amount) GROUP BY countryReadability; optional
SQL UDFs, session variables, EXECUTE IMMEDIATEMore procedural SQLSome logic moves into SQL
transformWithState, State Data SourceNew streaming state API; read checkpoint stateBetter custom streaming logic and debugging
PlatformJava 17 default (8/11 dropped), Scala 2.13 only (2.12 dropped), Python 3.8 droppedCustom JARs must be rebuilt for Scala 2.13
Spark 4.1: Declarative Pipelines, Arrow-native Python UDFs/UDTFs, real-time streaming modeDeclarative ETL definitions; faster Python UDFs; millisecond streaming for stateless queriesAvailable on Glue 6.0
ANSI behaviour difference (we ran the 3.5 half on Spark 3.5.6)spark sql
SELECT CAST('abc' AS INT), 10/0, try_divide(10, 0);
-- Spark 3.5 (ANSI off):  NULL | NULL | NULL
-- Spark 4   (ANSI on):   error [CAST_INVALID_INPUT] ... Use `try_cast` to tolerate malformed input

Migrating a Glue 5.x job to Glue 6.0

  1. Run the job's tests on Spark 4 locally (pip install pyspark==4.1.*) and on a dev Glue 6.0 job with production-like data.
  2. Watch for ANSI errors; fix data or use try_* functions.
  3. Rebuild any Scala/Java JARs for Scala 2.13 / Java 17.
  4. Check Python dependencies for Python 3.13 support.
  5. Glue Studio offers a Spark upgrade agent and auto-upgrade options; still compare outputs row by row (counts, sums, NULL rates) before switching.
  6. Benefit: 30% lower DPU price on Glue 6.0 and Iceberg v3 features.

Most of our jobs run Spark 3.5 on Glue 5.1. I'm aware Spark 4 is available on Glue 6.0: the biggest behavioural change is ANSI mode on by default, so invalid casts and overflows fail instead of becoming NULL. It also adds the VARIANT type, a mature Spark Connect client-server mode, the Python data source API and new streaming state APIs. I'd migrate job by job with tests and output comparisons, fixing data issues that ANSI exposes.

  • Glue 6.0 = Spark 4.1. Most jobs are still 3.5.
  • ANSI on by default is the headline behaviour change.
  • VARIANT, Spark Connect, Python data sources, pipe syntax, transformWithState.

How to handle a live PySpark coding round

In a live round the interviewer watches how you think, not just the final code. Most problems are a combination of four building blocks: filter, aggregate, join and window. Say which blocks you will use before you type, write small steps with clear names, show the expected output, and then talk about scale: what shuffles, what could skew, what breaks with NULLs.

  1. Restate and clarify: “Ties count as the same rank? Should NULL salaries be excluded? Is the output one row per department?”
  2. Write 4–6 sample rows including an edge case (a tie, a NULL, a gap in dates).
  3. Name the building blocks: “This is a window problem: partition by department, order by salary descending, dense_rank.”
  4. Code in small named steps, not one giant chain.
  5. Show the result for your sample and check it by eye.
  6. Discuss scale: shuffles, skew, broadcast options, memory of collect_list, alternative with SQL.
Problem wordingBuilding block
“top N per group”, “second highest”, “latest record”Window + row_number / rank / dense_rank
“compared with previous”, “change since last”, “gaps”Window + lag / lead
“running total”, “moving average”Window frame (rows / range)
“consecutive days”, “streaks”Gaps and islands (date − row_number)
“sessions”, “inactivity”lag → flag → running sum
“missing”, “not in”, “new vs deleted”left_anti join
“per X, how many / total”groupBy + agg
“nested JSON”, “list column”explode / higher-order functions

All solutions below assume from pyspark.sql import functions as F, Window as W, and every output shown was produced by running the code on Spark 3.5.6. Try each problem before opening the solution.

Problems 1–5: ranking and top-N

Sample datapyspark
emp = spark.createDataFrame([
    (1, "Asha",  "ENG", 180000, None), (2, "Ravi", "ENG", 150000, 1),
    (3, "Meena", "ENG", 150000, 1),    (4, "John", "ENG", 120000, 2),
    (5, "Li",    "OPS",  90000, None), (6, "Sara", "OPS",  95000, 5),
    (7, "Omar",  "OPS",  70000, 5)],
    "emp_id int, name string, dept string, salary int, manager_id int")

1. Second highest salary in each department

Approach: rank salaries inside each department from highest to lowest. Use dense_rank so tied salaries share a rank and the next salary gets rank 2 (not 3). Keep rank 2.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("dept").orderBy(F.desc("salary"))
(emp.withColumn("r", F.dense_rank().over(w))
    .filter("r = 2")
    .select("dept", "name", "salary").show())
Output
+----+-----+------+
|dept| name|salary|
+----+-----+------+
| ENG| Ravi|150000|
| ENG|Meena|150000|
| OPS|   Li| 90000|
+----+-----+------+

Talk about: with row_number only one of Ravi/Meena would appear, chosen arbitrarily; with rank, after a tie at 1 the next rank would be 3. A department with a single salary level returns nothing; ask whether they want NULL instead (then left-join back to the list of departments). Cost: one shuffle by dept + sort.

2. Top 3 products by revenue in each category, including ties

Approach: aggregate first (revenue per product), then rank within category with rank so ties at position 3 are all kept.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
rev = sales.groupBy("category", "product").agg(F.sum("amount").alias("revenue"))
w = W.partitionBy("category").orderBy(F.desc("revenue"))
rev.withColumn("rk", F.rank().over(w)).filter("rk <= 3").orderBy("category", "rk").show()
Output
+--------+-------+-------+---+
|category|product|revenue| rk|
+--------+-------+-------+---+
| Laptops|     l1|    900|  1|
| Laptops|     l2|    700|  2|
|  Phones|     p1|    500|  1|
|  Phones|     p2|    300|  2|
|  Phones|     p3|    300|  2|
+--------+-------+-------+---+

Aggregating before the window shrinks the data the window must sort. That is a scale point worth saying aloud.

3. Employees who earn more than their manager

Approach: self-join the employee table to itself: employee side's manager_id = manager side's emp_id. Alias both sides to avoid ambiguous columns.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
e, m = emp.alias("e"), emp.alias("m")
(e.join(m, F.col("e.manager_id") == F.col("m.emp_id"))
  .filter(F.col("e.salary") > F.col("m.salary"))
  .select(F.col("e.name").alias("employee"), F.col("e.salary"),
          F.col("m.name").alias("manager"), F.col("m.salary").alias("manager_salary"))
  .show())
Output
+--------+------+-------+--------------+
|employee|salary|manager|manager_salary|
+--------+------+-------+--------------+
|    Sara| 95000|     Li|         90000|
+--------+------+-------+--------------+

4. Each salary's share of its department and difference from the department average

Approach: a window with only partitionBy covers the whole department, so sum and avg over it give department totals on every row.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
wd = W.partitionBy("dept")
emp.select("dept", "name", "salary",
    F.round(F.col("salary") / F.sum("salary").over(wd) * 100, 1).alias("pct_of_dept"),
    (F.col("salary") - F.avg("salary").over(wd)).cast("int").alias("diff_from_avg")).show()
Output
+----+-----+------+-----------+-------------+
|dept| name|salary|pct_of_dept|diff_from_avg|
+----+-----+------+-----------+-------------+
| ENG| Asha|180000|       30.0|        30000|
| ENG| Ravi|150000|       25.0|            0|
| ENG|Meena|150000|       25.0|            0|
| ENG| John|120000|       20.0|       -30000|
| OPS|   Li| 90000|       35.3|         5000|
| OPS| Sara| 95000|       37.3|        10000|
| OPS| Omar| 70000|       27.5|       -15000|
+----+-----+------+-----------+-------------+

5. Median value per group

Approach: percentile_approx returns an actual value from the data (cheap, approximate); median (3.4+) interpolates the exact median.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
sales.groupBy("category").agg(F.percentile_approx("amount", 0.5).alias("median_approx"),
                              F.median("amount").alias("median_exact")).show()
Output
+--------+-------------+------------+
|category|median_approx|median_exact|
+--------+-------------+------------+
|  Phones|          300|       300.0|
| Laptops|          700|       800.0|
+--------+-------------+------------+

Laptops has two values (700 and 900): the exact median is 800, while percentile_approx picks an existing value. Mention the difference; it shows you know what the functions do.

Problems 6–8: dedup, CDC, snapshot diff

6. Latest record per order from a CDC feed (with ties)

Approach: window by order, newest first, with a sequence column as tie-breaker; keep row 1; then drop orders whose latest state is DELETED. Full worked output is in the dedup lesson.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("order_id").orderBy(F.desc("updated_at"), F.desc("seq"))
latest = (cdc.withColumn("rn", F.row_number().over(w))
             .filter("rn = 1").drop("rn")
             .filter(F.col("status") != "DELETED"))

Apply deletes after choosing the latest version; filtering deletes first would let an older, non-deleted version survive. At scale this becomes the source subquery of an Iceberg MERGE.

7. Find duplicate rows and how often they occur

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
df.groupBy(df.columns).count().filter("count > 1")          # fully identical rows
df.groupBy("order_id").count().filter("count > 1")          # duplicate keys

8. Compare yesterday's and today's snapshot: inserted, deleted, changed

Approach: anti joins in both directions find inserts and deletes. For changes, join on the key and compare a hash of the non-key columns (NULL-safe).

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
cols = ["name", "city"]
def with_hash(df):
    return df.withColumn("h", F.sha2(F.concat_ws("||",
        *[F.coalesce(F.col(c).cast("string"), F.lit("~")) for c in cols]), 256))
t, y = with_hash(today), with_hash(yesterday)

inserted = t.join(y, "id", "left_anti").drop("h")
deleted  = y.join(t, "id", "left_anti").drop("h")
changed  = (t.alias("t").join(y.alias("y"), "id")
             .filter(F.col("t.h") != F.col("y.h"))
             .select("id", "t.name", "t.city", F.col("y.city").alias("old_city")))
Output
inserted                deleted                  changed
+---+----+-----+        +---+----+------+        +---+----+------+--------+
| id|name| city|        | id|name|  city|        | id|name|  city|old_city|
+---+----+-----+        +---+----+------+        +---+----+------+--------+
|  4|Omar|Dubai|        |  3|  Li|Austin|        |  2|Ravi|Mumbai|    Pune|
+---+----+-----+        +---+----+------+        +---+----+------+--------+

The coalesce(..., "~") matters: concat_ws skips NULLs, so (NULL, "a") and ("a", NULL) would otherwise hash the same. exceptAll compares whole rows too, but cannot tell “changed” from “deleted + inserted”.

Problems 9–13: time series and sessions

9. Users who logged in on 3 or more consecutive days
