Approach (gaps and islands): remove duplicate days, number each user's days with row_number, and subtract that number from the date. Within a streak of consecutive days, date and row number both go up by 1, so date − row_number stays constant. Group by that constant.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
d = logins.dropDuplicates(["user_id", "d"])                 # two logins on one day = one day
w = W.partitionBy("user_id").orderBy("d")
step = (d.withColumn("rn", F.row_number().over(w))
         .withColumn("grp", F.date_sub("d", F.col("rn"))))
step.orderBy("user_id", "d").show()
(step.groupBy("user_id", "grp")
     .agg(F.min("d").alias("start"), F.max("d").alias("end"), F.count("*").alias("days"))
     .filter("days >= 3").select("user_id", "start", "end", "days").show())
Output
+-------+----------+---+----------+
|user_id|         d| rn|       grp|
+-------+----------+---+----------+
|     u1|2026-10-01|  1|2026-09-30|   <- same grp = same streak
|     u1|2026-10-02|  2|2026-09-30|
|     u1|2026-10-03|  3|2026-09-30|
|     u1|2026-10-05|  4|2026-10-01|   <- gap: new grp
|     u2|2026-10-01|  1|2026-09-30|
|     u2|2026-10-03|  2|2026-10-01|
|     u2|2026-10-04|  3|2026-10-01|
+-------+----------+---+----------+

+-------+----------+----------+----+
|user_id|     start|       end|days|
+-------+----------+----------+----+
|     u1|2026-10-01|2026-10-03|   3|
+-------+----------+----------+----+

10. Sessionise clicks: new session after 30 minutes of inactivity

Approach: three steps. (1) lag gives the previous click time. (2) Flag a row as a new session when there is no previous click or the gap is over 30 minutes. (3) A running sum of the flags numbers the sessions.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("user_id").orderBy("event_ts")
s = (clicks
     .withColumn("prev_ts", F.lag("event_ts").over(w))
     .withColumn("gap_min", (F.col("event_ts").cast("long") - F.col("prev_ts").cast("long")) / 60)
     .withColumn("new_session", F.when(F.col("prev_ts").isNull() | (F.col("gap_min") > 30), 1).otherwise(0))
     .withColumn("session_no", F.sum("new_session").over(w.rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0))))
s.show()
(s.groupBy("user_id", "session_no")
  .agg(F.min("event_ts").alias("start"), F.max("event_ts").alias("end"), F.count("*").alias("events"))
  .orderBy("user_id", "session_no").show())
Output
+-------+-------------------+-------------------+-------+-----------+----------+
|user_id|           event_ts|            prev_ts|gap_min|new_session|session_no|
+-------+-------------------+-------------------+-------+-----------+----------+
|     u1|2026-11-01 10:00:00|               NULL|   NULL|          1|         1|
|     u1|2026-11-01 10:10:00|2026-11-01 10:00:00|   10.0|          0|         1|
|     u1|2026-11-01 11:00:00|2026-11-01 10:10:00|   50.0|          1|         2|
|     u1|2026-11-01 11:05:00|2026-11-01 11:00:00|    5.0|          0|         2|
|     u2|2026-11-01 09:00:00|               NULL|   NULL|          1|         1|
+-------+-------------------+-------------------+-------+-----------+----------+

+-------+----------+-------------------+-------------------+------+
|user_id|session_no|              start|                end|events|
+-------+----------+-------------------+-------------------+------+
|     u1|         1|2026-11-01 10:00:00|2026-11-01 10:10:00|     2|
|     u1|         2|2026-11-01 11:00:00|2026-11-01 11:05:00|     2|
|     u2|         1|2026-11-01 09:00:00|2026-11-01 09:00:00|     1|
+-------+----------+-------------------+-------------------+------+

Scale talk: a bot user with 50 million clicks makes one giant window partition (skew); filter bots first. Sessions crossing midnight in daily runs need the previous day's last click per user carried over. Built-in alternative: F.session_window("event_ts", "30 minutes") in a groupBy.

11. Month-over-month revenue growth

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
monthly = orders.groupBy(F.trunc("d", "month").alias("month")).agg(F.sum("amount").alias("rev"))
w = W.orderBy("month")        # tiny table (one row per month): a single partition is fine; say so
(monthly.withColumn("prev", F.lag("rev").over(w))
        .withColumn("mom_pct", F.round((F.col("rev") - F.col("prev")) / F.col("prev") * 100, 1))
        .show())
Output
+----------+-----+-----+-------+
|     month|  rev| prev|mom_pct|
+----------+-----+-----+-------+
|2026-08-01|100.0| NULL|   NULL|
|2026-09-01|200.0|100.0|  100.0|
|2026-10-01|160.0|200.0|  -20.0|
+----------+-----+-----+-------+

12. Forward-fill missing sensor readings

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("sensor").orderBy("t").rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0)
readings.withColumn("temp_ff", F.last("temp", ignorenulls=True).over(w)).show()
Output
+------+-----+----+-------+
|sensor|    t|temp|temp_ff|
+------+-----+----+-------+
|    s1|10:00|21.5|   21.5|
|    s1|10:01|NULL|   21.5|
|    s1|10:02|NULL|   21.5|
|    s1|10:03|22.0|   22.0|
|    s1|10:04|NULL|   22.0|
+------+-----+----+-------+

Back-fill: F.first("temp", ignorenulls=True) over rowsBetween(0, W.unboundedFollowing).

13. Fill missing dates with zero revenue

Approach: build a calendar with sequence, cross-join it with the list of stores (small × small), left-join the real data, fill NULLs with 0.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
bounds = daily.agg(F.min("d").alias("lo"), F.max("d").alias("hi"))
cal = bounds.select(F.explode(F.sequence("lo", "hi", F.expr("interval 1 day"))).alias("d"))
(daily.select("store").distinct().crossJoin(cal)
      .join(daily, ["store", "d"], "left")
      .fillna({"revenue": 0})
      .orderBy("store", "d").show())
Output
+-----+----------+-------+
|store|         d|revenue|
+-----+----------+-------+
|   S1|2026-11-01|    100|
|   S1|2026-11-02|      0|
|   S1|2026-11-03|      0|
|   S1|2026-11-04|     80|
|   S2|2026-11-01|      0|
|   S2|2026-11-02|     50|
|   S2|2026-11-03|      0|
|   S2|2026-11-04|      0|
+-----+----------+-------+

Problems 14–17: text, arrays, JSON

14. Word count, top words without stop words

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
stop = spark.createDataFrame([(w,) for w in ["the", "a", "an", "and", "of", "to", "in"]], "word string")
(txt.select(F.explode(F.split(F.lower(F.regexp_replace("value", r"[^a-zA-Z\s]", "")), r"\s+")).alias("word"))
    .filter(F.length("word") > 0)
    .join(F.broadcast(stop), "word", "left_anti")             # remove stop words, no shuffle
    .groupBy("word").count()
    .orderBy(F.desc("count"), "word").show())
Output (input: "The cat and the hat." / "A cat in the hat, the end")
+----+-----+
|word|count|
+----+-----+
| cat|    2|
| hat|    2|
| end|    1|
+----+-----+

15. Flatten an order JSON with a nested customer and an items array

Inputjson
{"order_id":"o1","customer":{"id":"c9","address":{"city":"Hyderabad","pin":"500081"}},
 "items":[{"sku":"A1","qty":2,"price":250.0},{"sku":"B7","qty":1,"price":999.0}],
 "tags":["prime","festive"]}

Approach: two outputs: a header table (one row per order, struct fields selected with dots, totals with aggregate) and a lines table (one row per item with posexplode_outer). This avoids multiplying rows by exploding two arrays together.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
raw = spark.read.schema(order_schema).json("s3://in/orders_json/", multiLine=True)

header = raw.select(
    "order_id",
    F.col("customer.id").alias("customer_id"),
    F.col("customer.address.city").alias("city"),
    F.col("customer.address.pin").alias("pin"),
    F.array_join("tags", ",").alias("tags"),
    F.aggregate("items", F.lit(0.0), lambda acc, i: acc + i["qty"] * i["price"]).alias("order_total"))

lines = (raw.select("order_id", F.posexplode_outer("items").alias("line_no", "item"))
            .select("order_id", "line_no", "item.sku", "item.qty", "item.price"))
Output
header: o1 | c9 | Hyderabad | 500081 | prime,festive | 1499.0
lines:  o1 | 0 | A1 | 2 | 250.0
        o1 | 1 | B7 | 1 | 999.0

16. Extract the email domain, count users per domain, mask emails

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
(users.select(F.lower(F.regexp_extract("email", r"@(.+)$", 1)).alias("domain"),
              F.regexp_replace("email", r"(^.{2})[^@]*(@.*$)", "$1***$2").alias("masked"))
      .groupBy("domain").count())

No UDF needed; built-in regex functions are NULL-safe and run in the JVM.

17. Count how often each tag appears (array column)

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
posts.select(F.explode("tags").alias("tag")).groupBy("tag").count()
# distinct tags per user
posts.groupBy("user_id").agg(F.array_distinct(F.flatten(F.collect_list("tags"))).alias("tags"))

Problems 18–22: pipeline patterns

18. SCD Type 2 for a customer dimension

The full topic, with every SCD type, design choices, edge cases and more practice problems, has its own chapter: Slowly Changing Dimensions.

Slowly Changing Dimension Type 2 keeps history: when a customer's city changes, the old row is closed (end date set, is_current = false) and a new current row is added. Unchanged customers stay as they are; new customers get a new current row.

dim_customer (before)
idnamecitystartendcurrent
1AshaHyd2026-01-01NULLtrue
2RaviPune2026-01-01NULLtrue
3LiAustin2025-06-012025-12-31false
3LiDallas2026-01-01NULLtrue
today's source
idnamecity
1AshaHyd
2RaviMumbai
4OmarDubai

Approach: split the current rows from history; full-join current rows with the source; compare a hash of the tracked attributes; then produce four groups: history (unchanged), current-and-unchanged (keep), current-and-changed (expire), and new versions (changed + brand new), and union them.

Show solution (DataFrame version, tested)
Solutionpyspark
run_date = F.to_date(F.lit("2026-11-03"))
attrs = ["name", "city"]
def h(alias):   # NULL-safe hash of the tracked attributes on one side of the join
    return F.sha2(F.concat_ws("||", *[F.coalesce(F.col(f"{alias}.{a}").cast("string"), F.lit("~")) for a in attrs]), 256)

cur  = dim.filter("is_current")
hist = dim.filter(~F.col("is_current"))
j = (cur.alias("c").join(src.alias("s"), "customer_id", "full")
        .withColumn("ch", h("c")).withColumn("sh", h("s")))
cur_cols = [F.col("customer_id")] + [F.col(f"c.{x}") for x in ["name", "city", "start_date", "end_date", "is_current"]]

unchanged = j.filter(F.col("c.is_current").isNotNull() &
                     (F.col("s.name").isNull() | (F.col("ch") == F.col("sh")))).select(*cur_cols)
changed   = j.filter(F.col("c.is_current").isNotNull() & F.col("s.name").isNotNull() & (F.col("ch") != F.col("sh")))
expired   = (changed.select(*cur_cols)
             .withColumn("end_date", F.date_sub(run_date, 1)).withColumn("is_current", F.lit(False)))
new_rows  = (changed.select("customer_id", "s.name", "s.city")
             .unionByName(j.filter(F.col("c.is_current").isNull()).select("customer_id", "s.name", "s.city"))
             .withColumn("start_date", run_date)
             .withColumn("end_date", F.lit(None).cast("date"))
             .withColumn("is_current", F.lit(True)))

result = hist.unionByName(unchanged).unionByName(expired).unionByName(new_rows)
result.orderBy("customer_id", "start_date").show()
Output
+-----------+----+------+----------+----------+----------+
|customer_id|name|  city|start_date|  end_date|is_current|
+-----------+----+------+----------+----------+----------+
|          1|Asha|   Hyd|2026-01-01|      NULL|      true|
|          2|Ravi|  Pune|2026-01-01|2026-11-02|     false|   <- expired
|          2|Ravi|Mumbai|2026-11-03|      NULL|      true|   <- new version
|          3|  Li|Austin|2025-06-01|2025-12-31|     false|
|          3|  Li|Dallas|2026-01-01|      NULL|      true|   <- not in source: kept
|          4|Omar| Dubai|2026-11-03|      NULL|      true|   <- new customer
+-----------+----+------+----------+----------+----------+

Customer 3 is missing from today's source; here we keep the row (a partial feed). If the source is a full snapshot and missing means deleted, expire it too. Say which assumption you chose.

Show solution (Iceberg MERGE version, what you would ship)
One atomic MERGEspark sql
MERGE INTO glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer t
USING (
  SELECT s.customer_id AS merge_key, s.* FROM stg_customer s          -- normal match: expire / insert new
  UNION ALL
  SELECT NULL AS merge_key, s.* FROM stg_customer s                   -- changed rows again with NULL key:
  JOIN glue_catalog.dw.dim_customer d                                  --   they can never match, so they INSERT
    ON d.customer_id = s.customer_id AND d.is_current
   AND NOT (d.name <=> s.name AND d.city <=> s.city)
) u
ON t.customer_id = u.merge_key AND t.is_current
WHEN MATCHED AND NOT (t.name <=> u.name AND t.city <=> u.city) THEN
  UPDATE SET is_current = false, end_date = date_sub(current_date(), 1)
WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN
  INSERT (customer_id, name, city, start_date, end_date, is_current)
  VALUES (u.customer_id, u.name, u.city, current_date(), NULL, true)

The trick: each changed customer appears twice in the source. The copy with the real key matches the current row and expires it; the copy with a NULL key cannot match anything, so it is inserted as the new current row. All in one atomic commit. <=> is NULL-safe equality.

19. Idempotent incremental load with a high-water mark

Approach: store the last processed updated_at in a control table; read source rows newer than it minus a small lookback (to catch late commits); MERGE on the key; advance the mark only after the MERGE succeeds.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
hwm = (spark.table("glue_catalog.ops.load_control")
            .filter("table_name = 'orders'").select("last_updated_at").first()[0])

query = f"""(SELECT * FROM orders
             WHERE updated_at > TIMESTAMP '{hwm}' - INTERVAL '2 hours') q"""     # 2 h lookback
new = spark.read.format("jdbc").options(**jdbc_opts).option("dbtable", query).load()

new.createOrReplaceTempView("new_orders")
spark.sql("""
  MERGE INTO glue_catalog.curated.orders t
  USING (SELECT * FROM (SELECT *, ROW_NUMBER() OVER (PARTITION BY order_id ORDER BY updated_at DESC) rn
                        FROM new_orders) WHERE rn = 1) s
  ON t.order_id = s.order_id
  WHEN MATCHED AND s.updated_at >= t.updated_at THEN UPDATE SET *
  WHEN NOT MATCHED THEN INSERT *""")

new_hwm = new.agg(F.max("updated_at")).first()[0]
if new_hwm is not None:                                       # only after the MERGE committed
    spark.sql(f"UPDATE glue_catalog.ops.load_control SET last_updated_at = TIMESTAMP '{new_hwm}' "
              "WHERE table_name = 'orders'")

Why it is safe to re-run: the overlap window plus a keyed, timestamp-guarded MERGE means overlapping or repeated reads never create duplicates, and the mark only moves forward after success.

20. Pivot monthly sales per store and back

See the worked example with output in Aggregations: groupBy("store").pivot("month", months).agg(F.sum("revenue")) and unpivot.

21. Split rows into good and bad by data-quality rules in one pass

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
rules = {
    "null_order_id": F.col("order_id").isNull(),
    "neg_amount":    F.col("amount") < 0,
    "bad_country":   ~F.coalesce(F.col("country"), F.lit("")).rlike("^[A-Z]{2}$"),   # NULL counts as bad
}
checked = df.withColumn("dq_errors",
    F.filter(F.array(*[F.when(cond, F.lit(name)) for name, cond in rules.items()]),
             lambda x: x.isNotNull()))
checked.show(truncate=False)
good = checked.filter(F.size("dq_errors") == 0).drop("dq_errors")
bad  = checked.filter(F.size("dq_errors") > 0)
Output
+--------+------+-------+-------------------------+
|order_id|amount|country|dq_errors                |
+--------+------+-------+-------------------------+
|o1      |100.0 |IN     |[]                       |
|NULL    |50.0  |US     |[null_order_id]          |
|o3      |-5.0  |uk     |[neg_amount, bad_country]|
|o4      |20.0  |NULL   |[bad_country]            |
+--------+------+-------+-------------------------+

Without the coalesce, a NULL country makes the rule NULL, when returns NULL and the row is not flagged. Pointing that out is exactly the kind of NULL awareness interviewers look for. Cache checked if you write both outputs.

22. Cumulative distinct users per day

Approach: a running countDistinct over a window is not supported. Instead find each user's first day, count new users per day, and take a running sum.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
first = events.groupBy("user_id").agg(F.min("d").alias("first_day"))
(first.groupBy("first_day").agg(F.count("*").alias("new_users"))
      .withColumn("cumulative_users",
                  F.sum("new_users").over(W.orderBy("first_day").rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0)))
      .orderBy("first_day").show())
Output
+----------+---------+----------------+
| first_day|new_users|cumulative_users|
+----------+---------+----------------+
|2026-11-01|        2|               2|
|2026-11-02|        1|               3|
+----------+---------+----------------+

2 November had users u2 and u3, but u2 was already counted on 1 November, so only one user is new. Days with no new users (3 November) do not appear; join to a calendar if they must.

Problems 23–30: warm-up problems

Interviewers often start with a simple problem to see whether you can write PySpark fluently without help. These are the ones reported most often in screening rounds: cleaning data, running totals, anti joins, lists, renaming, and conditional aggregation. You should be able to write each in under five minutes. Every output below came from Spark 3.5.6.

23. Clean a messy orders file

Approach: trim text, standardise case, convert types safely so bad values become NULL instead of failing, try two date formats, drop rows without a key, then keep one row per order.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
clean = (raw.select(
            F.trim("order_id").alias("order_id"),
            F.lower(F.trim("customer_id")).alias("customer_id"),
            F.expr("try_cast(trim(amount) AS DECIMAL(10,2))").alias("amount"),          # 'abc' -> NULL
            F.coalesce(F.expr("try_to_timestamp(order_date, 'yyyy-MM-dd')"),            # try format 1
                       F.expr("try_to_timestamp(order_date, 'yyyy/MM/dd')")).cast("date").alias("order_date"),
            F.upper(F.trim("country")).alias("country"))
         .filter(F.col("order_id").isNotNull()))

w = W.partitionBy("order_id").orderBy(F.col("amount").isNull(), F.desc("amount"))   # prefer rows with a valid amount
clean = clean.withColumn("rn", F.row_number().over(w)).filter("rn = 1").drop("rn")
Output
input rows: (" o1 ","C1"," 120.50","2026-11-01","in")  ("o2","c2","abc",...)  ("o2","c2","80",...)
            (NULL,"c3","10",...)  ("o4","c3","-5","2026/11/02","US")
+--------+-----------+------+----------+-------+
|order_id|customer_id|amount|order_date|country|
+--------+-----------+------+----------+-------+
|      o1|         c1|120.50|2026-11-01|     IN|
|      o2|         c2| 80.00|2026-11-01|     IN|
|      o4|         c3| -5.00|2026-11-02|     US|
+--------+-----------+------+----------+-------+

Talk about: try_cast and try_to_timestamp (Spark 3.5) return NULL on bad input in both ANSI modes. The negative amount survived: say you would send it to a quarantine table with a rule, not silently drop it. In a real job the file is read with an explicit schema of strings first, then cleaned like this.

24. Running total, first and last order date, order number per customer

Approach: one window ordered by date for the running total and order number, and one window without ordering for the first and last dates.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w    = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy("d")
wall = W.partitionBy("customer_id")
orders.select("customer_id", "d", "amount",
    F.sum("amount").over(w.rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0)).alias("running_total"),
    F.min("d").over(wall).alias("first_order"),
    F.max("d").over(wall).alias("last_order"),
    F.row_number().over(w).alias("order_no")).show()
Output
+-----------+----------+------+-------------+-----------+----------+--------+
|customer_id|         d|amount|running_total|first_order|last_order|order_no|
+-----------+----------+------+-------------+-----------+----------+--------+
|         c1|2026-11-01|   100|          100| 2026-11-01|2026-11-07|       1|
|         c1|2026-11-03|    50|          150| 2026-11-01|2026-11-07|       2|
|         c1|2026-11-07|    30|          180| 2026-11-01|2026-11-07|       3|
|         c2|2026-11-02|    70|           70| 2026-11-02|2026-11-05|       1|
|         c2|2026-11-05|    20|           90| 2026-11-02|2026-11-05|       2|
+-----------+----------+------+-------------+-----------+----------+--------+

Always write the frame (rowsBetween) for running totals. With the default frame, two orders on the same date would both show the combined total.

25. Customers with no orders, and order counts including zero

Approach: a left anti join finds customers without orders. For counts including zero, left join and count a column from the orders side (it is NULL when there is no order).

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
no_orders = cust.join(orders, "customer_id", "left_anti")

counts = (cust.join(orders, "customer_id", "left")
              .groupBy("customer_id", "name")
              .agg(F.count("d").alias("orders"),                       # count(col) skips NULL -> 0
                   F.coalesce(F.sum("amount"), F.lit(0)).alias("total")))
Output
no_orders:  | c3 | Meena |

+-----------+-----+------+-----+
|customer_id| name|orders|total|
+-----------+-----+------+-----+
|         c1| Asha|     3|  180|
|         c2| Ravi|     2|   90|
|         c3|Meena|     0|    0|
+-----------+-----+------+-----+

The classic mistake is F.count("*"), which gives Meena 1 because the left join still produces one row for her.

26. Rows to a comma-separated list, and back to rows

Approach: collect_set (or collect_list to keep duplicates) builds an array per group; concat_ws joins it. split + explode reverses it.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
lst = items.groupBy("order_id").agg(
          F.concat_ws(",", F.sort_array(F.collect_set("item"))).alias("items"),   # sort: collect order is not guaranteed
          F.count("*").alias("n"))
back = lst.select("order_id", F.explode(F.split("items", ",")).alias("item"))
Output
+--------+--------+---+         +--------+----+
|order_id|   items|  n|         |order_id|item|
+--------+--------+---+         +--------+----+
|      o1|book,pen|  3|         |      o1|book|
|      o2|     bag|  1|         |      o1| pen|
+--------+--------+---+         |      o2| bag|
                                +--------+----+

Order o1 had “pen” twice: collect_set removed the duplicate (n is still 3). Mention that collect_list on a very popular key can run a task out of memory.

27. Rename all columns to snake_case and split a full name

Approach: column names are plain Python strings, so rename them with a list comprehension and toDF. Split the name into an array and pick parts by position, handling one-word names.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
import re
df = raw.toDF(*[re.sub(r"[^0-9a-zA-Z]+", "_", c).strip("_").lower() for c in raw.columns])

p = F.split("full_name", " ")
df.select("full_name",
          p.getItem(0).alias("first_name"),
          F.when(F.size(p) > 1, F.element_at(p, -1)).alias("last_name"),               # -1 = last element
          F.when(F.size(p) > 2, F.concat_ws(" ", F.slice(p, 2, F.size(p) - 2))).alias("middle"),
          "home_city").show()
Output
columns before: ['Full Name', 'Home City']
+---------------+----------+---------+------+---------+
|      full_name|first_name|last_name|middle|home_city|
+---------------+----------+---------+------+---------+
|Sai Kumar Reddy|       Sai|    Reddy| Kumar|      Hyd|
|           Asha|      Asha|     NULL|  NULL|     Pune|
|         Li Wei|        Li|      Wei|  NULL|   Austin|
+---------------+----------+---------+------+---------+

toDF(*names) renames every column in one projection, unlike a loop of withColumnRenamed.

28. Most frequent value (mode) per group

Approach: count per (group, value), rank the counts inside each group, keep rank 1. Spark 3.4+ also has a built-in mode aggregate.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
c = pay.groupBy("country", "method").count()
(c.withColumn("rk", F.rank().over(W.partitionBy("country").orderBy(F.desc("count"))))
  .filter("rk = 1").drop("rk").show())

pay.groupBy("country").agg(F.mode("method").alias("mode_method")).show()     # Spark 3.4+
Output
+-------+------+-----+
|country|method|count|
+-------+------+-----+
|     IN|   upi|    2|
|     UK|  cash|    1|
|     US|  card|    2|
+-------+------+-----+

rank keeps all tied values; F.mode returns one of them arbitrarily. Ask the interviewer which they want.

29. Average salary per department excluding the highest and lowest

Approach: put the department's max and min on every row with a window, filter them out, then aggregate.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
wd = W.partitionBy("dept")
(emp.withColumn("mx", F.max("salary").over(wd)).withColumn("mn", F.min("salary").over(wd))
    .filter((F.col("salary") != F.col("mx")) & (F.col("salary") != F.col("mn")))
    .groupBy("dept").agg(F.round(F.avg("salary"), 1).alias("avg_middle"), F.count("*").alias("n")).show())
Output
ENG salaries 180,150,150,120 | OPS 90,95,70 | HR 60,80
+----+----------+---+
|dept|avg_middle|  n|
+----+----------+---+
| ENG|     150.0|  2|
| OPS|      90.0|  1|
+----+----------+---+

HR disappeared: with only two salaries, both are the max or the min. Say this edge case aloud and ask what the business wants (NULL row, or keep small departments as they are).

30. Several conditional counts and sums in one pass

Approach: use count(when(...)) and sum(when(...)) inside one agg instead of filtering the DataFrame several times.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
orders.groupBy("d").agg(
    F.count("*").alias("orders"),
    F.count(F.when(F.col("status") == "SHIPPED", 1)).alias("shipped"),
    F.count(F.when(F.col("status") == "CANCELLED", 1)).alias("cancelled"),
    F.sum(F.when(F.col("status") != "CANCELLED", F.col("amount")).otherwise(0)).alias("net_revenue"),
    F.round(F.avg((F.col("status") == "CANCELLED").cast("int")) * 100, 1).alias("cancel_pct")).show()
Output
+----------+------+-------+---------+-----------+----------+
|         d|orders|shipped|cancelled|net_revenue|cancel_pct|
+----------+------+-------+---------+-----------+----------+
|2026-11-01|     3|      2|        1|      170.0|      33.3|
|2026-11-02|     2|      0|        1|       20.0|      50.0|
+----------+------+-------+---------+-----------+----------+

The average of a 0/1 flag is the rate. One scan and one shuffle instead of one per metric; Spark 3.5 also has F.count_if(cond).

Problems 31–40: joins and analytics

These are the mid-difficulty problems that make up most live rounds: comparing rows with their group, existence checks across products, percentiles, period-over-period growth, retention, funnels and gaps. For each one, state the building blocks first, then code, then mention how it behaves on large data.

31. Flag the cheapest and the fastest flight for each date

Approach: compare each row with the group minimum from a window. Ties all get “Yes”.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
wd = W.partitionBy("d")
flights.select("d", "airline", "price", "minutes",
    F.when(F.col("price")   == F.min("price").over(wd),   "Yes").otherwise("No").alias("cheapest"),
    F.when(F.col("minutes") == F.min("minutes").over(wd), "Yes").otherwise("No").alias("fastest")).show()
Output
+----------+-------+-----+-------+--------+-------+
|         d|airline|price|minutes|cheapest|fastest|
+----------+-------+-----+-------+--------+-------+
|2026-11-01|     6E| 4800|    165|     Yes|     No|
|2026-11-01|     AI| 5200|    150|      No|     No|
|2026-11-01|     UK| 5200|    140|      No|    Yes|
|2026-11-02|     6E| 6100|    150|     Yes|    Yes|
|2026-11-02|     AI| 6100|    150|     Yes|    Yes|
+----------+-------+-----+-------+--------+-------+

32. Customers who bought A and B but never C

Approach: collapse each customer to one row with a flag per product, then filter on the flags. This avoids three self-joins.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
flags = purchases.groupBy("customer_id").agg(
    F.max((F.col("product") == "A").cast("int")).alias("a"),
    F.max((F.col("product") == "B").cast("int")).alias("b"),
    F.max((F.col("product") == "C").cast("int")).alias("c"))
flags.filter("a = 1 AND b = 1 AND c = 0").select("customer_id").show()

# same idea with a set
(purchases.groupBy("customer_id").agg(F.collect_set("product").alias("ps"))
          .filter(F.array_contains("ps", "A") & F.array_contains("ps", "B") & ~F.array_contains("ps", "C")))
Output
c1: A,B   c2: A,B,C   c3: A   c4: B,A,A
+-----------+
|customer_id|
+-----------+
|         c1|
|         c4|
+-----------+

One shuffle. The join version (semi join for A, semi join for B, anti join for C) works too, but costs three shuffles.

33. Top 20% of customers by spend

Approach: ntile(5) splits ordered rows into 5 equal buckets; bucket 1 is the top 20%. percent_rank gives each row's relative position.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.orderBy(F.desc("spend"))          # no partitionBy: fine after aggregation to one row per customer
(spend.withColumn("pr", F.round(F.percent_rank().over(w), 2))
      .withColumn("bucket", F.ntile(5).over(w))
      .filter("bucket = 1").show())
Output
+-----------+-----+----+------+
|customer_id|spend|  pr|bucket|
+-----------+-----+----+------+
|         c1|  900| 0.0|     1|
|         c2|  850|0.11|     1|
+-----------+-----+----+------+

A global window moves everything to one task. That is acceptable for millions of aggregated rows; for billions, compute the cut-off with approxQuantile("spend", [0.8], 0.01) and filter.

34. Quarter-over-quarter and year-over-year growth per region

Approach: aggregate to one row per region and quarter, then lag(1) for the previous quarter and lag(4) for the same quarter last year.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
q = sales.groupBy("region", F.year("d").alias("yr"), F.quarter("d").alias("qtr")).agg(F.sum("amount").alias("rev"))
w = W.partitionBy("region").orderBy("yr", "qtr")
(q.withColumn("prev_q",  F.lag("rev").over(w))
  .withColumn("qoq_pct", F.round((F.col("rev") - F.col("prev_q")) / F.col("prev_q") * 100, 1))
  .withColumn("yoy_prev", F.lag("rev", 4).over(w))
  .withColumn("yoy_pct", F.round((F.col("rev") - F.col("yoy_prev")) / F.col("yoy_prev") * 100, 1)).show())
Output
+------+----+---+-----+------+-------+--------+-------+
|region|  yr|qtr|  rev|prev_q|qoq_pct|yoy_prev|yoy_pct|
+------+----+---+-----+------+-------+--------+-------+
| South|2025|  4|100.0|  NULL|   NULL|    NULL|   NULL|
| South|2026|  1|120.0| 100.0|   20.0|    NULL|   NULL|
| South|2026|  2| 90.0| 120.0|  -25.0|    NULL|   NULL|
| South|2026|  3|135.0|  90.0|   50.0|    NULL|   NULL|
| South|2026|  4|150.0| 135.0|   11.1|   100.0|   50.0|
+------+----+---+-----+------+-------+--------+-------+

lag(4) is only “same quarter last year” if no quarter is missing. If quarters can be missing, self-join on (region, yr − 1, qtr) instead. Raise it; it is the follow-up question.

35. Monthly retention and churn

Approach: reduce to one row per user per active month. Shift a copy back by one month and left join: a match means the user was also active the next month.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
m   = activity.select("user_id", F.trunc("d", "month").alias("m")).distinct()
nxt = m.select("user_id", F.add_months("m", -1).alias("m"), F.lit(1).alias("back"))     # "I was active the month after m"

(m.join(nxt, ["user_id", "m"], "left")
  .groupBy("m").agg(F.count("*").alias("active"), F.count("back").alias("retained_next_month"))
  .withColumn("retention_pct", F.round(F.col("retained_next_month") / F.col("active") * 100, 1))
  .withColumn("churned", F.col("active") - F.col("retained_next_month")).show())
Output
+----------+------+-------------------+-------------+-------+
|         m|active|retained_next_month|retention_pct|churned|
+----------+------+-------------------+-------------+-------+
|2026-09-01|     2|                  1|         50.0|      1|
|2026-10-01|     3|                  2|         66.7|      1|
|2026-11-01|     3|                  0|          0.0|      3|
+----------+------+-------------------+-------------+-------+

The latest month always shows 0% because next month has not happened yet: exclude it in the report. The same pattern with days instead of months gives day-1 or day-7 retention.

36. Funnel conversion: view → cart → purchase

Approach: one row per user with a 0/1 flag per step, then sum the flags, requiring earlier steps for later ones.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
steps = ["view", "cart", "purchase"]
f = events.groupBy("user_id").agg(*[F.max((F.col("event") == e).cast("int")).alias(e) for e in steps])
(f.agg(F.sum("view").alias("viewed"),
       F.sum(F.col("view") * F.col("cart")).alias("carted"),
       F.sum(F.col("view") * F.col("cart") * F.col("purchase")).alias("purchased"))
  .withColumn("view_to_cart_pct",     F.round(F.col("carted") / F.col("viewed") * 100, 1))
  .withColumn("cart_to_purchase_pct", F.round(F.col("purchased") / F.col("carted") * 100, 1)).show())
Output
+------+------+---------+----------------+--------------------+
|viewed|carted|purchased|view_to_cart_pct|cart_to_purchase_pct|
+------+------+---------+----------------+--------------------+
|     4|     2|        1|            50.0|                50.0|
+------+------+---------+----------------+--------------------+

User u4 purchased without a cart event and is not counted as a funnel purchase. A stricter funnel also checks that the timestamps are in order (min view time < min cart time).

37. Find missing invoice numbers

Approach: sort the numbers and compare each with the next one using lead. A difference above 1 is a gap; sequence expands it into individual numbers.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
gaps = (inv.withColumn("nxt", F.lead("invoice_no").over(W.orderBy("invoice_no")))
           .filter(F.col("nxt") - F.col("invoice_no") > 1)
           .select((F.col("invoice_no") + 1).alias("missing_from"), (F.col("nxt") - 1).alias("missing_to")))
missing = gaps.select(F.explode(F.sequence("missing_from", "missing_to")).alias("missing_invoice"))
Output
invoice numbers: 1001 1002 1003 1006 1007 1010
+------------+----------+        missing_invoice: 1004, 1005, 1008, 1009
|missing_from|missing_to|
+------------+----------+
|        1004|      1005|
|        1008|      1009|
+------------+----------+

At scale, partition the window (for example by branch or by floor(invoice_no / 1_000_000)) so it is not a single task; then handle gaps at bucket borders.

38. Average days between a customer's consecutive orders

Approach: lag gives the previous order date; datediff the gap; then aggregate. The first order has no gap and is ignored by avg.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("customer_id").orderBy("d")
(orders.withColumn("gap", F.datediff("d", F.lag("d").over(w)))
       .groupBy("customer_id")
       .agg(F.round(F.avg("gap"), 1).alias("avg_days_between"), F.max("gap").alias("max_gap"), F.count("*").alias("orders")).show())
Output
+-----------+----------------+-------+------+
|customer_id|avg_days_between|max_gap|orders|
+-----------+----------------+-------+------+
|         c1|             3.0|      4|     3|
|         c2|             3.0|      3|     2|
+-----------+----------------+-------+------+

39. Daily trip cancellation rate, excluding banned riders and drivers

Approach: semi-join trips against the list of allowed users twice (client and driver), then the average of a cancelled flag per day.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
ok = users.filter("banned = 'no'").select("user_id")
t = (trips.join(F.broadcast(ok.withColumnRenamed("user_id", "client")), "client", "left_semi")
          .join(F.broadcast(ok.withColumnRenamed("user_id", "driver")), "driver", "left_semi"))
(t.groupBy("d").agg(F.round(F.avg(F.col("status").startswith("cancelled").cast("int")), 2).alias("cancel_rate"),
                    F.count("*").alias("trips")).show())
Output
+----------+-----------+-----+
|         d|cancel_rate|trips|
+----------+-----------+-----+
|2026-11-01|        0.0|    2|
|2026-11-02|        0.5|    2|
+----------+-----------+-----+

A semi join filters without adding columns or duplicating rows. Broadcasting the small users list avoids shuffling the trips table.

40. First product, latest product and most expensive product per customer

Approach: min_by(value, ordering) and max_by (Spark 3.3+) return the value from the row with the smallest or largest ordering column, without a window.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
purchases.groupBy("customer_id").agg(
    F.min_by("product", "d").alias("first_product"),
    F.max_by("product", "d").alias("latest_product"),
    F.max_by("product", "amount").alias("priciest_product"),
    F.count("*").alias("orders")).show()
Output
+-----------+-------------+--------------+----------------+------+
|customer_id|first_product|latest_product|priciest_product|orders|
+-----------+-------------+--------------+----------------+------+
|         c1|          pen|           bag|             bag|     3|
|         c2|         lamp|          lamp|            lamp|     1|
+-----------+-------------+--------------+----------------+------+

Cheaper than row_number when you only need a few columns: it is a plain aggregation with a map-side combine. Ties are resolved arbitrarily.

Problems 41–47: advanced patterns

Senior rounds pick one or two harder problems to see how you reason: interval merging, sweep-line counting, hierarchies without recursion, running totals with resets, record matching, reconciliation and log parsing. Getting the edge cases and the scale discussion right matters more than speed.

41. Merge overlapping bookings per room

Approach: sort by start time. A booking starts a new merged block when its start is after the largest end seen so far (not just the previous row's end). A running sum of that flag numbers the blocks.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("room").orderBy("s", "e")
m = (bookings
     .withColumn("prev_max_end", F.max("e").over(w.rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, -1)))     # all earlier rows
     .withColumn("new_grp", F.when(F.col("prev_max_end").isNull() | (F.col("s") > F.col("prev_max_end")), 1).otherwise(0))
     .withColumn("grp", F.sum("new_grp").over(w.rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0))))
merged = m.groupBy("room", "grp").agg(F.min("s").alias("start"), F.max("e").alias("end"), F.count("*").alias("bookings"))
Output
R1: 09:00-10:00, 09:30-11:00, 11:00-11:30, 13:00-14:00     R2: 09:00-09:30, 10:00-10:30
+----+---+-----+-----+--------+
|room|grp|start|  end|bookings|
+----+---+-----+-----+--------+
|  R1|  1|09:00|11:30|       3|
|  R1|  2|13:00|14:00|       1|
|  R2|  1|09:00|09:30|       1|
|  R2|  2|10:00|10:30|       1|
+----+---+-----+-----+--------+

Using lag(e) instead of the running max is the common bug: a long booking followed by two short ones inside it would be split. Here touching intervals (end 11:00, start 11:00) merge because the test is >; use >= if they should stay separate.

42. Maximum number of concurrent sessions

Approach: turn each session into two events: +1 at the start and −1 at the end. A running sum over time is the number of open sessions; its maximum is the peak.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
ev = (sessions.select(F.col("s").alias("t"), F.lit(1).alias("delta"))
      .unionByName(sessions.select(F.col("e").alias("t"), F.lit(-1).alias("delta"))))
cc = ev.withColumn("concurrent", F.sum("delta").over(W.orderBy("t", "delta").rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0)))
cc.agg(F.max("concurrent").alias("peak")).show()
Output
sessions: 10:00-10:30, 10:10-10:40, 10:20-10:25, 10:30-11:00, 10:50-11:10
t=10:00 +1 → 1 | 10:10 +1 → 2 | 10:20 +1 → 3 | 10:25 −1 → 2 | 10:30 −1 → 1 | 10:30 +1 → 2 | ...
peak = 3

Ordering by (t, delta) processes the −1 before the +1 at the same instant, so a session ending at 10:30 and one starting at 10:30 do not count as overlapping. For big data, partition the window by day or by server and take the max of maxes.

43. Employee hierarchy: level and reporting path for everyone

Approach: Spark 3.5 has no recursive query, so loop: start from people with no manager (level 0), then repeatedly join employees to the previous level until no new rows appear.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
level = (emp.filter("manager_id IS NULL")
            .select("emp_id", "name", F.lit(0).alias("level"), F.col("name").alias("path")))
result = level
for i in range(1, 20):                                           # safety cap on depth
    level = (emp.alias("e").join(level.alias("m"), F.col("e.manager_id") == F.col("m.emp_id"))
                .select("e.emp_id", "e.name", F.lit(i).alias("level"),
                        F.concat_ws(" > ", "m.path", "e.name").alias("path"))
                .localCheckpoint())                              # cut lineage so the plan does not grow each loop
    if level.isEmpty():
        break
    result = result.unionByName(level)
Output
+------+-----+-----+-----------------------+
|emp_id|name |level|path                   |
+------+-----+-----+-----------------------+
|1     |Asha |0    |Asha                   |
|3     |Meena|1    |Asha > Meena           |
|2     |Ravi |1    |Asha > Ravi            |
|4     |John |2    |Asha > Ravi > John     |
|5     |Li   |3    |Asha > Ravi > John > Li|
|6     |Sara |0    |Sara                   |
|7     |Omar |1    |Sara > Omar            |
+------+-----+-----+-----------------------+

Each loop runs a job (isEmpty), and without the checkpoint the plan gets longer every iteration. Mention the depth cap as protection against cycles in bad data. Spark 4.1 adds recursive CTEs (WITH RECURSIVE) Spark 4 only; for very deep graphs, GraphFrames.

44. Running total that restarts at a reset marker

Approach: a running sum of the reset flag gives every row a segment number. Then compute the running total inside (account, segment).

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("acct").orderBy("seq")
tx2 = (tx.withColumn("grp", F.sum("is_reset").over(w.rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0)))
         .withColumn("running_since_reset",
                     F.sum("amt").over(W.partitionBy("acct", "grp").orderBy("seq")
                                        .rowsBetween(W.unboundedPreceding, 0))))
Output
+----+---+---+--------+---+-------------------+
|acct|seq|amt|is_reset|grp|running_since_reset|
+----+---+---+--------+---+-------------------+
|   a|  1| 40|       0|  0|                 40|
|   a|  2| 50|       0|  0|                 90|
|   a|  3| 30|       0|  0|                120|
|   a|  4| 80|       1|  1|                 80|
|   a|  5| 10|       0|  1|                 90|
|   a|  6| 95|       0|  1|                185|
+----+---+---+--------+---+-------------------+

Follow-up trap: “reset whenever the running total passes 100”. That depends on the previous result, so no window function can express it. Use groupBy("acct").applyInPandas(...) with a simple Python loop per account, and say that each account must fit in memory.

45. Deduplicate near-duplicate customers and keep the most complete row

Approach: build a normalised match key (lower-case, trimmed email), score each row by how many fields are filled, and keep the best row per key with the newest as tie-breaker.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
fields = ["name", "email", "phone"]
k = (cust.withColumn("k", F.lower(F.trim("email")))
         .withColumn("filled", sum(F.col(x).isNotNull().cast("int") for x in fields)))     # Python sum over Columns
w = W.partitionBy("k").orderBy(F.desc("filled"), F.desc("updated"))
(k.withColumn("rn", F.row_number().over(w))
  .withColumn("dupes", F.count("*").over(W.partitionBy("k")))
  .filter("rn = 1")
  .select("id", F.initcap(F.trim("name")).alias("name"), F.col("k").alias("email"), "phone", "dupes").show())
Output
input: (1," Asha K ","ASHA@MAIL.COM",NULL)  (2,"asha k","asha@mail.com","98480")  (3,"Ravi","ravi@mail.com","90000")  (4,"RAVI ","Ravi@Mail.com",NULL)
+---+------+-------------+-----+-----+
| id|  name|        email|phone|dupes|
+---+------+-------------+-----+-----+
|  2|Asha K|asha@mail.com|98480|    2|
|  3|  Ravi|ravi@mail.com|90000|    2|
+---+------+-------------+-----+-----+

A “golden record” would also fill each field from whichever duplicate has it: F.first(col, ignorenulls=True) over the ordered window. True fuzzy matching (typos) needs levenshtein or soundex within blocks, never a full cross join.

46. Reconcile a source table with its target

Approach: full outer join on the key and classify every row: missing in target, extra in target, value mismatch or match. Then summarise.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
r = (src.alias("s").join(tgt.alias("t"), "order_id", "full")
        .select("order_id", F.col("s.amount").alias("src_amount"), F.col("t.amount").alias("tgt_amount"))
        .withColumn("status",
            F.when(F.col("tgt_amount").isNull(), "MISSING_IN_TARGET")
             .when(F.col("src_amount").isNull(), "EXTRA_IN_TARGET")
             .when(F.abs(F.col("src_amount") - F.col("tgt_amount")) > 0.005, "MISMATCH")
             .otherwise("MATCH")))
r.groupBy("status").count().show()
Output
+--------+----------+----------+-----------------+
|order_id|src_amount|tgt_amount|           status|
+--------+----------+----------+-----------------+
|      o1|     100.0|     100.0|            MATCH|
|      o2|      50.0|      55.0|         MISMATCH|
|      o3|      75.0|      NULL|MISSING_IN_TARGET|
|      o4|      NULL|      10.0|  EXTRA_IN_TARGET|
|      o5|      20.0|      20.0|            MATCH|
+--------+----------+----------+-----------------+

This assumes amount is never NULL; with nullable columns, add a marker column on each side (F.lit(1).alias("in_src")) to test presence. For wide tables compare a row hash instead of each column. First step in practice is always cheap: counts and sums per partition on both sides, and the row-level join only for partitions that differ.

47. Parse web server logs and find the endpoints with the most 5xx errors

Approach: one regular expression with capture groups and regexp_extract per field. Lines that do not match give empty strings: count them as bad lines instead of losing them.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
pat = r'^(\S+) \S+ \S+ \[([^\]]+)\] "(\S+) (\S+) [^"]*" (\d{3}) (\d+)'
p = logs.select(
    F.regexp_extract("value", pat, 1).alias("ip"),
    F.to_timestamp(F.regexp_extract("value", pat, 2), "dd/MMM/yyyy:HH:mm:ss Z").alias("ts"),
    F.regexp_extract("value", pat, 3).alias("method"),
    F.regexp_extract("value", pat, 4).alias("path"),
    F.regexp_extract("value", pat, 5).cast("int").alias("status"),
    F.regexp_extract("value", pat, 6).cast("long").alias("bytes"), "value")
good, bad = p.filter("ip != ''").drop("value"), p.filter("ip = ''")

(good.groupBy("path").agg(F.count("*").alias("hits"), F.sum((F.col("status") >= 500).cast("int")).alias("errors_5xx"))
     .withColumn("error_pct", F.round(F.col("errors_5xx") / F.col("hits") * 100, 1))
     .orderBy(F.desc("errors_5xx")).show())
Output
sample line: 10.0.0.2 - - [03/Nov/2026:10:00:02 +0000] "POST /api/pay HTTP/1.1" 500 87
bad lines: 1
+-----------+----+----------+---------+
|       path|hits|errors_5xx|error_pct|
+-----------+----+----------+---------+
|   /api/pay|   2|         2|    100.0|
|/api/orders|   1|         0|      0.0|
|    /health|   1|         0|      0.0|
+-----------+----+----------+---------+

Built-in regex functions run in the JVM; a Python UDF with re would be several times slower. Read with spark.read.text; gzip log files are not splittable, so many medium files beat a few huge ones.

Problems 48–54 and mock rounds

The last set covers schema drift, value streaks, fraud-style comparisons, translating SQL to DataFrame code, calling external services from Spark, rolling distinct counts and dynamic pivots. The lesson ends with timed mock rounds and an index of every problem in the course by pattern.

48. Detect schema drift between two files and union them safely

Approach: compare df.dtypes as dictionaries in plain Python to report added, removed and re-typed columns. Then cast to the agreed type and unionByName(..., allowMissingColumns=True).

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
sa, sb = dict(a.dtypes), dict(b.dtypes)
only_a  = sorted(set(sa) - set(sb))
only_b  = sorted(set(sb) - set(sa))
changed = {c: (sa[c], sb[c]) for c in set(sa) & set(sb) if sa[c] != sb[c]}
print(only_a, only_b, changed)

merged = a.unionByName(b.withColumn("amount", F.col("amount").cast("double")), allowMissingColumns=True)
Output
['name'] ['country'] {'amount': ('double', 'string')}
+---+----+------+-------+
| id|name|amount|country|
+---+----+------+-------+
|  1|   x|   1.5|   NULL|
|  2|NULL|   7.0|     IN|
+---+----+------+-------+

In a pipeline: additive changes (a new nullable column) are logged and allowed; type changes and removed columns fail the job or route the file to quarantine. Plain union matches by position and would silently put the string amount under another column.

49. Longest winning streak per player

Approach: gaps and islands on a value: the difference between a row number over all matches and a row number within the same result is constant inside each streak.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("player").orderBy("match_no")
s = games.withColumn("grp", F.row_number().over(w)
                          - F.row_number().over(W.partitionBy("player", "result").orderBy("match_no")))
(s.filter("result = 'W'")
  .groupBy("player", "grp").agg(F.count("*").alias("streak"))
  .groupBy("player").agg(F.max("streak").alias("longest_win_streak")).show())
Output
p1: W W L W W W    p2: L W
+------+------------------+
|player|longest_win_streak|
+------+------------------+
|    p1|                 3|
|    p2|                 1|
+------+------------------+

Same trick as consecutive login days (problem 9), with “result” playing the role of the calendar.

50. Same card used in two different cities within 10 minutes

Approach: compare each transaction with the previous one on the same card using lag: different city and a time gap of at most 10 minutes.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
w = W.partitionBy("card").orderBy("ts")
(tx.withColumn("prev_city", F.lag("city").over(w))
   .withColumn("mins", (F.col("ts").cast("long") - F.lag("ts").over(w).cast("long")) / 60)
   .filter((F.col("city") != F.col("prev_city")) & (F.col("mins") <= 10)).show())
Output
+-----+-------------------+------+---------+----+
| card|                 ts|  city|prev_city|mins|
+-----+-------------------+------+---------+----+
|card1|2026-11-03 10:06:00|Mumbai|Hyderabad| 6.0|
+-----+-------------------+------+---------+----+

lag only looks at the directly preceding transaction. If any pair within 10 minutes counts, use a range self-join on card with a time condition, bucketed by card and hour to keep it an equi-join. In streaming this is a stateful per-card check with a watermark.

51. Translate SQL to the DataFrame API

Approach: map clause by clause: WHERE → filter before groupBy; GROUP BY + aggregates → groupBy().agg(); HAVING → filter after agg; window in SELECT → .over(Window…).

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
# SQL given by the interviewer
#   WITH t AS (SELECT country, customer_id, SUM(amount) AS spend, COUNT(*) AS n
#              FROM orders WHERE status = 'SHIPPED'
#              GROUP BY country, customer_id HAVING SUM(amount) > 60)
#   SELECT country, customer_id, spend,
#          DENSE_RANK() OVER (PARTITION BY country ORDER BY spend DESC) AS rnk FROM t

t = (orders.filter(F.col("status") == "SHIPPED")                              # WHERE
           .groupBy("country", "customer_id")                                 # GROUP BY
           .agg(F.sum("amount").alias("spend"), F.count("*").alias("n"))
           .filter(F.col("spend") > 60))                                      # HAVING
res = t.select("country", "customer_id", "spend",
               F.dense_rank().over(W.partitionBy("country").orderBy(F.desc("spend"))).alias("rnk"))
Output
both versions returned:
+-------+-----------+-----+---+
|country|customer_id|spend|rnk|
+-------+-----------+-----+---+
|     IN|         c1|400.0|  1|
|     US|         c3|500.0|  1|
|     US|         c4| 90.0|  2|
+-------+-----------+-----+---+

Useful mapping to memorise: CASE WHEN → F.when().otherwise(); IN (subquery) → left semi join; NOT EXISTS → left anti join; UNION → unionByName().distinct(); COALESCE → F.coalesce.

52. Call an external service efficiently, and count problems with an accumulator

Approach: use mapPartitions so the client or connection is created once per partition and rows are sent in batches. An accumulator counts events on executors and is read on the driver.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
def enrich(rows):
    client = make_client()                 # ONE connection per partition, created on the executor
    batch = []
    for r in rows:
        batch.append(r)
        if len(batch) == 500:              # send in batches, not one call per row
            yield from client.lookup(batch); batch = []
    if batch:
        yield from client.lookup(batch)

out = orders.repartition(16).rdd.mapPartitions(enrich).toDF("order_id string, country_name string")

bad = spark.sparkContext.accumulator(0)
def count_bad(rows):
    for r in rows:
        if r.status == "CANCELLED":
            bad.add(1)
orders.foreachPartition(count_bad)         # action: accumulator updates happen exactly once per successful task
print(bad.value)
Output
tested with a dictionary standing in for the client:
o1 India | o2 India | o3 India | o4 United States | o5 United States | o6 United States
cancelled seen: 1

Things to say: the client must be created inside the function (connections cannot be pickled and shipped from the driver); repartition(16) caps parallel connections so you do not overload the service; retries and timeouts belong in the client; a failed task is retried, so the call must be idempotent. Accumulators updated inside transformations can double-count on retries.

53. Rolling 7-day distinct active users

Approach: a window frame cannot do countDistinct. Join each reporting day to the activity rows from its previous 7 days and count distinct users.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
days = activity.select("d").distinct()
(days.alias("x").join(activity.alias("y"),
        (F.col("y.d") <= F.col("x.d")) & (F.col("y.d") > F.date_sub(F.col("x.d"), 7)))       # range join
     .groupBy("x.d").agg(F.countDistinct("y.user_id").alias("active_7d")).show())
Output
activity: 11-01 u1,u2 | 11-03 u1 | 11-05 u3 | 11-09 u2,u4
+----------+---------+
|         d|active_7d|
+----------+---------+
|2026-11-01|        2|
|2026-11-03|        2|
|2026-11-05|        3|
|2026-11-09|        4|
+----------+---------+

The join has no equality key, so Spark uses a nested-loop join: fine when days is tiny and broadcast. The scalable version explodes each activity row into the 7 report dates it belongs to (F.explode(F.sequence(d, date_add(d, 6)))) and does a normal groupBy. Approximate alternative: F.size(F.array_distinct(F.flatten(F.collect_list(...)))) over a range window, or HyperLogLog sketches.

54. Pivot with unknown values and add a row total

Approach: pivot without a value list (accepting the extra job), then build the total by adding the pivoted columns with Python's sum over Column objects.

Show solution
Solutionpyspark
pv = (orders.filter("status = 'SHIPPED'")
            .groupBy("customer_id").pivot("country").agg(F.sum("amount")).fillna(0))
value_cols = [c for c in pv.columns if c != "customer_id"]
pv.withColumn("total", sum(F.col(c) for c in value_cols)).show()
Output
+-----------+-----+-----+-----+
|customer_id|   IN|   US|total|
+-----------+-----+-----+-----+
|         c1|400.0|  0.0|400.0|
|         c2| 50.0|  0.0| 50.0|
|         c3|  0.0|500.0|500.0|
|         c4|  0.0| 90.0| 90.0|
+-----------+-----+-----+-----+

Without a value list Spark first runs a job to find distinct countries, and the output schema changes when a new country appears, which breaks downstream tables. In production, take the list from a reference table.

How to practise

Type each solution from an empty file in a local pyspark shell with no autocomplete, build the sample rows yourself, and say your reasoning out loud. After solving, answer three questions without notes: how many shuffles does it have, where could it skew, and what happens with NULLs?
