Scenario questions with model answers

Senior rounds are mostly scenarios. Use the same shape every time: clarify (size, platform, SLA) → hypothesis → evidence (Spark UI, plan, logs) → fix → prevent. Numbers make your answer believable.

1. A join between a 2 TB fact table and a 40 GB dimension takes 3 hours. Walk me through it.

Evidence: SQL tab shows a SortMergeJoin with two Exchanges; the stage's max task reads 50× the median, so the join key is skewed; reduce tasks spill.

Fixes in order: (1) select only needed columns and filter both sides before the join; 40 GB may shrink to a few GB, which AQE can broadcast at run time or I can broadcast with a raised threshold if executors have memory; (2) find hot keys; NULL or default IDs are filtered or handled separately; (3) confirm AQE split the skewed partitions (skew=true in the final plan), tune the thresholds; (4) salt the hot keys if AQE can't; (5) right-size shuffle partitions: 2 TB / 200 MB ≈ 10,000, or let AQE coalesce from a high start; (6) if this join runs every day, store both tables bucketed on the key in Iceberg and use storage-partitioned joins to remove the shuffle.

2. A Glue job fails with “Container killed … exceeding memory limits” only at month-end.

Month-end data is bigger and probably concentrated on some keys. In the Spark UI from event logs I check whether the same task index fails every attempt (data-driven) or random tasks fail (resource-driven). A container kill means off-heap overhead, so I suspect Python UDFs, Arrow batches or too many cores per executor. Fixes: replace UDFs with built-ins, reduce rows per task (more partitions), fix skew (pre-aggregate, salt), and for that run use a larger or memory-optimised worker (G.2X or R.1X). To prevent it, alarm on Glue observability memory and skew metrics, and make worker type a parameter the workflow can raise at month-end.

3. The daily output has 40,000 small files and Athena is slow.

Files written ≈ write tasks × partition values per task. I fix the writer with repartition("dt") before partitionBy("dt") (or by dt plus a small random bucket for big days), and enable AQE coalescing with parallelismFirst=false and a larger advisory size. I compact existing data with a one-off job or, for Iceberg, rewrite_data_files and the Glue compaction optimizer. Target 256 MB–1 GB files, then compare Athena scanned bytes and runtime before and after.

4. The pipeline wrote duplicate rows after a retry. Why, and how do you stop it?

The job appended output, then failed (or the orchestrator retried after a timeout) before it marked success, and the retry appended again. Bookmarks don't prevent this because they only commit at the end. I make writes idempotent: dynamic partition overwrite for the run date, or an Iceberg MERGE on the business key with a timestamp guard, or write to a run-ID staging location and publish atomically. A uniqueness check before publishing catches anything that slips through.

5. A job is fast in dev and slow in prod with the same code.

Compare: data volume and distribution (prod has hot keys dev doesn't), file counts (prod has small files), Glue version and worker settings, effective configs in the Environment tab, join strategy (a prod dimension above the broadcast threshold flips BHJ to SMJ), catalog partition counts (planning time), and contention (other jobs on the same S3 prefixes or the same source database). I compare the two Spark UIs stage by stage.

6. Reprocess 90 days after a logic bug, safely.

Parameterise the job by date range, run in chunks of a week to bound resources, write to the same partitions with dynamic overwrite or, better, to an Iceberg branch; validate counts and sums against the old output; then publish (fast-forward the branch). Notify downstream consumers, throttle concurrency to protect S3 and source systems, and keep the old snapshot so I can roll back.

7. Upstream added a column and changed amount from int to string. What breaks?

With an explicit schema the new column is ignored and amount becomes NULL in PERMISSIVE mode, or the job fails in FAILFAST. Parquet files with mixed types can throw “column cannot be converted”. I guard with a schema contract check at ingestion (fail or quarantine on breaking changes, allow additive ones), NULL-rate alerts per column, and Iceberg for safe evolution of the curated layer.

8. Design: clickstream JSON lands in S3 every 5 minutes; analysts query it in Athena.

Raw: S3 landing as-is, partitioned by ingest hour. Process: a Glue job with availableNow streaming over the S3 source (or scheduled every 15 minutes via EventBridge and Step Functions), explicit schema, quarantine for bad records, dedup on event_id within a watermark, sessionisation in a daily job. Store: Iceberg tables in the Glue Data Catalog partitioned by days(event_ts), merge-on-read where upserts happen, Glue table optimizers for compaction, snapshot retention and orphan cleanup. Serve: Athena views and pre-aggregated tables for dashboards. Operate: CloudWatch alarms on failures and lateness, DQ checks, SNS alerts, cost tags. I'd quote sizing: events per day, bytes per day, files per day, expected partitions and workers.

9. The driver runs out of memory right at the start, before any task runs.

That's planning-time memory: listing huge numbers of S3 objects or partitions, inferring schema over thousands of JSON files, a gigantic plan from loops, or collecting a broadcast table that is large in memory. Fixes: partition predicates so fewer partitions are listed (Glue catalog partition indexes or Iceberg manifests), explicit schemas, collapsing loops into one select, checkpointing long lineages, disabling the broadcast, and a larger driver (on Glue a larger worker type, since the driver runs on a worker).

10. Cut the cost of a Glue job by half.

First measure DPU-hours and where time goes. Typical wins: auto scaling instead of fixed max workers; Flex for non-urgent jobs; reading less (pruning, Parquet instead of CSV, only needed columns); removing double reads (one action, or cache); replacing UDFs; fixing skew so workers are not idle waiting for one task; compacting small input files; incremental processing instead of full reloads; a sensible timeout so hung runs stop; and, where Spark 4 is acceptable, Glue 6.0's lower DPU price.

11. A workflow of 30 Glue jobs fails randomly at night. Make it robust.

Classify failures from logs: transient (S3 throttling, capacity, JDBC timeouts, concurrent run limits) versus deterministic (data). Add Step Functions retries with exponential backoff only for transient error types, and a Catch that sends an alert with the job run ID. Make every job idempotent so retries are safe. Reduce contention by staggering jobs that write the same table and increasing Iceberg commit retries. Record run metadata (rows in/out, duration) in a control table to spot drift before it fails.

12. Iceberg MERGE jobs are getting slower every week.

Check the table's files and all_delete_files metadata tables: merge-on-read writes delete files, and small files accumulate. Also check whether the MERGE scans the whole table: adding a partition predicate on the target in the ON clause (for example only the last 30 days) limits files read. Fix with compaction including delete files, snapshot expiry, manifest rewrite, and enable Glue table optimizers. Consider a sort order on the merge key to improve file pruning.

13. You must join a 500 GB table to a 300 GB table every day on the same key.

Both sides are too big to broadcast, so a sort-merge join shuffles 800 GB daily. If both tables are written with the same bucketing on the key, the shuffle disappears: with Iceberg, partition both by bucket(256, key) and enable storage-partitioned joins (spark.sql.sources.v2.bucketing.enabled), or use Spark bucketed tables if only Spark reads them. Also pre-filter to the changed partitions only, if the business logic allows incremental joins.

14. Tell me about a hard Spark problem you solved.

Use the story builder: situation with volumes and SLA, the symptom, how you diagnosed it with the Spark UI and plans, what you changed and why you rejected alternatives, and the measured result. Prepare one performance story, one reliability or data-correctness story, and one design story.

More scenarios: AWS pipelines and incidents

These scenarios come from questions reported in recent senior and lead data engineering interviews: pipeline design on AWS, Glue specifics, stuck or wrong jobs, writing to databases, late and duplicate data, migrations and backfills. They continue the numbering of the previous lesson. Answer each aloud in about two minutes before opening it.

15. Design a pipeline that turns 10 TB of S3 log files per day into daily aggregates.

Clarify: format and compression, file sizes, lateness, who reads the result. Design: land raw logs partitioned by date and hour. Convert to Parquet or Iceberg early in an hourly job, so the heavy 10 TB parse happens once and in parallel across hours rather than in one giant daily run. Aggregate from the columnar layer: only needed columns, partial aggregates per hour, then a small daily roll-up. Scale: about 80,000 input partitions of 128 MB per day; EMR or EMR Serverless with auto scaling is usually cheaper than Glue at this volume. Risks: small or gzip files, skew on a hot key such as a bot IP, late files (re-run the affected hour; aggregates are overwritten by partition). Operate: row-count reconciliation raw vs parsed, alarms on lag and bad-line ratio.

16. A lookup table used in a broadcast join keeps growing. What is your plan?

Today it is 50 MB and broadcasts fine. I track its size in the job log and alert at a threshold. As it grows I first prune it (only needed columns and only active rows), then raise the broadcast threshold while executors have memory. Past a few hundred MB I stop broadcasting: sort-merge join with AQE, or bucket both tables on the key. I keep an explicit size check so the job switches strategy instead of failing with a driver out-of-memory error one day.

17. When do Glue job bookmarks fail or give wrong results?

When sources are read with plain spark.read instead of Glue sources with a transformation_ctx; when job.commit() is not reached; when files are overwritten in place with the same name; when files arrive with older modification times than the bookmark; when the JDBC bookmark key is not strictly increasing or rows are updated in place; and when jobs run concurrently on the same bookmark. After a failure the next run re-reads the batch, so the sink must be idempotent. For anything critical I use an explicit high-water mark in a control table or Iceberg incremental reads.

18. Design a daily incremental load from S3 into Redshift.

A Glue job reads the new partition, cleans and deduplicates it, and writes Parquet to a staging prefix. Redshift COPY loads that into a staging table (parallel and far faster than JDBC inserts). In one transaction, MERGE (or delete-then-insert by key) from staging into the target, then truncate staging. The load is keyed by run date so a retry replaces the same data. Step Functions orchestrates Glue then the Redshift Data API call, with alerts. For read-mostly history I would leave data in S3 as Iceberg and query it through Spectrum instead of loading it.

19. Glue, EMR or Lambda for a transformation: how do you decide?

Lambda for small, event-driven work: files up to a few hundred MB, under 15 minutes, no shuffle (validate, route, convert one file). Glue for scheduled Spark ETL from gigabytes to a few terabytes with no cluster to manage. EMR or EMR Serverless when I need full Spark control, very large or long-running jobs, spot pricing, custom libraries or other engines. I also weigh start-up latency, cost per run and what the team can operate.

20. A Spark job hangs: no errors, no progress for an hour.

I open the Spark UI. If a stage shows one running task, it is skew or one huge unsplittable file: check that task's input and shuffle read. If no tasks are running, the driver is busy: listing millions of S3 objects, planning a huge query, a broadcast being collected, or driver-side Python such as a loop or collect; a driver thread dump from the Executors tab shows which. If tasks are waiting, executors are missing: capacity, subnet IPs or auto scaling limits. Other causes: a JDBC query waiting on a database lock, or an S3 commit of very many files. I add a job timeout so a hang cannot burn cost overnight.

21. After someone added cache() the job got slower and executors started dying.

The cached DataFrame is probably large and placed before filters, so Spark stores everything, storage memory squeezes execution memory, tasks spill and garbage collection rises. Or it is cached and used only once. I check the Storage tab for size and fraction cached. Fix: cache after filters and projections, only if reused by several actions, unpersist as soon as it is no longer needed, or drop the cache and re-read Parquet, which is often cheaper.

22. The job succeeded, but the business says the numbers are wrong.

I reproduce with one concrete example (one customer, one day) and trace it through each step, comparing row counts and sums between stages. The usual causes: a join that multiplied rows because of duplicate keys, rows dropped by an inner join or NULL comparisons, a non-deterministic dedup, a time-zone boundary, a partial re-run that appended, late data not included, or an upstream change. I fix the cause, backfill the affected partitions, and add a check that would have caught it: key uniqueness, reconciliation totals against source.

23. You must write 50 million rows from Spark to a relational database or DynamoDB.

A database is the bottleneck, not Spark. For RDS/Aurora I use the JDBC writer with batchsize around 5,000–10,000 and limit partitions to what the database's connections and CPU can take (for example 8–16), writing to a staging table and merging in the database so retries are safe. For Redshift, S3 plus COPY. For DynamoDB, the Glue DynamoDB connector with a write-capacity percentage, or on-demand capacity, and keys designed to avoid hot partitions. I always ask whether the full 50 million must go there or only an aggregate.

24. Data for a day keeps arriving up to three days late. How do you keep daily tables correct?

Separate event date from arrival. Each daily run processes what arrived since the last run and writes it to the partitions of the event dates it contains: with Iceberg, a MERGE or overwritePartitions for the affected dates; with plain Parquet, re-compute and dynamically overwrite the last three event-date partitions. Aggregates are recomputed for those dates. Downstream consumers are told that the last three days are provisional, or reports read through a view that marks them.

25. A Kinesis stream delivers duplicates and out-of-order events. Design the Spark side.

A Glue streaming job (or EMR) reads the stream with a checkpoint in S3. I set a watermark on event time for the tolerated lateness and deduplicate on the event id within the watermark (dropDuplicatesWithinWatermark). The sink is Iceberg: appends for immutable events, or foreachBatch with a MERGE keyed on the id for upserts, which makes replays harmless. I monitor input versus processing rate, state size and iterator age, and run scheduled compaction because streaming creates small files.

26. A file lands in S3 and must be queryable within five minutes.

S3 event → EventBridge → Step Functions (or SQS to batch bursts) → processing. For small files a Lambda converts and commits directly. For larger ones a Glue job; to avoid cold starts and one job per file, I prefer a micro-batch every one to two minutes using availableNow streaming over the landing prefix, which picks up all new files and commits once. Idempotency by file name or checksum in a control table, a dead-letter queue for failures, and compaction afterwards.

27. Design an analytics platform with Glue, Athena and Redshift Spectrum.

S3 in three layers: raw as delivered, cleaned (typed, deduplicated, Iceberg), curated (business models and aggregates). Glue jobs move data between layers, orchestrated by Step Functions or MWAA. The Glue Data Catalog is the single metastore; Lake Formation controls access by table, column and row. Athena serves ad-hoc queries on Iceberg; Redshift holds the hot dimensional model for dashboards and reads colder history through Spectrum. Around it: data quality checks, CloudWatch alarms, CI/CD with infrastructure as code, cost tags, and Iceberg table maintenance.

28. You own 200 Glue 3.0 and 4.0 jobs. Plan the move to Glue 5.1.

Inventory the jobs with their versions, libraries and connectors. Read the migration guide for behaviour changes (Spark 3.1/3.3 → 3.5, Python 3.11, Java 17, dependency versions). Group jobs by pattern and migrate a pilot from each group in dev: run old and new side by side on the same input and compare counts, sums and schema. Fix date-parsing and dependency issues once, in shared code. Roll out in waves from least to most critical with a one-line rollback (the Glue version parameter). Track runtime and cost before and after.

29. A pipeline handles personal data. What do you do in the Spark jobs?

Minimise first: do not carry columns that are not needed. Mask or tokenise in the cleaning layer (sha2 with a secret salt for join keys, partial masking for display), keep the raw copy in a restricted bucket with KMS encryption, and expose only masked data downstream. Access through Lake Formation column and row permissions. No personal values in logs or error messages. Support erasure requests: with Iceberg a DELETE followed by snapshot expiry. Glue's sensitive-data detection can help find unexpected personal data.

30. Backfill two years of history while the daily job keeps running.

Use the same code path with a date-range parameter so logic cannot diverge. Run the backfill as separate, smaller jobs (a month at a time) with limited concurrency, oldest or newest first as the business needs. Keep it away from the daily job's partitions: different dates mean no conflict; with Iceberg, commits on different partitions do not collide, and I raise commit retries. Throttle to protect S3 prefixes and source systems, checkpoint progress in a control table so it can resume, and validate each chunk before moving on.

31. Writes fail with S3 “503 Slow Down”.

Too many requests hit one prefix: thousands of tasks each writing small files, or deleting and listing very many objects. I reduce the number of files (repartition before the write, AQE coalescing, larger target size), spread writes across partition prefixes, compact existing small files, and rely on the built-in retry with backoff. With Iceberg, the object-storage layout option adds a hash to data paths to spread load.

32. You have 300 source tables to ingest. How do you avoid writing 300 jobs?

A metadata-driven framework: one parameterised job and a config table (or YAML) with an entry per source holding the connection, load type (full, incremental by column, CDC), keys, target, schedule and quality rules. The orchestrator fans out over the config with controlled concurrency. Adding a table is a config change with no new code. I add per-table audit records (rows read and written, duration, status) and let exceptions have custom steps, so the framework does not become a tangle of special cases.

33. Source and target row counts do not match after a load.

First check whether they should match: filters, deduplication and rejected records legitimately change counts, so I reconcile as source = loaded + rejected + filtered. Then compare counts per partition or per day to find where they differ, and for that slice do a key-level full outer join to list missing and extra keys. Typical causes: duplicates removed or created by a join, NULL keys, a time-zone boundary putting rows in another day, a partial load, or the source still changing while it was read.

Rapid-fire questions

Expect 10 to 15 of these in the first quarter of a senior round. Answer each in two to four sentences. The answers below are written to be said out loud.

cache() vs persist()?

cache() is persist() with the default level, MEMORY_AND_DISK for DataFrames. persist() lets you choose the storage level. Both are lazy.

repartition vs coalesce?

repartition does a full shuffle, can increase or decrease, and gives even partitions. coalesce avoids a shuffle by merging partitions, can only decrease, may be uneven, and reduces the parallelism of the upstream stage it is pipelined with.

Narrow vs wide transformation?

Narrow: each output partition depends on one input partition (filter, select, withColumn); no shuffle. Wide: an output partition needs data from many input partitions (groupBy, join, distinct, orderBy); requires a shuffle and a new stage.

What triggers a new stage?

A shuffle dependency, i.e. a wide transformation such as a non-broadcast join, groupBy, distinct, orderBy, repartition or a window with partitionBy.

How many tasks will a stage have?

One per partition: about input size / 128 MB for a file scan, spark.sql.shuffle.partitions after a shuffle (adjusted by AQE), or n after repartition(n).

What is lineage?

The chain of transformations that produced a dataset. Spark uses it to recompute lost partitions instead of replicating data. Very long lineage slows planning and recovery; checkpointing cuts it.

Broadcast variable vs broadcast join?

A broadcast variable ships a read-only object (like a lookup dict) to each executor once. A broadcast join ships a small table's hash table to each executor so the big table is joined without a shuffle. Same mechanism, different API.

What are accumulators?

Counters that executors add to and the driver reads. Updates inside transformations can be counted twice on task retries; only updates inside actions are guaranteed once.

Why is the default of 200 shuffle partitions often wrong?

It ignores data size: 200 partitions of 5 GB spill and fail; 200 partitions of 1 MB waste scheduling and write tiny files. Size from the shuffle volume, or set a high start and let AQE coalesce.

What does AQE do?

Re-optimises at each shuffle boundary using real statistics: coalesces small partitions, switches sort-merge joins to broadcast or shuffled hash joins, and splits skewed join partitions.

Catalyst vs Tungsten?

Catalyst optimises the query plan (analysis, rule-based and cost-based optimisation, physical planning). Tungsten executes efficiently: binary row format, memory management, whole-stage code generation.

How does Spark recover from a failed task?

It retries the task up to four times, possibly on another executor. Lost executors' tasks are re-run, lost cached partitions recomputed, and lost shuffle output triggers re-running the parent stage for the missing partitions. After repeated failures the job aborts.

What is speculative execution?

Launching duplicate copies of unusually slow tasks and using whichever finishes first. It helps with bad nodes, not skew, and needs idempotent output.

Dynamic allocation?

Spark adds executors when tasks are waiting and removes idle ones. It needs the external shuffle service or shuffle tracking so removed executors' shuffle files stay available.

map vs mapPartitions?

map runs per row; mapPartitions runs once per partition with an iterator, so expensive setup like a database connection happens once per partition.

reduceByKey vs groupByKey?

reduceByKey combines values on the map side before shuffling; groupByKey shuffles every value and builds a list per key, which is slower and can run out of memory.

union vs unionByName?

union matches columns by position, a source of silent bugs; unionByName matches by name and with allowMissingColumns=True fills missing columns with NULL. Neither removes duplicates.

Client vs cluster deploy mode?

Client: the driver runs where you submitted, logs in your terminal, dies if you disconnect. Cluster: the driver runs inside the cluster; used for production.

Why prefer built-in functions over UDFs?

They run in the JVM inside generated code, Catalyst can optimise around them, and they handle NULLs consistently. Python UDFs serialise every row to a Python worker.

Temp view vs global temp view vs table?

A temp view is a session-scoped name for a plan. A global temp view is shared by sessions in one application under global_temp. A table is registered in a catalog such as Glue with data in storage and is visible to other engines.

How does Spark read Parquet efficiently?

It reads the footer, skips row groups using min/max statistics for pushed filters, reads only the needed column chunks and decodes them in vectorised batches.

toPandas() on 50 GB?

Don't: it loads everything into driver memory. Aggregate or sample in Spark first, or use applyInPandas or the pandas API on Spark for distributed pandas logic.

Static vs dynamic partition overwrite?

Static (the default) deletes all partitions under the target path before writing. Dynamic replaces only the partitions present in the DataFrame. Set dynamic for idempotent reloads.

What is a skipped stage?

A stage whose shuffle output already exists from an earlier job in the same application, so Spark reuses it.

How do you test PySpark code?

Pure DataFrame-in, DataFrame-out functions; pytest with a session-scoped local SparkSession; small hand-built inputs with edge cases; assertDataFrameEqual from pyspark.testing; integration tests on sample data in a dev Glue environment; data-quality checks in production.

What is the difference between partitionBy on a window and on a writer?

On a window it defines groups of rows for the calculation and causes a shuffle. On a writer it defines the folder layout on storage. Same word, unrelated features.

What changes in Spark 4 that could break a job?

ANSI mode on by default (invalid casts, overflow and divide-by-zero raise errors), Java 17 and Scala 2.13 only, and Python 3.8 dropped. Most DataFrame code is unchanged.

Glue DynamicFrame vs DataFrame?

DynamicFrames have per-record schemas with choice types and integrate with bookmarks and Glue connectors; DataFrames have fixed schemas and the full optimised Spark API. Common practice: read and write with DynamicFrames where useful, transform as DataFrames.

Question bank: basics

Even senior interviews open with fundamentals to check that your base is solid. Answer in two or three clear sentences. These add to the rapid-fire list and do not repeat it.

What is PySpark, and when would you use it instead of pandas?

PySpark is the Python API for Apache Spark, a distributed engine. pandas runs on one machine and must fit the data in its memory. I use pandas for data up to a few GB on one machine and PySpark when data is larger than memory, lives in a data lake, or the job must run reliably on a cluster such as Glue or EMR. For small data Spark is slower than pandas because of its start-up and scheduling overhead.

SparkSession vs SparkContext?

SparkContext is the original connection to the cluster and the entry point for RDDs. SparkSession, introduced in Spark 2.0, wraps it and adds the DataFrame and SQL entry points, configuration and the catalog. You create one SparkSession and reach the context through spark.sparkContext when you need low-level features such as accumulators or the log level.

In how many ways can you create a DataFrame?

From files or tables with spark.read (Parquet, CSV, JSON, JDBC, catalog tables); from Python data with spark.createDataFrame(list_or_pandas_df, schema); from an RDD with toDF; with spark.range(n); from a SQL query with spark.sql; and as the result of transforming another DataFrame.

What is the difference between a transformation and an action?

A transformation (select, filter, join, groupBy) returns a new DataFrame and only extends the plan. An action (count, show, collect, write) asks for a result and makes Spark optimise and run the plan as a job. Nothing is computed until an action runs.

select vs withColumn vs filter?

select chooses or computes the output columns. withColumn adds or replaces one column and keeps the rest. filter (same as where) keeps rows that satisfy a condition. For many new columns, one select or withColumns is better than a chain of withColumn calls.

show vs collect vs take vs first?

show(n) prints the first rows. take(n) and first() return a few rows to the driver as Row objects. collect() returns every row to the driver, so it is only safe on small results; on big data it crashes the driver with an out-of-memory error.

How do you turn a DataFrame column into a Python list?

[r[0] for r in df.select("country").distinct().collect()]. It is only acceptable when the result is small, for example a list of distinct countries to pass to pivot or isin. For a large set, keep it as a DataFrame and join.

count(*) vs count(col) vs countDistinct?

count("*") counts rows. count("col") counts rows where that column is not NULL. countDistinct("col") counts different non-NULL values and is expensive on big data; approx_count_distinct is the cheap approximate version.

distinct vs dropDuplicates?

distinct() removes rows identical in all columns. dropDuplicates([cols]) keeps one row per combination of the listed columns, but which row survives is not defined. To keep a specific row, such as the latest, use row_number over an ordered window.

How do you handle missing values?

Find them with isNull() and a per-column null count. Remove rows with na.drop(subset=[...]), fill defaults with na.fill({col: value}), pick the first available value with coalesce, or replace placeholder values such as "N/A" with na.replace. Which one depends on the meaning: I never fill a key or an amount silently.

How do you rename columns and change types?

withColumnRenamed(old, new) for one, withColumnsRenamed({...}) or toDF(*names) for many, and alias inside select. Types change with F.col("c").cast("decimal(12,2)"); use try_cast when bad values should become NULL instead of failing under ANSI mode.

Which join types does PySpark support?

inner, left, right, full outer, left_semi (rows of the left that have a match, left columns only), left_anti (rows of the left with no match) and cross. Semi and anti joins are the DataFrame forms of EXISTS and NOT EXISTS.

What is a partition?

A chunk of the DataFrame's rows that one task processes on one core. The number of partitions sets the parallelism; their size sets how much memory each task needs. A good size is 100–200 MB.

orderBy vs sortWithinPartitions?

orderBy (same as sort) produces a total order across the whole DataFrame, which needs a range shuffle. sortWithinPartitions sorts rows inside each partition without a shuffle; it is used before writing files so Parquet statistics are tight.

What is a UDF and what does it cost?

A user-defined function is custom Python logic applied per row. It costs speed, because each row is serialised to a Python worker and back, and optimisation, because Catalyst cannot see inside it. Prefer built-in functions; if custom logic is unavoidable, use a pandas UDF, which works on Arrow batches.

How do you read a CSV file from S3 with a schema?

spark.read.schema(schema).option("header", "true").option("mode", "PERMISSIVE").csv("s3://bucket/path/"). I pass an explicit schema instead of inferSchema, because inference reads the data an extra time and can guess wrong types, and I capture bad rows with a corrupt-record column.

What are the save modes when writing?

errorifexists (the default), append, overwrite and ignore. With partitioned output, overwrite replaces the whole path unless spark.sql.sources.partitionOverwriteMode is dynamic.

explode vs explode_outer vs posexplode?

explode creates one row per array element and drops rows whose array is empty or NULL. explode_outer keeps those rows with a NULL element. posexplode also returns the element's position.

limit vs sample?

limit(n) returns n rows, usually from the first partitions, so it is not random. sample(fraction, seed) returns an approximately random fraction of all rows and is what you use to test logic on representative data.

What does spark-submit do?

It launches a Spark application on a cluster: it starts the driver (on your machine in client mode or inside the cluster in cluster mode), passes configuration such as executor memory and cores, ships your Python files and dependencies, and runs your script. Glue and EMR Serverless run the equivalent for you.

Question bank: intermediate

The middle of a PySpark round: how Spark executes your code and how you use the API well. Each answer is written the way you would say it.

What happens internally when you call df.count()?

The driver takes the logical plan, Catalyst analyses and optimises it, and the planner produces a physical plan. Because count needs no column values, Spark prunes all columns and can answer from Parquet footers for a plain table. The DAG scheduler splits the plan into stages, each task counts its partition, the partial counts are shuffled to one partition, summed, and one number returns to the driver.

What do the DAG scheduler and task scheduler do?

The DAG scheduler turns a job into stages by cutting the plan at shuffles and submits stages in dependency order, re-submitting them if shuffle data is lost. The task scheduler takes each stage's tasks, assigns them to free executor cores with locality preference, and handles task retries and speculation.

Which storage levels exist and when do you use them?

MEMORY_AND_DISK is the DataFrame default and the right choice almost always. MEMORY_ONLY recomputes what does not fit. DISK_ONLY is for large intermediate results that are expensive to recompute. The _2 variants replicate for faster recovery. I cache only data reused by several actions and unpersist it afterwards.

cache vs checkpoint?

Cache keeps the data on executors and keeps the lineage, so lost partitions are recomputed. Checkpoint writes the data to reliable storage and cuts the lineage, which is useful when the plan has grown very long in a loop. localCheckpoint cuts lineage using executor storage: faster, not fault tolerant.

How does Spark serialise data?

DataFrame rows use Tungsten's compact binary format, so they are shuffled and cached without Java object overhead. RDDs of JVM objects use Java serialisation by default, or Kryo if configured. Data crossing to Python uses pickle for plain UDFs and Apache Arrow for pandas UDFs and toPandas. Closures sent to executors are serialised too, which is why they cannot contain connections.

foreach vs foreachPartition? How do you write to an external system?

foreach calls your function per row; foreachPartition once per partition with an iterator, so you open one connection per partition and write in batches. For databases I prefer the JDBC writer with batchsize and a controlled numPartitions. Writes must be idempotent (upserts keyed on a business key), because tasks are retried.

How would you join a 500 GB table with a 50 MB lookup table?

Broadcast the lookup: big.join(F.broadcast(small), key). The small table is sent to every executor as a hash table and the 500 GB table is joined without being shuffled. I select only the needed lookup columns first, and check that 50 MB on disk is not hundreds of MB in memory.

When is a cross join legitimate?

When you really need every combination of two small sets: a calendar × stores grid to fill missing days, all product pairs for similarity, or generating salt values. On large inputs it explodes, so it must be deliberate and written as crossJoin.

How do you count distinct values efficiently at scale?

If an approximation is acceptable, approx_count_distinct (HyperLogLog) in a single pass. For exact counts, pre-aggregate to distinct (group, value) pairs first and then count, and avoid several different countDistinct in one query because Spark expands the data for each.

How many partitions should a job have?

Enough that each holds 100–200 MB and at least two to three times the number of cores. For a shuffle of 300 GB that is about 1,500–3,000. With AQE I set a generous number and let it merge small partitions.

What is predicate pushdown and why does Parquet help?

Spark sends filters to the file reader. Parquet stores min/max statistics per row group in its footer, so the reader skips row groups that cannot match, and being columnar it reads only the needed columns. CSV and JSON have no statistics, so every byte is read.

What is executor memory overhead?

Memory outside the JVM heap that the executor's container may use: Python worker processes, Arrow buffers, network buffers and thread stacks. By default it is the larger of 384 MB and 10% of executor memory. Exceeding it gets the container killed by YARN, typically in jobs with Python UDFs.

What types of pandas UDFs are there?

Series to Series (a vectorised column function), Iterator of Series to Iterator of Series (lets you load a model once per task), Series to scalar (a grouped aggregate), and the function APIs applyInPandas for grouped map, mapInPandas, and cogrouped map.

How do you tune JDBC reads and writes?

Reads: partitionColumn, lowerBound, upperBound and numPartitions for parallel range queries, fetchsize for rows per round trip, and a pushdown query in dbtable/query to filter in the database. Writes: batchsize, a limited number of partitions so the database is not overloaded, and for big loads into Redshift, write to S3 and COPY instead of row inserts.

Window function or groupBy plus join?

A window keeps all rows and adds the group value in one shuffle. GroupBy plus a join back needs an aggregation and a join. Windows are simpler and usually cheaper, but they must sort each partition; for a single aggregate on a very skewed key, aggregate first (map-side combine) and broadcast-join the small result back.

What is dynamic partition pruning?

When a partitioned fact table joins a filtered dimension, Spark first evaluates the dimension filter, collects the matching partition values, and uses them at run time to read only those fact partitions. It shows as dynamicpruningexpression in the plan.

Partitioning vs bucketing?

Partitioning splits data into folders by column value and enables skipping folders on filters; use it for low-cardinality filter columns like date. Bucketing hashes rows into a fixed number of files by key, which lets joins and aggregations on that key avoid a shuffle; use it for high-cardinality join keys.

How do NULLs behave in joins and aggregations?

NULL keys never match in equi-joins (use eqNullSafe if they should). Aggregates such as sum, avg and count(col) skip NULLs; count(*) does not. In groupBy, NULL forms its own group. In sorting, NULLs come first ascending by default.

Sort-merge join vs shuffled hash join?

Both shuffle both sides by the key. Sort-merge then sorts each partition and merges; it handles any size because sorting can spill. Shuffled hash builds a hash table from the smaller side per partition and skips the sort; it is faster when the build side fits in memory but can run out of memory if it does not.

How does explain() help you optimise?

It shows what Spark will actually do: whether partition filters and pushed filters are applied, which columns are read, the join strategy, and every Exchange (shuffle). I use it to confirm pruning, to spot unexpected sort-merge or nested-loop joins and Python UDF nodes, and after a run to see what AQE changed.

Question bank: advanced

Questions that separate senior candidates: execution details, streaming guarantees, failure behaviour, AWS-specific judgement and Spark 4 awareness.

Does data have to fit in memory for Spark to process it?

No. Spark processes one partition per task and streams rows through operators. Sorts, aggregations and joins that exceed execution memory spill to local disk. What must fit is a single partition's working set per task, a broadcast table, and anything you collect to the driver. That is why partition sizing and skew matter more than total data size.

How do you control partitioning without a custom partitioner?

In the DataFrame API I repartition by an expression: repartition(n, key) for hash, repartitionByRange for sorted ranges, or by a derived column such as a salted key or pmod(hash(key), n). A true custom partitioner exists only on pair RDDs (rdd.partitionBy(n, func)) and is rarely needed.

How does Spark choose a join strategy?

For equi-joins it checks hints first, then whether a side is below the broadcast threshold (broadcast hash join), then whether a shuffled hash join is allowed and one side is much smaller, otherwise sort-merge join. Non-equi joins use broadcast nested loop if a side is small, otherwise a Cartesian product. AQE re-checks the choice with real sizes after shuffles.

What is the cost-based optimiser and when does it matter?

With spark.sql.cbo.enabled and statistics from ANALYZE TABLE ... COMPUTE STATISTICS FOR COLUMNS, Spark estimates row counts after filters and joins and can reorder multi-way joins. Without statistics it only knows file sizes. On AWS lakes statistics are often missing, so AQE's runtime statistics do most of the work; Iceberg supplies file-level statistics itself.

When does whole-stage code generation not apply?

Around Python UDFs, some complex expressions, and when the generated method would be too large (very wide tables or huge CASE expressions), in which case Spark falls back to interpreting operators. In the plan, operators without the *(n) prefix are not fused.

How do you get exactly-once results in Structured Streaming?

Three parts together: a replayable source such as Kafka or Kinesis offsets, a checkpoint that records which offsets each micro-batch covered, and an idempotent or transactional sink. With Iceberg the commit is transactional; with foreachBatch I use a keyed MERGE or record the batch id so a replayed batch has no extra effect.

What makes streaming state grow without bound, and how do you stop it?

Stateful operations (aggregations, dedup, stream-stream joins) keep state per key. Without a watermark Spark can never drop old keys. I set a watermark on event time, add time-range conditions to stream-stream joins, use dropDuplicatesWithinWatermark, monitor state rows in the Streaming tab, and use the RocksDB state store for large state.

How do spot interruptions affect a Spark job on EMR?

A reclaimed node takes its executors and their shuffle files with it. Running tasks are retried, and stages whose shuffle output was lost are partly re-run after fetch failures. I keep core nodes on-demand, put only task nodes on spot with diverse instance types, and enable graceful decommissioning so blocks are migrated before the node disappears.

Why can speculation be dangerous with S3 writes?

Two attempts of the same task may both write output. With a committer that renames files this could leave duplicates or partial files; non-idempotent side effects (database inserts, API calls) run twice. With the EMRFS S3-optimised committer or Iceberg, only the winning attempt's files are committed, so file output is safe; side effects are still your responsibility.

How do you recognise and reduce garbage-collection problems?

The Executors tab shows GC time in red when it exceeds about 10% of task time, and tasks slow down or time out on heartbeats. Causes are large partitions, many Python or JVM objects, and too many cores sharing a heap. I make partitions smaller, replace UDFs and RDD code with built-ins, reduce cores per executor or add memory; G1GC is already the default on Java 17.

A single 100 GB gzip CSV arrives daily. How do you process it?

Gzip is not splittable, so Spark reads it with one task. Best fix is upstream: deliver many smaller files, or bzip2/zstd-splittable or Parquet. If I cannot change the source, I read it once with one task, immediately repartition to spread the rows, and write Parquet to a staging area; all later steps use that. I size that executor's disk and memory for the single long task.

How do you design a Spark job to be idempotent?

The run date is a parameter, never the wall clock. Output for a run goes to deterministic locations and replaces what was there (dynamic partition overwrite) or is merged by business key. Surrogate keys and hashes are deterministic. Side effects such as notifications or control-table updates happen only after the data commit succeeds.

What is Spark Connect?

A client-server mode: a thin client (Python without a local JVM) sends unresolved plans over gRPC to a Spark server that executes them. It separates the application from the cluster, improves stability and allows upgrades of each side independently. It matured in Spark 4; some RDD and SparkContext APIs are not available through it.

Are Python UDFs still slow in Spark 4?

They are faster but not free. Spark 3.5 added Arrow-optimised Python UDFs as an option, Spark 4 improved them and added Arrow-native UDFs and UDTFs, which cut serialisation cost. Catalyst still cannot optimise through a UDF, so built-in functions remain the first choice.

Glue Data Catalog vs Hive metastore?

The Glue Data Catalog is a managed, serverless metastore that is Hive-compatible: it stores databases, tables, partitions and schemas. It is shared by Glue, Athena, EMR and Redshift Spectrum, integrates with IAM and Lake Formation, and needs no database to operate. A Hive metastore is self-managed, usually on RDS, and is only needed for specific compatibility cases.

Would you run Glue crawlers in production?

For discovering unknown data and for quick starts, yes. For production tables I prefer explicit DDL or schema managed in code, because a crawler can change a table's schema or create unwanted tables when a bad file lands, and it adds cost and delay. Iceberg tables need no crawler: the writer commits the metadata. If I use crawlers, I restrict them to add partitions only.

How does Lake Formation fit with Glue jobs?

Lake Formation adds database-style grants on catalog tables: table, column, row and cell level. A Glue job's role needs the grants as well as IAM. Glue 5.x enforces fine-grained access natively in Spark for DataFrames and SQL, so a job only sees the columns and rows its role is allowed to read.

Delta Lake vs Iceberg: what would you say?

Both add ACID transactions, MERGE, time travel and schema evolution over Parquet. Delta keeps a JSON transaction log in _delta_log and is deepest on Databricks; Iceberg uses a metadata and manifest tree with a catalog pointer, hidden partitioning and partition evolution, and has the broadest AWS support (Athena, Glue, EMR, Redshift, S3 Tables). I choose by the engines that must read and write the table.

What are the limits of AQE?

It only acts at shuffle boundaries, so it cannot help the scan stage. Skew handling covers joins, and for outer joins only the preserved side; not aggregations or windows. It relies on thresholds (5× median and 256 MB) that may not fit the data. It does not apply to streaming queries.

How would you size a Glue job for 1 TB of daily input?

Start from the data: 1 TB of Parquet is roughly 8,000 partitions of 128 MB. G.1X has 4 cores, so 50 workers give about 200 task slots and 40 waves of tasks. I begin with G.1X, auto scaling on with a maximum around 50–100, run once, and read the Spark UI: spill or OOM means move to G.2X; idle executors mean lower the maximum; one slow task means skew, which more workers will not fix.

Lead-level and experience questions

For senior and lead roles, part of the round is about how you work: reviewing code, sizing, setting standards, handling incidents and people. There is no single right answer; these are sound structures to adapt with examples from your own projects.

How do you review a PySpark pull request?

Correctness first: join types and key uniqueness, NULL handling, deterministic dedup, time zones, idempotent writes with the right overwrite mode. Then efficiency: filters and column selection early, no collect or toPandas on large data, no avoidable UDFs, no repeated actions without a cache, sensible repartitioning before writes. Then maintainability: transformations as testable functions, explicit schemas, parameters instead of hard-coded paths and dates, tests with edge cases, logging of counts. I ask for the explain plan of any new heavy join.

How do you estimate the cluster for a new pipeline?

From the data: daily volume, format and compression give the partition count at 128 MB each. The heaviest shuffle (largest join or aggregation) gives the memory needed per task. I pick a target runtime, derive the cores needed, then run on a representative sample at 10% and at full size, and read the Spark UI for spill, GC and idle time before fixing the size. I state the assumptions and revisit after a week of production runs.

What standards do you set for your team's Spark jobs?

Explicit schemas at ingestion; idempotent, date-parameterised jobs; dynamic partition overwrite or MERGE, never blind append; UTC session time zone; built-in functions before UDFs; a shared library for common logic such as SCD2 and data-quality checks; unit tests in CI; event logs and metrics enabled on every job; target file sizes and table maintenance schedules; and a short runbook for each pipeline.

Describe how you handle a production incident.

Stabilise first: stop the damage (pause downstream loads, prevent bad data from publishing), tell stakeholders what is affected and when the next update comes. Diagnose with evidence: orchestrator error, logs, Spark UI. Restore service with the safest fix or a rollback, then re-run and validate the affected dates. Afterwards a blameless review: timeline, root cause, what detection was missing, and concrete actions such as a new check or alarm.

You disagree with an architect's design. What do you do?

I make sure I understand their constraints, then put the disagreement in measurable terms: cost per run, runtime, operational effort, risk. If it is cheap, I run a small proof of concept on real data so we compare numbers instead of opinions. I present options with trade-offs, and once a decision is made I commit to it and document the reasoning.

A junior engineer keeps using collect() and Python UDFs. How do you coach them?

I show the effect instead of quoting a rule: we open the Spark UI on their job, look at the driver memory and the Python evaluation node, then rewrite one case with built-in functions and compare runtimes. I pair on the next task, point them to the internals behind it, and add the pattern to the review checklist so the feedback is about the code, not the person.

How do you balance delivery pressure against technical debt?

I separate debt that risks correctness or operability (no idempotency, no tests on core logic, no monitoring) from cosmetic debt. The first kind is part of “done”. For the rest I record it with its cost, agree a date, and reserve a fixed share of each sprint for it. When I take a shortcut for a deadline, the business knows the trade-off and the follow-up is scheduled.

How do you agree a data contract with an upstream team?

We write down the schema with types and nullability, keys and their uniqueness, delivery time and frequency, how changes and deletes are represented, volume expectations, and how breaking changes are announced. I enforce it at ingestion with schema and quality checks that fail fast or quarantine, and share a dashboard of violations so problems are fixed at the source.

What do you monitor for a pipeline, and what are your SLAs?

Freshness (data available by an agreed time), completeness (row counts and reconciliation against source), correctness (quality rule pass rates, NULL and duplicate rates), and health (run duration trend, failure and retry counts, cost per run, small-file and snapshot counts for Iceberg). Alerts go to the on-call channel with the job run id and a runbook link, and are tuned so they are acted on.

How do you control cost across many Spark jobs?

Make cost visible first: tags per pipeline, DPU-hours or vCPU-hours per run tracked over time. Then the standard levers: auto scaling and right-sized workers, Flex or spot for non-urgent work, incremental processing instead of full reloads, fixing skew and small files, timeouts, and retiring unused pipelines. I review the top ten jobs by cost each month, because they are usually most of the bill.

What would you redesign in your current pipeline if you could?

Have an honest, specific answer ready from your own project: for example moving a full daily reload to incremental MERGE, replacing a crawler-managed table with Iceberg, or adding reconciliation that is missing today. Explain why it was built the way it was, what it costs now, and how you would migrate safely. Interviewers use this question to see whether you think critically about your own work.

How do you explain a technical trade-off to a non-technical stakeholder?

In their terms: time, money and risk. For example: “Option A delivers the report by 6 a.m. and costs about X more per month; option B is cheaper but the report arrives at 9 a.m. on heavy days.” I give a recommendation, the main risk, and what I need from them to proceed.

Project story builder

The project deep-dive decides most senior interviews. The panel wants to hear that you made engineering decisions, with numbers, and that you understand the trade-offs. Prepare three stories using the template below and practise each until it takes about two minutes.

PartWhat to sayExample phrasing
ContextBusiness purpose, sources, volume, frequency, consumers, SLA“A daily pipeline loading about 40 million order events (120 GB of JSON) from S3 into curated Iceberg tables for finance dashboards in Athena, due by 6 a.m.”
ArchitectureServices and why“EventBridge triggers Step Functions; Glue 5.1 jobs on G.1X with auto scaling; Iceberg in the Glue Catalog; Athena; SNS alerts.”
ProblemThe symptom, measured“Runtime grew from 35 minutes to 2.5 hours over a quarter and missed the SLA twice.”
DiagnosisHow you found the cause: Spark UI, plans, metadata“The History Server showed one join stage with max task time 60× the median; the hot key was a default merchant ID used for 30% of rows.”
DecisionOptions considered, why you chose one“Broadcast wasn't possible at 25 GB; AQE couldn't split it because the skew was on the right side of a left outer join; I salted only the default key and isolated it.”
ResultNumbers“Back to 28 minutes, 40% fewer DPU-hours, no SLA misses since.”
PreventionWhat you changed so it can't recur“Added a skew check on top keys, CloudWatch alarms on runtime, and scheduled Iceberg compaction.”

Questions panels ask about your project

  • How much data per run, how many files, how many partitions? What worker type and count, and why?
  • How do you handle late or duplicate data? Is the job idempotent?
  • How do you handle schema changes from the source?
  • What happens when the job fails at 3 a.m.? Who is alerted, what do they look at?
  • How do you test before deploying? How is it deployed?
  • What would you change if data grew 10×?
  • What does it cost per run, and how did you reduce it?
Prepare these three
  1. A performance story (skew, small files, join strategy, a memory problem).
  2. A reliability or data-correctness story (duplicates, idempotency, schema drift, late data).
  3. A design story (why Iceberg, why Glue vs EMR, how you structured layers).

One-page revision sheet

Execution

  • Action → job → stages (cut at shuffles) → tasks (1 per partition)
  • Narrow = same stage; wide = shuffle
  • Lazy: plan first, run on action; re-runs per action unless cached
  • Driver plans; executors run; driver death = job death

Partitions

  • 100–200 MB each; 2–3× cores
  • Read: 128 MB maxPartitionBytes; gzip = 1 task/file
  • Shuffle: 200 default; AQE coalesces
  • coalesce = no shuffle but shrinks upstream

Joins

  • BHJ: small side < 10 MB est. → no shuffle
  • SMJ: 2 shuffles + 2 sorts
  • SHJ: shuffle, hash per partition
  • BNLJ / Cartesian = missing equality key

AQE

  • Coalesce → 64 MB advisory (set parallelismFirst=false)
  • SMJ → BHJ at run time
  • Skew split: > 5× median AND > 256 MB, joins only

Memory

  • Heap: 300 MB reserved, 60% unified (storage ↔ execution), 40% user
  • Overhead: max(384 MB, 10%), Python lives here
  • Heap OOM → smaller tasks/skew; YARN kill → overhead

Spark UI

  • Jobs → Stages → SQL → Executors
  • Max ≫ median = skew
  • Few long tasks = under-partitioned
  • Tiny tasks + delay = small files
  • High GC + spill = memory pressure

Logs

  • Glue 5.x: /aws-glue/jobs/error, /output; streams <runId>-driver / -N
  • EMR: S3 log URI containers/…/stderr.gz
  • Python line = action; same task × 4 = data; last Caused by = cause

Writes

  • partitionOverwriteMode=dynamic
  • repartition(col) before partitionBy(col)
  • 128 MB–1 GB files
  • Idempotent: overwrite partition or MERGE

Iceberg

  • Catalog → metadata.json → manifest list → manifests → data
  • Atomic pointer swap; snapshots; time travel
  • COW slow write/fast read; MOR opposite
  • Maintain: compact → expire → manifests → orphans

SCD

  • Type 1 overwrite, 2 history rows, 3 previous column
  • SCD2 row: sk, natural key, valid_from, valid_to (half-open), is_current, row_hash
  • Iceberg: one MERGE with NULL merge key
  • Late data: rebuild chain with lag/lead

Versions

  • Glue 5.1 = Spark 3.5.6; Glue 6.0 = Spark 4.1
  • EMR 7.14 = Spark 3.5.8
  • Spark 4: ANSI on, VARIANT, Connect, Python data sources

Final self-check quiz

28 questions. Pick an answer to see the explanation. Aim for 24 or more before your interview.

Glossary A–Z

Action
An operation that returns a result or writes data (count, show, write), which triggers a job.
AQE (Adaptive Query Execution)
Re-optimisation of the remaining query after each shuffle using real data sizes.
Broadcast join
Join where the small table is copied to every executor, so the big table is not shuffled.
Bucketing
Storing a table pre-hashed by a key into a fixed number of buckets to avoid shuffles in later joins.
Catalog
Registry of databases and tables; on AWS, the Glue Data Catalog.
Catalyst
Spark's query optimiser.
Checkpoint (batch)
Saving a DataFrame to reliable storage to cut its lineage.
Checkpoint (streaming)
Folder storing offsets, state and commits so a stream can resume exactly where it stopped.
Coalesce
Reduce the number of partitions without a shuffle.
Compaction
Rewriting many small files into fewer large ones (Iceberg rewrite_data_files).
Copy-on-write / Merge-on-read
Iceberg strategies for row changes: rewrite data files, or write delete files and merge at read time.
DAG
Directed acyclic graph: the plan of stages and their dependencies.
DataFrame
Distributed table with named, typed columns.
DPU
Glue billing unit: 4 vCPU and 16 GB.
Driver
The process that runs your program, plans jobs and schedules tasks.
Dynamic partition overwrite
Overwrite only the partitions present in the data being written.
Dynamic partition pruning
Skipping fact-table partitions using values from a filtered dimension at run time.
Event log
File of Spark events used by the History Server to rebuild the UI.
Exchange
A shuffle in a physical plan.
Executor
Worker process with cores and memory that runs tasks.
Lazy evaluation
Transformations only build a plan; work happens at an action.
Lineage
The recipe of transformations used to rebuild lost data.
Manifest
Iceberg file listing data files with their statistics.
Memory overhead
Off-heap memory for an executor: Python workers, buffers, native code.
Narrow / wide transformation
Without / with a shuffle.
Orphan file
A file in a table's location that no Iceberg metadata references.
Partition (in memory)
Chunk of rows processed by one task.
Partition (on disk)
Folder per value of a partition column, e.g. dt=2026-11-03/.
Partition pruning
Reading only folders matching a filter on the partition column.
Predicate pushdown
Sending filters into the file reader to skip row groups using statistics.
Py4J
Library connecting the Python process to the JVM.
SCD (Slowly Changing Dimension)
Rules for recording changes to dimension attributes: overwrite (Type 1), keep history rows (Type 2), keep a previous value (Type 3) and variants.
Salting
Adding a random component to a hot key to spread its rows across tasks.
Surrogate key
Warehouse-generated key unique per dimension version; stored in fact tables.
Shuffle
Redistributing rows across executors by key; expensive.
Skew
Uneven distribution of rows across keys or partitions.
Snapshot
A complete version of an Iceberg table after one commit.
Spill
Writing in-memory data to disk when execution memory is short.
Stage
A set of tasks that run the same code without a shuffle in between.
Task
Unit of work on one partition.
Transformation
Operation returning a new DataFrame (lazy).
Tungsten
Spark's efficient execution engine (binary format, code generation).
UDF
User-defined function; Python UDFs move data to Python workers.
Watermark
Streaming threshold for how late data may arrive before state is dropped.
Whole-stage codegen
Fusing operators of a stage into one generated Java function; *(n) in plans.
⌂ All courses
